<meta>This page continues the catalog of engraved gems and talismans. While Plate XXI focused on planetary deities and their corresponding archangels (such as Saturn and Mercury), Plate XXII shifts toward the "Labors of Hercules" and more enigmatic, rustic symbolism. These images transition from celestial governance to the physical mastery of the hero and the mysterious signatures of nature.</meta>

# PLATE XXII

->**Plate XXII**<-

***

<column-break/>

->**89.**<-

<note>This scene depicts the first of the twelve labors of Hercules, a favorite subject for ancient amulets meant to grant the wearer strength or protection against physical ailments.</note>

**Hercules and the Nemean Lion**
A muscular figure representing **Hercules** <term>the greatest of the Greek and Roman heroes</term> is shown in a desperate struggle with a lion. This is the **Nemean Lion** <term>a monstrous beast with a hide that could not be pierced by iron, bronze, or stone</term>. Because weapons were useless, Hercules is shown here using his bare hands to choke the beast, symbolizing the triumph of human courage over primal, invincible force.

***

->**[The Reverse of 89]**<-

<note>The back of the Hercules gem contains a magical formula. These types of inscriptions were often used in the ancient world as "healing charms," specifically believed to cure stomach pains or colic.</note>

**The Inscribed Talisman**
This oval represents the reverse side of the previous gem. In the center are three stylized characters resembling the letter 'K'. Around the border is a Greek inscription: **Eubarra Rabauruhss** <note>original: "ΕΥΒΑΡΡΑ ΡΑΒΑΥΡVΗCC"</note>. This is a "word of power" <term>a phrase used in magic that does not have a literal translation but is intended to invoke spiritual protection</term>.

***

->**90.**<-

**The Triumph of Hercules**
In a variation of the wrestling motif, **Hercules** is shown lifting the **Nemean Lion** off the ground. By depriving the beast of its footing, the hero demonstrates total mastery. Such gems were often carried by travelers and soldiers as a sign of endurance and victory.

<column-break/>

->**91.**<-

<note>This image depicts a "herm," a type of sacred boundary marker used in the ancient countryside to invoke the protection of the gods over the land.</note>

**The Herm of a Horned Deity**
A bust of a horned figure—likely **Pan** <term>the god of the wild, shepherds, and rustic music</term> or a **Satyr**—sits atop a stone pedestal. He is flanked by two smaller pillars adorned with leafy branches. This represents a rural shrine, a place where travelers would stop to offer thanks for a safe journey through the wilderness.

***

->**92.**<-

<note>This unusual image likely serves as a medical or biological allegory, linking the human body to the growth of the natural world.</note>

**The Tree of the Torso**
A stylized human torso, which some scholars interpret as a representation of the **lungs** <term>the organs of breath and spirit</term>, serves as the base for a flourishing tree. Around the image are the letters **A**, **C**, and **NI**. This may symbolize the "Breath of Life" or the idea that the human soul and the natural world are sustained by the same vital force.

***

->**93.**<-

<note>This motif refers to the "oscilla," small masks that Romans would hang from trees in orchards to swing in the wind, believing they would bring fertility and ward off evil spirits.</note>

**The Tree of Masks**
A tree is shown with three human faces or masks hanging from its branches. These masks represent the different "personae" or characters of human life. The letters **W**, **I**, and **A** are placed around the trunk, likely a cryptic invocation or the initials of a sacred phrase intended to protect a garden or grove.

***

<summary>This page illustrates talismans featuring Hercules' victory over the Nemean Lion alongside rustic symbols like the herm of Pan and "mask trees" used for agricultural protection. These gems represent the intersection of heroic myth, folk medicine, and rural religious practices.</summary>
<keywords>Hercules, Nemean Lion, Pan, herm, talismans, Greek inscriptions, healing charms, oscilla, nature spirits</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of engraved gems and talismans. While Plate XXII focused on the physical mastery and legendary feats of Hercules, Plate XXIII shifts toward the esoteric world of Gnostic and magical amulets. These images move from classical mythology into the realm of the "voces magicae" (magical words) and hybrid deities like Abraxas and Anubis-Hermes, reflecting the mystical traditions of the late Roman Empire.</meta>

# PLATE XXIII

->**Plate XXIII**<-

***

<column-break/>

->**94.**<-

<note>This double-sided amulet reflects the blending of Egyptian and Greek religious traditions common in the first centuries AD.</note>

**The Messenger of the Underworld**
The obverse (front) of this oval gemstone shows a standing deity, likely **Anubis-Hermes** <term>a hybrid god combining the Egyptian jackal-headed Anubis with the Greek Hermes, both of whom guided souls to the afterlife</term>. He is shown holding a **caduceus** <term>a winged staff entwined by two snakes, symbolizing peace and trade</term> and a **situla** <term>a ritual bucket used for pouring libations or holy water</term>. He is surrounded by a circular Greek inscription.

***

->**[The Reverse of 94]**<-

**The Hidden Syllables**
The back of the stone contains four lines of Greek text: **Ypsexia / Iaēxiō / Yō X O / Ōx Oa** <note>original: "ΥΨΗΞΙΑ / ΙΑΗΞΙW / ΥW Ξ Ο / WΞ ΟΑ"</note>. These are <term>voces magicae</term>, or "words of power." They are not intended to be read as standard sentences but are ritualistic sounds meant to vibrate with divine energy and compel spiritual forces to protect the wearer.

***

->**95.**<-

**Abraxas, the Solar Deity**
The obverse of this amulet depicts the most famous Gnostic symbol: **Abraxas** (or **Abrasax**) <term>a supreme deity whose name, when calculated by the numerical value of its letters, equals 365, the number of days in a year</term>. He is shown with the head of a cock (symbolizing vigilance and the sun), the armored torso of a warrior, and legs made of two serpents (representing wisdom or the earth). He holds a whip and a shield. Beneath him is the name **Iao** <note>original: "ΙΑW"</note> <term>the Greek phonetic spelling of the Hebrew name for God, Yahweh</term>.

***

->**[The Reverse of 95]**<-

**The Sacred Name**
The reverse features an oval frame containing the inscription: **Iaoa / Brasax** <note>original: "ΙΑWΑ / ΒΡΑΣΑΞ"</note>. This reinforces the connection between the God of the Hebrews and the solar power of the Gnostic deity, a common theme in ancient protective magic.

***

->**96.**<-

**The Celestial Voyager**
This single-sided oval amulet depicts a figure holding a shield and a long staff or spear. The figure stands upon a **crescent moon** (or perhaps a celestial boat used to traverse the heavens). At their feet sits a small animal, likely a lion, which often symbolized the sun or the zodiac sign Leo, suggesting the deity’s power over the celestial cycles.

***

->**97.**<-

**The Hierarchy of Power**
This complex amulet depicts two figures. At the top stands a deity holding a spear and a globe <term>a symbol of universal sovereignty and the cosmos</term>. Below him is another **Abraxas** figure with serpent legs, shield, and whip. Around the border is a long magical inscription: **Ivmvbwai / Lam / Evi / Maler** <note>original: "ΙVΜVΒWΑΙ / ΛΑΜ / ΕVΙ / ΜΑΛΕR"</note>. These names appear frequently in the **Greek Magical Papyri** <term>ancient handbooks of spells and rituals</term> and were believed to guard the wearer against demons or illness.

<summary>This page catalogs four Gnostic gemstones featuring protective deities like Abraxas and Anubis-Hermes, alongside "voces magicae" (words of power) and the name Iao (Yahweh). These artifacts highlight the synthesis of Greek, Egyptian, and Jewish mystical traditions in late antiquity.</summary>

<keywords>Gnostic gems, Abraxas, Iao, Anubis-Hermes, voces magicae, amulets, talismans, late antiquity magic</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sequence from Plate XXIII, transitioning from the complex Gnostic deities like Abraxas and Anubis into the specific iconography of the scarab and eternal symbols found on ancient amulets and signet rings. While the previous page focused on the "words of power" (voces magicae), this page emphasizes the visual metaphors of rebirth, cosmic cycles, and ritual transformation.</meta>

# PLATE XXIV

->**Plate XXIV**<-

***

<column-break/>

->**98.**<-

**The Sun-Beetle within the Infinite**
This **gemstone** <term>original: "gemma"; a semi-precious stone carved with an image or inscription</term> depicts an **Ouroboros** <term>a serpent or dragon swallowing its own tail, representing eternity, the cyclical nature of the universe, and the concept of "all is one"</term>. Within the circle of the serpent is a **scarab beetle** <term>a powerful Egyptian symbol of the sun-god Khepri and the process of rebirth</term>. This particular beetle is shown with a human head crowned with solar rays and outstretched human arms, signifying the divine intelligence that guides the sun across the sky.

***

->**99.**<-

**The Winged Soul**
This **symbol** <term>original: "symbolum"; a sign or object representing something else, often used in a religious or secret context</term> shows a winged insect or beetle with a segmented body. It is positioned above a horizontal tied ribbon or sacred fillet. In the ancient world, winged beetles often represented the "Ba" or the soul taking flight, or the swift movement of divine providence over the earthly sphere.

***

->**100.**<-

**The Ritual of the Mask**
A nude male figure is shown in profile, leaning forward to place a mask or perhaps a ritual skin over a pedestal. A small sphere or globe rests at his feet. This scene likely depicts a moment from the **mysteries** <term>secret religious rituals that were only open to initiated members</term>, possibly relating to the theater of Dionysus or the philosophical idea of stripping away the "mask" of the physical body to reveal the soul.

***

->**101.**<-

**The Triumph of the Spirit**
A bird, possibly a dove or eagle, faces right while holding a laurel wreath in its beak—a classic sign of victory. To the left of the bird is a small scarab emblem tied with a large ribbon. This composition suggests the union of Egyptian rebirth (the scarab) with Greco-Roman victory (the wreath), representing the soul’s ultimate triumph over death.

***

->**102.**<-

**The Sacred Mask of the Beetle**
These two views show a large object shaped like a **scarab** <term>original: "scarabaeus"</term>. The image on the left shows the top view with markings resembling eyes and wing cases. The image on the right shows the reverse side or perhaps a hollowed-out mask version, featuring side apertures through which ribbons are threaded. Such items were often used as protective amulets or funerary masks to shield the wearer from spiritual harm.

***

<summary>This page illustrates various symbolic engravings, primarily focusing on the scarab beetle and the Ouroboros as emblems of eternity and solar rebirth. It highlights the syncretism between Egyptian protective symbols and Greco-Roman ritual imagery.</summary>

<keywords>scarab, Ouroboros, amulets, rebirth, eternity, solar deity, ritual masks, ancient gems, iconography</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sequence from Plate XXIV, moving from the solar scarab imagery and the "Winged Soul" to complex Gnostic seals and the prominent cult of Serapis. It illustrates the late antique fusion of Greek, Egyptian, and Hebrew mystical traditions often found on protective amulets.</meta>

# PLATE XXV

->**Plate XXV**<-

***

<column-break/>

->**103.**<-

**The Enshrined Priest**
This **medallion** <term>a large medal or coin-like object, often worn as an amulet or kept as a commemorative piece</term> depicts a standing figure, possibly a deity or a high priest, wearing a long robe. The figure is encased in a distinctive conical, ribbed structure that resembles a cage or a specialized ritual basket. This may represent a specific form of a "primitive" or "archaic" cult statue, such as those found in Ephesus or Syria, where the body was tightly bound in sacred wrappings.

***

->**The Reverse of 103**<-

**The Pentagram of Protection**
On the reverse of the previous medallion is a **pentagram** <term>a five-pointed star drawn with a single continuous line, used in antiquity as a symbol of health, harmony, and protection</term> inscribed within a circle. Between the points of the star are several **magical characters** <term>original: "charakteres"; non-alphabetic symbols believed to hold divine power</term> and Greek letters used in the "Gnostic" or "Theurgic" traditions to invoke celestial protection.

***

->**104.**<-

**The Divine Countenance of Serapis**
A large, finely detailed engraving of a youthful divine head, most likely representing **Serapis** <term>a syncretic god created in Ptolemaic Egypt to bridge the traditions of Osiris, Apis, and Zeus</term>. The god wears the **modius** <term>also called a *calathus*; a basket-shaped headdress symbolizing the grain-measure, representing fertility, the harvest, and the god’s role as the ruler of the underworld and provider for the living</term>. His wide, staring eyes are a hallmark of "late antique" style, intended to show a soul gazing into the divine realm.

***

->**105.**<-

**The Gracious Goddess**
A circular medallion depicting a female figure in classical robes. She holds a **patera** <term>a shallow bowl used for pouring liquid offerings (libations) during a sacrifice</term> in her right hand and a long scepter or staff in her left. This likely represents a personification of a virtue, such as **Pietas** (Dutifulness) or **Concordia** (Harmony), common on Roman-era coinage and amulets.

***

->**The Reverse of 105**<-

**The Sacred Names of Wisdom**
The reverse of the goddess medallion contains a three-line Greek inscription in capital letters:
**SAKASHE**
**SOPHE**
**ADONAI**
<note>original: "САКАШЄ СОФН АЛШNАI"; The first word is an obscure magical name often found in papyri; "Sophe" is the Greek word for Wisdom (Sophia); "Adonai" is the Hebrew word for "Lord," showing how diverse religious traditions were blended in these charms.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the blending of diverse religious traditions in late antiquity, featuring the Greco-Egyptian god Serapis alongside a Gnostic pentagram and a charm invoking the Hebrew 'Adonai'. These artifacts demonstrate how ancient practitioners combined symbols from multiple cultures to create powerful protective amulets.</summary>

<keywords>Serapis, pentagram, modius, Gnosticism, Sophia, Adonai, amulets, Greco-Egyptian syncretism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of engraved gems and amulets from Plate XXV, transitioning from the singular, youthful depictions of Serapis and the ritualistic "Enshrined Priest" to more complex imperial and military compositions. These items demonstrate how the cult of Serapis merged with Roman state symbols, such as the eagle, the she-wolf, and the military standards.</meta>

# PLATE XXVI

->**PLATE XXVI**<-

***

<column-break/>

->**106.**<-

**The Enthroned Serapis and the Standards of Victory**
This medallion features a profile bust of a bearded deity, identified by his **modius** <term>a basket-shaped crown representing fertility and the harvest</term> as **Serapis** <term>the Greco-Egyptian god of the sun, healing, and the afterlife</term>. Below the bust, an eagle—the messenger of Jupiter—spreads its wings. The deity is flanked by two winged figures of **Victory** <term>originally: "Victoria"; the Roman goddess of triumph</term> standing atop military standards, suggesting this amulet was intended to ensure success in battle or to honor a specific military leader.

***

->**107.**<-

**The Foundations of Rome**
A highly complex scene depicting the divine protection of the Roman state. A seated goddess, likely **Roma** or **Minerva**, watches over a soldier. At the base of the scene, the famous **She-wolf** <term>original: "Lupa Romana"</term> suckles the twins **Romulus and Remus**, the legendary founders of Rome. The presence of the eagle, the crescent moon, and the star indicates that the Roman Empire was viewed as part of a divine, celestial order.

***

->**108.**<-

**Sacrifice to the Divine Light**
A bearded profile, possibly an emperor in the guise of Jupiter, dominates the upper half of this gem. Below him, a winged Victory holds a laurel wreath over a ritual altar. To the left is the vertical inscription **LVCI** <note>original Latin: "LVCI"; this likely refers to the name "Lucius" or is a shortened form of "Lucis," meaning "of the Light"</note>. This may have been a commemorative piece for a specific festival or an imperial anniversary.

<column-break/>

->**109.**<-

**The Sun-King Serapis**
This gem depicts the god Serapis facing right. In this variation, he wears a **radiate crown** <term>a crown with protruding rays, identifying the wearer with Sol, the sun god</term> in addition to his usual modius. This emphasizes his role as the "Lord of All," a deity who governs the movement of the sun and the cycles of life.

***

->**110.**<-

**Serapis-Ammon**
A profile bust showing a fascinating combination of cultures. The figure wears the modius of the Egyptian Serapis, but also the curved **Ram’s Horn** <term>the "Horn of Ammon," a symbol of the Egyptian god Amun and the Greek Zeus-Ammon</term>. This syncretic <note>the blending of different beliefs or gods into one</note> image was famously associated with Alexander the Great and remained a potent symbol of divine authority throughout the Roman era.

***

->**111.**<-

**The Frontal Majesty of the God**
Unlike the previous profiles, this gem shows the face of the deity from the front. The wild, flowing hair and beard are characteristic of the "Jupiter-type" gods. He wears the modius and is flanked by stylized horns or wings, creating a powerful, protective **apotropaic** <term>an object or image intended to turn away evil or bad luck</term> image intended to stare down and repel any malevolent forces directed at the wearer.

<summary>This page illustrates the fusion of the Serapis cult with Roman imperial ideology, featuring symbols of victory, the foundation myth of Romulus and Remus, and the blending of Egyptian and Roman divine attributes.</summary>
<keywords>Serapis, Victory, Romulus and Remus, She-wolf, Jupiter-Ammon, military standards, modius, Roman Empire, amulets</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of engraved gems from Plate XXVI. While the previous plate focused on the intersection of Serapis with Roman imperial and military power, Plate XXVII moves deeper into the mystical and syncretic world of Romano-Egyptian religion. It features the "Isiac Circle"—the family of Isis, Serapis, and Harpocrates—alongside complex depictions of Anubis and the Roman god Mercury, highlighting the blend of commerce, magic, and afterlife protection.</meta>

# PLATE XXVII

->**PLATE XXVII**<-

***

<column-break/>

->**112.**<-

**The Divine Union of Serapis and Isis**
This engraved gem displays a **jugate** <note>profiles overlapping one another so both are visible</note> portrait of the two primary deities of the Alexandrian cult. In the foreground is **Serapis**, identifiable by his beard and the **modius** <term>a grain-measure or basket worn on the head to symbolize the fertility of the earth</term>. Behind him is his consort, **Isis**, adorned with a lotus flower upon her brow. This pairing represents the harmony of the masculine and feminine forces governing the universe and the Nile’s bounty.

***

->**113.**<-

**The Secret Rites of the Isiac Procession**
Three figures are depicted in a ritualistic arrangement. On the left, a priestess of Isis holds a **situla** <term>a ceremonial bucket used to carry holy water from the Nile</term>. In the center stands a winged child, likely **Harpocrates** <note>the Greek name for the infant Horus, often depicted with a finger to his lips to signify silence and mystery</note>. To the right, a male figure, perhaps a priest or the god Serapis himself, holds a torch and a staff, guiding the initiate through the darkness of the mysteries.

***

->**114.**<-

**The Egyptian Holy Triad**
This medallion shows a standing family group common in Egyptian-style amulets. It depicts **Isis** and **Osiris** (in his Hellenized form as Serapis) flanking their son, **Horus**. They wear traditional Egyptian headgear, signaling the wearer's devotion to the protective power of the divine family, which was believed to guard the household and ensure rebirth in the afterlife.

***

<column-break/>

->**115.**<-

**Anubis and the Symbols of the Underworld**
A highly complex mystical scene centered on **Anubis** <term>the jackal-headed god of mummification and the guide of souls</term>. He stands holding a staff of authority. He is surrounded by a collection of sacred symbols: a seated **cynocephalus** <term>a dog-headed baboon, often associated with the god Thoth and the measurement of time</term>, a bird (possibly a phoenix or ibis), and a scorpion. These symbols likely relate to the **Decans** <note>star groups used in ancient Egyptian astronomy and magic</note>, suggesting this gem served as an astrological or protective talisman.

***

->**116.**<-

**Mercury, Master of All Trades**
The Roman god **Mercury** <note>the Greek Hermes</note> is shown here with his full array of attributes. He wears the **petasos** <term>a winged traveler’s hat</term> and holds the **caduceus** <term>a herald’s staff entwined with two snakes, symbolizing peace and commerce</term> and a **marsupium** <term>a leather money-purse</term>. Surrounding him are the animals sacred to him: the rooster (herald of the day), the ram (fertility), and the tortoise (from which he fashioned the first lyre). A scorpion at his feet suggests an astrological connection to the zodiac sign of Scorpio.

***

->**117.**<-

**Mercury the Provider**
A simpler depiction of the nude Mercury, emphasizing his role as the god of profit and luck. He carries his purse and caduceus, accompanied by a ram and a rooster. Such gems were frequently worn by merchants and travelers in the Roman world to ensure safe passage and successful business transactions.

***

<summary>This plate illustrates the blending of Egyptian and Roman deities, focusing on the protective "Isiac Triad" and the multifaceted role of Mercury as a god of magic, commerce, and the soul's journey.</summary>

<keywords>Serapis, Isis, Anubis, Mercury, Hermes, Harpocrates, caduceus, modius, situla, Egyptian mysteries, Roman gems, amulets, syncretism</keywords>

# PLATE XXVIII

->**PLATE XXVIII**<-

***

<column-break/>

->**118.**<-

**The Ritual Cap of the Sacred Sage**
These two illustrations provide a detailed study of a specific form of ancient headgear. On the left, we see a frontal view of a bearded man wearing a close-fitting cap or bonnet, which is secured by long **vittae** <term>sacred ribbons or headbands worn by priests, poets, and those seeking religious sanctuary</term>. On the right, the rear view of the same head shows how these ribbons were tied in an intricate bow at the nape of the neck. Such headwear is often associated with the "mystic" or "oriental" priesthoods that became popular throughout the Roman Empire, where specific garments signaled a person’s rank within a religious mystery cult.

***

->**119.**<-

**The Youthful Initiate and the Sacred Branch**
This pair of circular objects represents the two sides of a single gem or medallion. On the left is the portrait of a youthful figure with wavy hair, wearing a cap tied with ribbons similar to those seen in figure 118. The softness of the features suggests it may represent a young initiate or perhaps a youthful deity like **Apollo** or **Dionysus**. The right-hand image shows the reverse side, decorated with a botanical motif consisting of a branch bearing berries or buds. This likely represents a laurel or olive branch, symbols of purification and divine favor.

***

->**120.**<-

**The Celestial Seal and the Ceremonial Bonnet**
The left illustration depicts a complex seal or coin featuring two standing figures in patterned tunics. They flank a central **crab** <note>original: *cancer*</note>, a motif that often carries astrological significance, representing the zodiac sign of Cancer or the moon's influence. A small plant grows at the base, suggesting a connection between celestial cycles and the fertility of the earth. To the right is an individual depiction of a bell-shaped cap with ribbons tied on either side. This standalone image highlights the importance of the object itself as a sacred artifact, perhaps used as an amulet for protection or as a signifier of religious office.

# CONCERNING
# JEAN MACARIUS,
# AND HIS WRITINGS.

->Valerius Andreas Desselius, in the *Belgian Library*.<-
->Antonius Sanderus, Canon and Penitentiary of Ypres,<-
->Book II, *On the Writers of Flanders*.<-
->Franciscus Swertius, in *Belgian Athens*.<-

JEAN MACARIUS, *commonly called* L’HEUREUX <note>The name Macarius is derived from the Greek word for "happy" or "blessed," which translates to "L'Heureux" in French.</note>, *a Fleming from Gravelines and a Canon of Aire in the region of Artois, was a man of exceptional knowledge in the Greek and Latin languages. These he drank in as a boy at Bergues-Saint-Winoc in Flanders under* PAUL LEOPARD <note>A famous 16th-century humanist scholar.</note>. *He spent twenty years in the City <note>Rome</note>, the mistress of the world, diligent in scrutinizing the monuments of the ancients and examining libraries; many things brought from the heart of Greece he bestowed upon the Latin world <note>i.e., he translated them into Latin</note>. Nevertheless, such was his modesty—which was somewhat vexing to all good and learned men—that he allowed nothing to be published or to reach the mouths of the learned during his lifetime. Upon his death, he bequeathed his writings and papers to the Library of the Busleyden Trilingual College at Louvain <note>A famous Renaissance college dedicated to the study of Latin, Greek, and Hebrew</note>. From these, the following are worthy of the light of the printing press:*

ON the Ancient Method of Writing.
ON the Nature of the Middle Verb, and on nearly the entire nature of Greek verbs: from a rougher draft of a larger work.
GREEK Inscriptions, *translated by Macarius, with his own notes.*
THE Roman Emendation of the Bible.
AN ANTIQUARIAN DISQUISITION ON THE BASILIDIAN GEMS, *titled* ABRAXAS, *or* THE UNBELIEVING FAITHFUL <note>original: "Apistopistus." This refers to the Gnostic gems discussed in the main body of this book.</note>.
A COMMENTARY on Ancient Sacred Paintings and Sculptures, especially those found at Rome, *titled* HAGIOGLYPTA <note>Literally "Sacred Carvings."</note>.
BASIL, Bishop of Seleucia, on the Life of St. Thecla, translated by Macarius.

->Aubertus Miraeus, Dean of Antwerp, in the *Ecclesiastical Library*, or Scholia to the book of Saint Jerome on Ecclesiastical Writers, chapter 21.<-

John Macarius, *Canon of Aire in Artois, formerly our friend, most learned in Greek and Latin, wrote* An Antiquarian Disquisition on Basilidian Gems, *the title of which is* Abraxas, *or* The Unbeliever. <note>original: "Apistopistus." The Basilidians were an early Gnostic sect; "Abraxas" was a word of mystic significance found on their engraved gemstones.</note> *The manuscript survives at Leuven in the Trilingual College, and at Tournai in the Winghe Library, which is now that of the Cathedral Basilica.*
He departed this life at Aire on June 11, <term>1614</term> <note>M. DC. XIV.</note> and was buried in Saint Peter’s, with this title and marble ornament, which is seen in the chapel of Saint Mary Magdalene.

# To God, Best and Greatest

### Sacred to the remains of the most honored and learned man, Lord and Master Jean Macaré, otherwise known as L’Heureux, one of the fourteen Canons in this church.

I am Jean Macaré <note>original: "Ianus Macarivs"</note>—in French you would say "L’Heureux" <note>The name translates to "The Happy" or "The Fortunate"</note>—born to a mother from the Beaurain family and a father of the same surname. I was brought into the light of the world on the shores of Gravelines. As a boy, the town of Bergues polished my skills under the guidance of Leopardus <note>Paulus Leopardus, a noted 16th-century humanist scholar</note>, training me in the arts of Greek and Latin eloquence; then Louvain imbued me with the teachings of Wisdom <note>referring to Philosophy and Theology at the University of Louvain</note>. For more than twenty years, the citizens of Rome <note>original: "Quirites"</note> made use of my industrious teaching. They showed such gratitude that, through the petitions of leading Princes whom I had pleased—and whom my writings had pleased—the Pope voluntarily granted me a pension in this very Temple, which they call a Canonry <term>Canonry: a prestigious ecclesiastical office involving administrative and liturgical duties in a cathedral or collegiate church</term>. I lived for twelve five-year periods plus three years <note>This calculates to 63 years of age</note>; the light of the eleventh day of June took away my life in the year 1614.

To you, O Christ, may all that we are, all that we have been, and all that we shall be in the next age, be turned to praise.

<summary>This page presents the funerary epitaph of Jean Macaré (L'Heureux), a Flemish humanist and canon, detailing his education under Leopardus and his career in Rome before his death in 1614.</summary>
<keywords>Jean Macaré, L'Heureux, Gravelines, Bergues, Louvain, Rome, Canonry, Humanism, Epitaph, 17th century</keywords>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Gnostic artifacts, specifically focusing on the physical evidence of gems used by the followers of Basilides, as documented by 16th and 17th-century scholars.</meta>

# SEVERAL TESTIMONIES
# OF MORE RECENT WRITERS
# CONCERNING THE BASILIDIAN GEMS.

->Cardinal Caesar Baronius, of the Holy Roman Church, in Volume 2 of the<-
->Ecclesiastical Annals, for the year of Christ 120, number 10.<-

To prove and confirm these matters more certainly, a monument of antiquity—worthy of belief and thoroughly examined—is added here. It declares clearly enough what kind of images were used by the followers of *Basilides* <note>Basilides was a 2nd-century Gnostic teacher in Alexandria who taught that the world was created by lower angels.</note>, as *St. Irenaeus* <note>An early Church Father who wrote "Against Heresies" to combat Gnostic teachings.</note> says they were accustomed to do. Indeed, not long ago—specifically after the first edition of this volume was published in Rome—there emerged into the light from the ruins (as often happens) an amethyst gem, carved with these very images and marks, a copy of which we have taken care to represent here (as you see). The gem itself is held by a distinguished man, respected for his sincere faith and excellent learning, *Fulvius Ursinus* <note>Fulvio Orsini (1529–1600), a famous Italian humanist, historian, and antiquarian.</note>. His incomparable skill in examining and identifying ancient things has shed light on this gem, which seemed entirely obscure to other students of antiquity; namely, that this is one of those images which the *Basilidian* heretics used to use, which they would wear superstitiously in place of a locket <note>original: "Bullæ"</note>, or as an amulet, or for some other reason.

<image-desc>Two circular woodcut illustrations of an ancient Gnostic/Basilidian gem. The left circle shows a standing figure with a rooster's head and serpent legs (Abraxas), holding a shield and a whip. The figure's body and the surrounding space are covered in Greek magical inscriptions (e.g., ABLANATHANALBA <note>A common magical palindrome used in ancient protective spells.</note>). The right circle shows the reverse side of the gem, featuring a smaller coiled serpent or lion-headed figure within a circular border.</image-desc>

<summary>This page cites the historian Caesar Baronius regarding the discovery of an amethyst Gnostic gem in Rome, identifying it as a superstitious amulet used by the followers of Basilides. It includes a woodcut illustration of the "Abraxas" figure, a hybrid creature with a rooster's head and snake legs.</summary>

<keywords>Basilidians, Gnostics, Abraxas, Caesar Baronius, Fulvio Orsini, St. Irenaeus, amethyst, amulets, archaeology, Roman ruins</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient gems and amulets, specifically focusing on the collections and scholarly opinions of prominent 17th-century humanists regarding Gnostic artifacts.</meta>

### Lorenzo Pignoria, Book I of the Epistolary Symbols.

There are more distinguished engraved gems that adorn my small cabinets: *Serapis, Nemesis, Cupid, Venus, Mercury, Fortune, and Rome* in military attire. Likewise, the *Lyre* of *Eunomus of Locri* <note>A legendary musician said to have been helped by a cicada when a string on his lyre broke</note>, a *Flower* upon which sits a *butterfly, a Fly, the She-wolf* nursing the Twins: the follies of the Egyptians, and several monsters of the followers of Basilides <note>Basilides was an early Gnostic teacher in Alexandria; his followers were known for using complex mystical symbols</note>.

### The same author in the Supplement to Egyptian Characters.

I have added the Hieroglyphics—or more truly, the trifles—of the ancient sectarians, moved by the encouragement of learned men and driven by the example of the Most Illustrious man, *Cardinal Caesar Baronius*, who wished the history of the year 120 AD to be adorned with an engraved *Amethyst* of this kind.

### Claude Saumaise, in his book on Climacteric Years, where he discusses the God Abraxas with the head of a rooster.

Many Gems are kept in the cabinets of the curious which display that face. One is also seen in the *Gem-Cabinet of Gorlaeus* <note>Abraham Gorlaeus was a famous collector of engraved gems and rings</note> without an inscription; instead of a whip in the right hand, it holds a wreath that appears thorny, and in the left, a helmet. *Pignoria* took care to publish six such gems in his *Commentary on the Isiac Tablet*, three of which bear the name ABRAXAS <note>original Greek: ΑΒΡΑΣΑΞ; a mystical word of power in Gnostic cosmology</note> clearly expressed. Three or four similar ones are seen in the *Gem-Cabinet of Gorlaeus*. I myself have seen many others of the same or very similar form in the treasuries <note>original Greek: ταμιείοις</note> of Antiquarians.

### Joseph Scaliger, Book 2, Letter 119 to Casaubon.

Nothing is so common as to see these relief-figures <note>original Greek: ἔκτυπα</note> enclosed in Gems, even very ancient ones. No one understands them except the person who ordered them to be made, and effort is spent in vain on interpreting them. For how many plausible things can be said, none of which might reveal the intent of those who made them? The ancient Heretics, especially the *Valentinians and Marcosians* <note>Gnostic sects known for their complex systems of "Aeons" and sacred numbers</note>, left behind many such things, which even today are preserved in the cabinets of Antiquarians along with their god ABRAXAS.

### The same author, Book 3, Letter 226 to Marquard Freher.

I have omitted to mention those Figures, which I said were called "elements" <note>original Greek: στοιχεῖα; here referring to astrological or magical signs</note> by the ancients; infinite numbers of these exist today, very ancient and carved into Gems, along with the stars under whose horoscopes they were engraved; with arguments, on account of the pro- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; the catchword "pro-" indicates the start of the next page's first word.</note>

<summary>This page compiles excerpts from renowned 17th-century scholars—Pignoria, Saumaise, and Scaliger—discussing their collections and interpretations of ancient Gnostic gems, particularly those featuring the deity Abraxas.</summary>
<keywords>Lorenzo Pignoria, Claude Saumaise, Joseph Scaliger, Abraxas, Gnosticism, engraved gems, amulets, antiquarianism, Basilidians, Valentinians</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the origins of Gnostic sects; this page continues with a specific focus on the physical appearance of their talismans and the etymology of the word Abraxas. The catchword "IOAN-" refers to the beginning of the next section's author, Ioannes Seldenus.</meta>

...a prodigious physical appearance, confused and composed of many things and animals, in the most obscure ways. For among them, some are armored, with a human neck and the head of an Eagle or a rooster, holding a shield in one hand and a whip or scourge in the other: standing upon serpents instead of legs. We have seen many of this kind inscribed with the letters *ABRAXAS* <note>original: ΑΒΡΑΞΑΣ</note>. These are the fabrications of the ancient Valentinians and other Heretics.

->John Selden, in Treatise 1 on the Syrian Gods, chapter 2.<-

I hardly doubt that *that amulet for curing a semitertian fever* <note>A "semitertian" fever (hemitritaeos) was a specific type of recurring malarial fever common in antiquity.</note>, *in which must be written—according to the precepts of the Magi—the word* ABRACADABRA, *first flowed from the name* ABRAXAS. *For the traces of the name are sufficiently apparent in that portentous word. And these things more than persuade me, as do so many* <term>amulets</term> <note>original: φειάμματα (pheimata), referring to protective charms or ligatures worn on the body</note> *and Gems inscribed with the same elements, sometimes repeated, an abundance of which you may find in the French letters of Scaliger to Vazetus.*

->Pierre Gassendi, concerning Nicolas-Claude Fabri de Peiresc, in his Life.<-

He had collected several items to illustrate what he [the Annalist Baronius] *had cast into the second Volume, regarding the Amethyst of Fulvio Orsini concerning the word* ABRAXAS <note>original: ΑΒΡΑΞΑΣ</note>, *by which the Basilidians* <note>A Gnostic sect founded by Basilides in Alexandria in the 2nd century AD.</note> *understood the supreme God, containing seven Angels, the seven presiding rulers of the heavens; and furthermore their three hundred and sixty-five virtues, corresponding to the days of the year; and this because of the seven letters and their numerical value (by joining the numbers according to the order of each letter in the Alphabet) and similar matters of that kind, which he also subsequently explained in letters to Benedetto Natali.*

->Athanasius Kircher on the Gnostics and their Amulet Magic, Part 2, Volume 2 of the Egyptian Oedipus, Class 11, chapter 6.<-

Resuscitating from the underworld the Egyptian superstitions of which they boasted themselves the heirs, they infected the entire East with them. For insisting upon their erroneous dogmas, they established a new characteristic Art, by which they fashioned various Amulets. These were carved into various stones according to the nature and property of the Deity which they invoked through them, circumscribed with ancient Egyptian characters, and marked with the Names of powerful Angels, placed according to the precepts of Hieroglyphic Laws; with these, they boasted they could perform wonders.

<summary>This page discusses the iconography and etymology of "Abraxas" and "Abracadabra," linking Gnostic talismans to Egyptian magic and numerical mysticism. It cites prominent 17th-century scholars like Selden, Gassendi, and Kircher to explain how these amulets were used for healing and spiritual protection.</summary>
<keywords>Abraxas, Abracadabra, Gnosticism, Basilidians, amulets, magic, Athanasius Kircher, John Selden, Pierre Gassendi, gem engraving, numerology</keywords>

--- Page 55 ---
<meta>This page begins the formal treatise by Jean L'Heureux (Ioannes Macarius) regarding the Gnostic deity Abraxas, following the introductory front matter.</meta>

# THE ABRAXAS
## BY JEAN L'HEUREUX
# CANON OF AIRE
### OR
# THE UNBELIEVABLE BELIEVER;
### WHICH IS AN ANTIQUARIAN
### INVESTIGATION INTO
### THE BASILIDIAN GEMS.

<margin>I.</margin>
<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'I' featuring floral motifs, leaves, and a central fruit, possibly a pomegranate or citrus, within a square frame.</image-desc>
IN ancient gems, there is a certain monster carved with the head of a rooster, the torso of a human body, and feet ending in two serpents which curve back upon themselves; it holds a shield in one hand and a whip in the other. Led by certain evidence, I have judged this to be the fabricated god of the impious heretic Basilides <note>Basilides was an early 2nd-century Gnostic teacher in Alexandria who claimed that Abraxas was the supreme power.</note>, whose name is **Abraxas**. The most illustrious Seraphin Olivier-Razali can bear witness to my opinion on this matter; he was formerly the Primary Auditor and Dean of the Rota <note>The Roman Rota is the highest appellate tribunal of the Catholic Church.</note> and is now among the Cardinals. For he once asked me what I thought of that monstrous form. When I told him that I believed it to be **Abraxas**, a gem was immediately found which had that monster on one side and the name **Abraxas** on the other, which snatched away all opportunity for doubt.

<margin>II.</margin>
Regarding Abraxas, Tertullian <note>An early Christian author from Carthage (c. 155 – c. 220 AD) who wrote extensively against heresy.</note> says the following in his book *On the Prescription against Heretics*: *Afterwards came* **Basilides** *the heretic...*

<summary>Jean L'Heureux introduces his study of the "Abraxas" gems, identifying a specific rooster-headed, serpent-legged figure as the god of the Gnostic heretic Basilides. He validates his identification by citing a physical specimen that bears both the image and the name, as well as the testimony of Cardinal Seraphin Olivier-Razali.</summary>
<keywords>Abraxas, Basilides, Gnosticism, engraved gems, Seraphin Olivier-Razali, Tertullian, rooster-headed god, archaeology</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Gnostic cosmogony, specifically focusing on the system of Basilides and the figure of Abraxas as viewed by the Church Fathers.</meta>

-># A B R A X A S<-

<margin>The heretic Basilides</margin>
...the heretic burst forth: he says that there is a Supreme God named ABRAXAS, by whom Mind was created, which he calls in Greek <term>Nous<note>The Greek term for intellect or divine mind.</note></term>. From Mind came the *Word*, from the Word came *Providence*, from Providence came *Virtue* and *Wisdom*; from these then were made *Principalities*, *Powers*, and *Angels*. Next followed infinite editions and emanations <note>original: "probolas," a Gnostic term for the flowing forth of divine beings</note> of Angels; by these Angels, three hundred and sixty-five heavens were established. Among the lowest Angels, specifically those who made this world, he places the God of the Jews as the very last—that is, the God of the Law and the Prophets. He denies that this figure is God, asserting instead that he is an Angel. Not without merit does the Blessed Jerome call Abraxas "monstrous" <note>original: "portentosum"</note> in his commentary on those words of the first chapter of Obadiah: *How has Esau been pillaged, and his hidden things searched out?* See Marcion, he says, and Valentinus, and all the heretics who, having their consciences seared by the doctrines of demons, applaud themselves and deceive simple souls with composed speech, as if initiating them into certain divine mysteries. But when their discourse brings into the light the thirty Aeons <note>Eternal divine beings or ages</note>, and the Groups of Four, and the Groups of Eight, and the Groups of Twelve, and the dual God, and the MONSTROUS ABRAXAS, then the prudence of Esau will be shown as folly, and his secrets will be investigated. Likewise, in the first chapter of Nahum, regarding the passage: *From you shall come one who plots evil against the Lord, a wicked counselor.* Does it not seem like malice and transgression against God to say, as Valentinus does, that the Creator was produced as a sort of "abortion" from the furthest edge of a wandering Wisdom? Is not the thought of BASILIDES an indecency against God, along with the MONSTROUS NAME ABRAXAS, which is preferred over the Lord the Creator? Again, in the third chapter of Amos, on these words: *Assemble yourselves upon the mountains of Samaria, and see the many disturbances in her midst.* And because, he says, according to the laws of Tropology <note>A method of biblical interpretation that looks for moral or spiritual allegories</note>, Samaria refers to the heretics who falsely claim to be guardians of God's commandments, the Divine word commands that...

<summary>This page details the Gnostic hierarchy of divine emanations according to Basilides, culminating in the figure of Abraxas, and records the fierce condemnations of these "monstrous" doctrines by Saint Jerome.</summary>
<keywords>Abraxas, Basilides, Gnosticism, Saint Jerome, emanations, Aeons, heresy, Valentinus, Marcion, biblical commentary</keywords>

--- Page 57 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of heretical sects and their mystical interpretations of divine names, specifically focusing on the Gnostic systems of Basilides and others compared to pagan solar worship.</meta>

# THE UNBELIEVING BELIEVER
<note>original title: "APISTOPISTVS"</note>

<sig>B 2</sig>

...they preach among the Gentiles, who have no knowledge of God; in whose doctrine there is a consuming fire, and tribulation, and distress, so that they might consider the false Samaria, and ascend to the mountains of her pride, and see the many madnesses in the midst of the city, while each one fashions what he wishes, and worships his own fiction: such as **Marcion** <note>A 2nd-century heretic who rejected the Old Testament God</note> with his "good and idle God"; or **Valentinus** <note>A prominent Gnostic teacher</note> with his thirty *Aeons* <note>divine emanations or ages</note>, and the final Christ, whom he calls *ektroma*, that is, an **abortion** <note>original Greek: ἔκτρωμα</note>; or **BASILIDES**, who calls the almighty God by the **MONSTROUS NAME** of **ABRAXAS**, and says that this same name, according to the Greek letters and the number of the annual course, is contained within the circle of the **SUN**, whom the Heathens call **MITHRAS** <note>A Persian sun god popular in the Roman Empire</note> under the same number of different letters; and the Iberian follies marvel at *Balsamus* and *Barbelo*. Are these not madnesses, and many madnesses, with each person fashioning whatever has fallen into his mind?

I shall clarify Jerome's meaning by quoting Augustine: **BASILIDES**, he says, claimed there were three hundred and sixty-five heavens, the number of days by which the year is concluded. For this reason, he recommended a supposedly **HOLY NAME**, which is **Abraxas**: the letters of which name, according to Greek calculation, complete that same number. For there are seven: *alpha*, and *beta*, and *rho*, and *alpha*, and *xi*, and *alpha*, and *sigma*: that is, one, and two, and one hundred, and one, and sixty, and one, and two hundred, which in total make three hundred and sixty-five.

<margin>III.</margin>
And many others have handed down the tradition that this number is contained in the name **Abraxas**. However, I have seen no one who has explained that the same calculation is included in the name of **Mithras**. Yet I find it to be so, if it is pronounced in Greek as **MITHRES** <note>original: ΜΙΘΡΗΣ</note>, which is also called **MITHRAS**. For there are six Greek letters: *Mu*, and *Iota*, and *Theta*, and *Rho*, and *Eta*, and *Sigma*. And *mu* is 40; the mark *iota* is 10; *theta* is 8; *rho* is 100; *eta* is 7; *sigma* is 200; the sum of which is the same as in Abraxas: 365. Do not wonder, however, that I say *theta* is 8 and *eta* is 7, since otherwise...

<summary>This page examines the Gnostic use of numerology (gematria) to equate the mystical name Abraxas with the 365 days of the solar year, further linking it to the pagan sun god Mithras. The author cites Church Fathers Jerome and Augustine to critique these "madnesses" of early heretical sects.</summary>

->ABRAXAS<-

otherwise it might be used. For it seems that those who calculated this number proceeded in the name <term>MITHRAS<note>original: ΜΙΘΡΗΣ. In Greek gematria, the letters of Mithras total 365, the same as Abraxas.</note></term> in the correct order, by counting the letters of the Alphabet up to Theta, which is the eighth letter, and perhaps making that the ninth which is usually inserted in the sixth place as a mark; nor can this number be found in the name of MITHRAS otherwise. Therefore, the <term>Basilidians<note>Followers of Basilides, a 2nd-century Gnostic teacher in Alexandria.</note></term> worshipped under another name the God of the Heathens, Mithras, who was familiar to the Persians and even crept into the Roman world; the Ancients noted that his Sacred Rites were very closely related to Christian Rites. For they also dipped people in a bath <note>referring to baptism</note>, and marked them on the forehead, and they had a form of the Eucharist, as well as an initiation into martyrdom, and a belief in the resurrection. And indeed, concerning the form of the Eucharist, Justin Martyr says in his *Second Apology* for the Christians: *The Apostles,* he says, *in the Commentaries written by them, which are called Gospels, handed down that JESUS had so commanded them. For having taken bread, when he had given thanks, he said: Do this in remembrance of me. This is my Body. And having likewise taken the cup, and given thanks, he said: This is my Blood; and he delivered it to them alone.* Which indeed *evil demons have taught to be done by imitation in the mysteries and initiations* of MITHRAS. *For that bread and a cup of water are placed in the sacrifices, or the Rebirth of the one being initiated, with certain words added, you either certainly know, or can learn.* Concerning the same form of the Eucharist and other matters, Tertullian also writes in his book *On the Prescription of Heretics*: *But it is asked,* he says, *by whom is the understanding of those things interpreted which lead to Heresies? By the devil, of course, whose role is to subvert the truth, and who emulates the very things of the Divine Sacraments in the mysteries of Idols. He himself also dips certain people, namely his own believers and faithful; he promises the expiation of sins from a bath, and thus initiates them into* MITHRAS; *there he marks his soldiers on their foreheads,*

# THE UNBELIEVING BELIEVER
<note>original: "APISTOPISTVS" - a Greek-derived title meaning one who believes in falsehoods or a paradoxical believer.</note>

B 3

He also celebrates the offering of bread, introduces a representation of the resurrection, and redeems a crown beneath a sword. What it means, however, *to redeem a crown beneath a sword*, he explains in his book *On the Military Crown*: *Blush, you Roman fellow-soldiers, who are now to be judged not by Christ himself, but by some* SOLDIER OF MITHRAS. <note>Tertullian, a 2nd-century Christian author, is being quoted here to show how pagan rites "mimicked" Christian ones.</note> *When he is initiated in the cave—truly in the camps of darkness—a crown is offered to him at the point of a sword as a kind of mimicry of martyrdom. Then, after it is placed upon his head, he is instructed to push it off with his hand and shift it, perhaps, to his shoulder, saying that MITHRAS is his crown. From that moment on, he is never crowned again; he holds this as a sign to prove himself whenever he is tested regarding his oath. He is immediately recognized as a Soldier of* MITHRAS *if he casts down the crown and says that his crown resides in his God.* Let us recognize the cleverness of the devil, who affects certain divine things for the purpose of confounding us and judging us by the faithfulness of his own followers.

<margin>IV.</margin>
To this class of "fictitious martyrdom" I refer a passage from Lampridius <note>A Roman historian, one of the supposed authors of the Augustan History.</note> concerning the Emperor Commodus: *He polluted the* MITHRAIC RITES *with actual murder, whereas it was customary for something to be said or feigned there for the appearance of terror.* It was "feigned," he says: for a homicide or martyrdom was simulated by the interposition of a sword. But Commodus, such was his insolence, drove the sword home, as I believe, even unto death. He showed similar impudence when he commanded those serving Bellona <note>An ancient Roman war goddess.</note> to truly cut their arms, and forced the followers of Isis to beat themselves with pinecones even to the point of ruin; and when he himself carried the statue of Anubis, he would heavily strike the heads of the Isiacs with the face of the idol. I understand Suidas <note>A 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia.</note> to be speaking of this same simulated martyrdom under the entry for MITHRAS: *The Persians believe* MITHRAS *to be the* SUN, *and they offer many sacrifices to him. No one can be initiated into his rites unless, having passed through several stages of punishments, he shows himself to be holy and impassive.* <note>original Greek: "Μίθραν νομίζουσιν οἱ Πέρσαι εἶναι τὸν Ἥλιον... ὅσιον καὶ ἀπαθῆ."</note> *The Persians think* MITHRAS *is the* SUN, *and they slaughter many victims to him; no one can be initiated to him except through many degrees of tortures...*

# A B R A X A S

*...he showed himself to be holy and endowed with freedom from pain.* <note>The author refers to the grueling initiation rites of Mithras, where the initiate had to prove their endurance.</note> Indeed, the commentator on Gregory of Nazianzus, in his first discourse against Julian <note>referring to the Emperor Julian the Apostate</note>, teaches that there were twelve degrees of these tortures.

<margin>V.</margin> Furthermore, that Mithras, Titan, Apollo, and Osiris are one and the same, you may find at the end of the first book of the *Thebaid* <note>An epic poem by the Roman poet Statius</note>:

> ->O Father PHOEBUS. After some intervening lines:<-
> *Be present, mindful of our hospitality, and look*
> *Favorably upon the fields of Juno, whether it pleases you to be called rosy TITAN*
> *According to the rite of the Achaemenian race <note>The Persians</note>, or fruitful OSIRIS,*
> *Or MITHRAS, who beneath the rocks of the PERSIAN CAVE*
> *Twists the horns of the bull that scorns to follow.*

*The image of Mithras* (says Lutatius, the commentator on Statius) *was fashioned as if holding back the horns of a struggling Bull, by which it was signified that the Moon receives light from him when she begins to be separated from his rays. Moreover, the SUN himself was depicted with the face of a lion, wearing a tiara in the ancient dress; with both hands, as we said, grasping the horns of the ox. For the SUN impresses the principal sign and bridles the lion, namely because this god excels others in the force of his name and power, just as the lion does among other wild beasts, or because it is a rapid animal.* I have seen Mithras in many places in Rome in almost the same form. Almost, I say; for the head is not that of a lion, but human, with a small Parthian cap or tiara. With only one hand he grasps the horns of the bull, and with the other, leaning his knee upon the bull, he plunges a knife into its back. Many things are placed around him, especially in that image of Mithras which is seen on a clay tablet inserted into a wall in the house formerly called that of Octavius Zenus; where there are also larger statues of Mithras made of marble. That tablet has been published, and they gave it the name *The Type of Agriculture*; but not correctly, for it is a representation of Mithras. Furthermore, Tertullian, in his work *Against Marcion*, teaches that lions were the sacraments <note>or sacred symbols/mysteries</note> of Mithras: *Just as...*

*The sacraments of a dry and burning nature,* he says, *are philosophized by the* LIONS OF MITHRAS <note>original: "LEONES MITHRÆ". In the Mithraic mysteries, "Lion" was the fourth grade of initiation, associated with the element of fire.</note>. From this we learn why, in that figure of Abraxas published by Baronius <note>Cesare Baronio (1538–1607), a Cardinal and historian known for his work on early church history.</note>—which is of human form, for it is not always seen as one and the same—a lion is placed on the other side.

<margin>6.</margin>
Furthermore, the worship of Mithras remained for a long time even under the Christian Emperors. Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), famous for translating the Bible into Latin.</note> relates that these rites were abolished at Rome in his own time, in his *Letter to Laeta on the Education of her Daughter*: *A few years ago, your kinsman Gracchus, whose name echoes Patrician nobility, while he held the urban prefecture, did he not overthrow, break, and burn the cave of* MITHRAS, *and all those* MONSTROUS IMAGES <note>original: "PORTENTOSA SIMVLACRA"</note>, *by which the Raven, the Bridegroom, the Soldier, the Lion, the Persian, the Sun, and Father Bromius are initiated?* <note>original: "Corax, Niphus, Miles, Leo, Perses, Helios, Bromius Pater". These represent the seven grades of Mithraic initiation, though "Niphus" (Bridegroom) and "Bromius" (Bacchus/Liber) show the blending of different mystery traditions.</note> This adds light to the words of Prudentius <note>A Roman Christian poet (c. 348–413 AD).</note>, in his first book *Against Symmachus*:

> Why should I now run through the Gracchi in song,
> Those worshippers of the people, supported by the right of power,
> And foremost in the heights of the Senate, who ordered
> The idols of the gods to be torn down,
> And together with their own attendants, humbly
> Consecrated themselves to be ruled by almighty Christ.

<margin>7.</margin>
But let us illuminate those words a little: *The monstrous images by which the Raven, the Bridegroom, the Soldier, etc., are initiated.* I believe that Mithras was not depicted in only one form, as I said, but also in the form of a Lion, a Soldier, Helios (that is, the Sun), Father Bromius (that is, Bacchus), and others, even of gods and objects. Thus, many gods and various things were, as it were, initiated into Mithras himself and passed over into his sacred rites. I gather this because, since Mithras lies hidden within the name Abraxas, and I have seen Abraxas stones formed in diverse ways—even under the likeness of gods—it seems likely that the same happened to Mithras. For I have seen, besides that form which is in Baronius, one in the form of a naked man

<margin>IV.</margin> I have also seen one in the form of Isis with the name ABRAXAS attached. And since that rooster-headed monster, which I described at the beginning, very often has the word IAO placed on its shield or below at the rim—along with various letters on the back that sometimes do not allow for a connection to form a syllable—whenever that word IAO appears without the name of Abraxas, along with those words that often accompany it, such as SABAO <note>original: "SABAO," likely a corruption of the Hebrew "Sabaoth" meaning "of Hosts"</note> and the like, and those letters of disconnected sound, it must be considered a certain type of Abraxas, or belonging to him. <margin>VIII.</margin> I came across a Gem <term>Gemma: an engraved gemstone or cameo used as an amulet or seal</term> in which a man with a lion's head was carrying a bucket <term>fitula: a small ceremonial vessel or bucket used in Egyptian rituals</term>, holding in his right hand a serpent with a radiated head; in my judgment, this refers to the Isiac Abraxas <note>an Abraxas figure associated with the cult of the goddess Isis</note>, in whose sacred rites the bucket was carried. I also saw a man equipped with a sword in the manner of a soldier, but his head was that of a lion and he held the head of Medusa in his right hand; I would call him the Soldier Abraxas, or the Soldier Mithras <note>Mithras was a Persian sun god whose mystery cult was popular among Roman soldiers</note>. For a true soldier devoted to Mithras is not to be understood here, as I see some have felt, since this Soldier is numbered among the portentous <note>monstrous or supernatural</note> images. I have seen a Gem which had a small bird on one side carrying a branch (perhaps of laurel), and on the other side the name ABRAXAS was placed.

Therefore, a similar monster with only two wings will also belong to the same, having a phallus attached, under whose feet are fashioned, within a short space, various small animals. I would willingly give this monster the name of Phaethon or Protogonus <note>The "First-Born" creator deity in Orphic cosmogony</note>, especially that one to whom Orpheus addressed a Hymn. The wings are perhaps to be referred to the seasons of the year, which fly past; in the primary division there are two, Summer and Winter; in the secondary there are four: Spring, Summer, Autumn, and Winter. The phallus is attached to denote generation: for *God and the Sun*, as the Philosopher <note>Likely referring to Aristotle</note> bears witness, *generate all things*. And those small animals are attached to indicate the things produced by the Sun. From which it is permitted to gather further, that whenever there are found in Gems ani-

# ABRAXAS

Animals standing upright on their feet, such as dogs with their private parts exposed, surrounded by words and letters of disconnected sounds, are to be referred to the same subject. Furthermore, one might judge that the naked winged monster belongs to Abraxas from the fact that our first monster with the head of a cock has sometimes been seen by me with similar wings, and in the same number. For that Abraxas with the head of a bird is not seen figured in one and the same way. The head is not always of the same bird; most often it is a cock, sometimes like a hawk or another bird of prey, and you might sometimes say it is a raven. And Antoine le Pois <note>A 16th-century French physician and antiquarian known for his work on ancient coins and gems.</note>, the Antiquarian, in his French book on Engravings, includes one figure which he did not recognize, yet it is of Abraxas, which has an eagle's head with the addition of stag's horns. Indeed, it even has the head of a lion, says Camillo Leonardi <note>An Italian astronomer and physician, author of "The Mirror of Stones" (Speculum Lapidum, 1502).</note> in *The Mirror of Stones*; and I myself have a cast of an Abraxas of that kind. The feet most often end in two serpents, sometimes in only one; it is most often without wings, but sometimes winged at the shoulders and chest.

<margin>10.</margin> It is also permitted to proceed to the point that, since we see that Phaëthon <note>In Greek mythology, the son of the Sun god Helios; here used as a solar symbol.</note> whom I named above, grasping or carrying a crab, whenever we see a crab alone on gems with letters and words, it will be a symbol of Abraxas. Indeed, this applies also to lizards and scarab beetles, all of which appear to be solar animals, or at least dedicated to Mithras <note>A Persian sun god whose mystery cult was popular in the Roman Empire.</note> and Abraxas. And the crab is indeed also found as a companion of the true Mithras on the clay tablet mentioned by me. I have seen a gem in which, on one side, there was a certain insect, perhaps a scarab beetle, and around it a serpent in a circle, grasping its tail with its head <note>The Ouroboros, an ancient symbol of eternity and the cyclical nature of the universe.</note>, by which I believe the year is signified; on the other side was IAW SABAOTH ADONAI <note>original: "ΙΑΩ CΑΒΑΩ ΑΔΩΝΕΙ" — These are Greek transcriptions of Hebrew names for God: Yahweh, Sabaoth (Hosts), and Adonai (Lord), frequently used in Gnostic magic.</note>. And nothing is more frequent than scarab beetles fashioned from the solid body of a gem, which were worn perforated.

<margin>11.</margin> Let us also add to the crowd of Abraxas figures all those gems,

# THE UNBELIEVING BELIEVER
<note>original: "APISTOPISTVS" - a Greek-derived term for one who claims faith but lacks it.</note>

...those which feature a straight serpent with a coiled tail and a radiated head, particularly that of a lion. For in that figure of Abraxas (which I mentioned above under the guise of a priest of Isis carrying a bucket in one hand and a serpent in the other), the serpent had a radiated head. If a box <note>original: "pyxis," a small cylindrical container for medicine or relics</note> is placed anywhere with that same serpent, let that be the Abraxas-Aesculapius <note>The god of healing</note>. Thus it is finally true that Abraxas is like a Trojan Horse; just as that horse carried the Greek heroes in its belly, so Abraxas hides in his belly the families of all the Gods—whether Egyptian, Greek, Latin, or Persian—and compels them to be steeped in and consecrated by his own mysteries. Truly these are the Basilidian "Unbelieving Believers," that is, purely unfaithful under the name of the faithful, Pagan wolves under the fleece of the Christian sheep. Their father, Simon Magus <note>A biblical figure considered the first heretic</note>, went before them and held the torch in this kind of impiety. Epiphanius says regarding the Heresy of the Simonians: *[Simon] delivered to them a certain image, as if it were his own, and they worship it in the form of Jupiter; furthermore, he delivered to them another image of Helen in the figure of Minerva; and those who have been deceived by him worship these.* And Theodoret says of the Valentinians, who are a branch of the Gnostics: *They celebrate, he says, the Festivals of the Heathens.*

<margin>XII.</margin>
Moreover, during the time of these heretics, the Sun was held in great veneration among the Romans, so much so that other gods almost began to seem worthless compared to his worship. Frequent mention is made of the god Mithras <note>A Persian sun god popular among Roman soldiers</note> in both sacred and profane writers who wrote in the age of these heretics; his sacred rites endured even under the Christian Emperors. This is gathered from the fact that Saint Jerome and Prudentius testify that these rites were abolished in their own time, as I mentioned above. There is an ancient inscription which, under the Consuls Gratian and Merobaudes, names Rufus Ceionia as a devotee of Mithras:

> *The same Augur, venerable worshiper of the triple Diana,*
> *And Babylonian Priest of the temple of the treacherous* MITHRAS.

<summary>The author argues that the Gnostic figure Abraxas acts as a "Trojan Horse" for paganism within Christianity, linking Gnostic practices to the cults of Simon Magus and the Roman worship of Mithras.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the spread of Mithraic worship and its conflict with early Christianity, following the previous page's focus on Roman cults.</meta>

# A B R A X A S

Mithras was not only worshipped in Rome, but had also forced his way into Greece. Indeed, Epiphanius, in his book against Heresies <note>referring to the *Panarion*, a 4th-century work cataloging various sects</note> when discussing Marcion, says that *Epimenides, the ancient philosopher*, was a priest of the temple of <term>MITHRAS</term>, or the <term>SUN</term> <note>original: "SOLIS"</note>, among the Cretans. Furthermore, the leaders of the Churches in Antioch labored to tear down the sacred rites of that god at almost the same time as, or a little before, the Gracchi did so in Rome. For thus Nicephorus Callistus reports in the tenth book of his History: *Moreover, at that time also, what had been done concerning the* <term>MITHRAEUM</term> <note>original: "MITHRÆO"</note>, *(that is, the shrine of Mithras), increased the riot moved against him (namely George of Alexandria, who was an Arian)* <note>George of Cappadocia, an Arian bishop who was lynched by a mob in 361 AD</note>. *For Constantius had granted that long-neglected place to the Church of Alexandria. George, however, while clearing it out so that he might raise a sacred house to God, discovered a subterranean cave in which there were idols, and certain other symbols and signs of Greek trifles and deceptions, so to speak, and of those who had been instructed and initiated into the rites by them. But the Greeks* <note>here meaning Pagans or non-Christians</note>, *affected by shame because their mysteries and secrets were revealed, immediately gathered a band and made an attack on the Christians with swords, stones, and clubs; they killed many, wounded some, and even drove some of them onto crosses to insult their doctrine, and then departed. George left the work he had begun unfinished.* Behold how tenaciously the Greeks, that is the Pagans, clung to those sacred rites in that age.

<margin>XIII.</margin> Indeed, even that Apostate Julian, when he defected from the true God, turned to no other than his own <term>HELIOS</term> <note>original Greek: ΗΛΙΟΝ</note>, that is, the <term>SUN</term>, as he himself confesses in his *Saturnalia*. He even composed a Hymn of Praise to his god, the <term>SUN</term>. It is no wonder, therefore, if the corrupters of the Christian Faith and Religion—or rather the deserters—fled to the camps of Mithras and of a certain Sun, and devoted themselves to his sacred rites and profane mysteries.

It also highlights Emperor Julian the Apostate's personal devotion to the Sun god as a primary rival to Christianity.</summary>

# THE UNBELIEVING BELIEVER
<note>original: "APISTOPISTVS"</note>

C 3

and execrable things. The symbols of these sacred rites expressed on the Gems are partly explained and clear now, and we will understand them better if we remember that Mithras <note>The Persian god of light and the sun, whose mystery cult was popular in the Roman Empire</note> is the same as Apollo, the same as Osiris, and many other gods. For to take one example for many: if it is asked why on one side there is a raven with a laurel branch, and on the other the name Abraxas <note>A mystical word of power used by Gnostics, often representing the supreme deity</note>; we shall say that the raven and the laurel are dedicated to Apollo, whom Mithras includes among his initiates. Why is the head of the first monster sometimes that of a raven, as it seems to me? For the same reason. Why a kite, or a hawk? Because, unless I am mistaken, just as the lion is dedicated to Mithras among the quadrupeds, so among the birds is the kite, a bird of prey. Or because, as Ovid says:

> The Persian placates Hyperion <note>The Sun</note> girt with rays with a horse,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Lest a slow victim be given to the swift god;

so the rapid birds, swift for prey, are assigned to Mithras. For regarding the rooster, it is easy to think of it as the bird that heralds the Sun and the light.

<margin>XIII.</margin>
The blessed Jerome <note>St. Jerome, the 4th-century Church Father</note> rightly called the images of the Mithraic cave "portentous," just as these figures on the Gems are. But what shall we say? The worshipers of Mithras themselves were entirely portentous, and nothing but mere masks. Let us hear Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher and critic of Christianity</note> in the first book *On Abstinence from Eating Flesh*: *In the mysteries of Mithras, wanting to hint at our commonality with the animals, they are accustomed to designate us through animal names; so that they call the initiates who participate in the same rites Lions, the women Hyenas, and those who serve Ravens. Regarding the "Fathers," these are addressed as Eagles and Hawks. When one receives the Lion-rites, he puts on all kinds of animal forms.* <note>original Greek: "Ἐν τοῖς τοῦ Μίθρα μυστηρίοις... παντοδαπὰς ζώων μορφάς."</note> *In the mysteries*, he says, *of MITHRAS, wishing to indicate the communion which we have with animals, they have accustomed themselves to distinguish us by the names of animals; so much so that those who are already initiated and partakers of the same sacred rites, they call LIONS,*

<summary>This page explores the animal symbolism in Mithraic mysteries, linking the god Mithras to Apollo and explaining why ravens, lions, and hawks appear on Gnostic gems. It cites Porphyry to describe the different animal-themed ranks of initiates within the cult.</summary>

<keywords>Mithras, Gnosticism, Abraxas, Apollo, Porphyry, animal symbolism, initiation rites, sun worship, St. Jerome</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the symbolic animal forms used in Gnostic and pagan iconography, specifically focusing on the hybrid nature of the Abraxas figure.</meta>

# ABRAXAS

...and the women themselves they call **HYENAS**; while those serving are called **RAVENS**. Likewise among the "Fathers"; for these are called **EAGLES** and **HAWKS**. And he who assumes the **LEONINE** <note>Lion-like</note> nature, clothes himself in **ALL KINDS OF ANIMAL FORMS**.

XV. Moreover, that the feet end in serpents is no less a marvel than the rest. I know that the Pagans did not lack two-formed Gods. Tertullian <note>An early Christian author from Carthage, c. 155–220 AD</note> says in his *Apology*: *“But they ought to have immediately worshiped a **TWO-FORMED DEITY**, because they accepted Gods mixed with **LION** and **DOG** heads, horned from **GOATS** and **RAMS**, **GOATS** from the loins, **SERPENTS** from the leg, and **WINGED** from the sole or the back.”* The Giants also, who fought against Jupiter, were imagined by Antiquity to have had serpents for feet. Ovid <note>Famous Roman poet</note> writes in the *Tristia*, Book IV, Elegy III:

> Both the Sphinx and Harpies, and the **SERPENT-LEGGED** giants,
> And hundred-handed Gyges, and the man who was half-bull.

And in Book V of the *Fasti*:

> Earth brought forth fierce offspring, **IMMENSE MONSTERS**, the giants,
> Who dared to go against the home of Jupiter.
> She gave them a thousand hands, and **SNAKES INSTEAD OF LEGS**;
> And said: "Take up arms against the great Gods."

Certainly, to such a monster as the *Apistopisti* <note>A derogatory term likely meaning "unbelievers" or "faithless ones," referring here to the Gnostics</note> depicted, serpents had to be given for legs, because like a giant it would take up arms against the great God and attempt to thrust him from his throne. They also gave snaky feet to Erichthonius <note>A legendary early ruler of Athens</note>, of whom Servius <note>A 4th-century grammarian and commentator on Virgil</note> says, regarding that passage in Virgil’s *Georgics*, Book II:

> Erichthonius was the first who dared to join the chariot
> And four horses, and to stand as victor upon the rapid wheels:

He says: *“To hide the foulness of his feet, he used a chariot with joined horses, because he had **SNAKY FEET**.”* Of this Erichthonius...
