<keywords>Abraxas, Gnosticism, animal symbolism, Tertullian, Ovid, Giants, serpent-legged, Erichthonius, mythology, hybrid deities</keywords>

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the origins of the name Abraxas; this page continues the mythological comparison of his birth and the symbolism of his physical attributes.</meta>

# THE UNBELIEVING BELIEVER

Those who have spent even a little time studying myths know of his origin, and both Servius <note>A 4th-century Roman grammarian and commentator on Virgil</note> and Lactantius <note>An early Christian author (c. 250 – c. 325) who became an advisor to Constantine I</note> in the first book of his *Divine Institutes* place him in the same category. I will omit this for the present, as well as the double derivation of his name, which anyone who wishes may seek from other sources. I add only this: there is a great affinity between the births of Erichthonius <note>A legendary early ruler of Athens, born from the earth</note> and Mithras <note>The primary deity of the Roman mystery cult Mithraism</note>, as witnessed by Saint Jerome in his argument against Jovinian. I shall use Jerome's own words on this matter: *“Who,” he says (referring to Erichthonius and Mithras), “according to the myths of the Pagans, were generated either in a stone or in the earth, from the mere heat of lust.”* To show this similarity of birth, I believe the "Unbelieving Believers" <note>original: "Apistopistis" — likely referring to the Gnostics who claimed true faith but were viewed as heretics</note> gave Abraxas snake-like feet, just like those of Erichthonius. And indeed, the sacred rites of Basilides <note>An early Gnostic religious teacher in Alexandria</note> were impure; although they are deeply shameful, Saint Epiphanius—and Baronius <note>Cesare Baronio (1538–1607), a cardinal and ecclesiastical historian</note> following him—did not blush to speak of them.

<margin>16.</margin>
But why does this monster hold a shield in one hand and a whip in the other, or sometimes have a shield at his feet? Is it so that he may fight like a giant against the Most High God? Anyone saying so would seem to have a plausible reason. Yet I think it more truthful to say that these gems were fashioned as amulets <term>amuleta: charms worn to protect the owner from evil or disease</term> to ward off evils. Perhaps they wished to signify that Abraxas would defend his believers and protect them with a shield, while punishing his opponents with a whip; for this reason, his chest is very often covered by a breastplate. This relates to what I mentioned above: our monster was found with an inscription on the back: *Slayer of Giants* <note>original Greek: ΓΙΓΑΝΤΟΛΕΤΟΡ (Gigantoleter)</note>; meaning that, like a certain Jupiter, he would destroy the giants and adversaries rising up against him.

<margin>17.</margin>
Furthermore, I noted that these were amulets from a coin marked on one side with the usual monster of Abraxas, and on the other having a figure fashioned with three heads. One of these appeared to be that of Isis, the goddess whose wrath they greatly feared.

<summary>This page explores the mythological parallels between Abraxas and figures like Erichthonius, while interpreting the deity's shield and whip as symbols of protection and punishment on magical amulets.</summary>
<keywords>Abraxas, Gnosticism, amulets, Mithras, Erichthonius, Basilides, protective magic, iconography</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient amulets and the syncretism between pagan imagery and early Christian practices, specifically focusing on the use of coins as protective charms.</meta>

# ABRAXAS

The Pagans feared her greatly because, as they believed, when provoked she would bring about heavy plagues and hardships. Add to this that there was a hole in the coin for threading a small cord, so that it might be worn around the neck; for it is well established that the Ancients wore suspended coins, not only against evils, but also, as I believe, to procure good things. Saint Chrysostom <note>John Chrysostom (c. 347–407 AD), an influential Early Church Father and Archbishop of Constantinople.</note> mentions this regarding the image of Alexander the Great in his 25th Homily to the People of Antioch: *But what would someone say of those who use incantations and amulets <note>original: "ligaturis," referring to objects bound to the body for magical protection.</note>, and of those who bind* GOLDEN COINS OF ALEXANDER THE MACEDONIAN *to their head or feet? Tell me, are these our hopes, that after the cross and death of the Lord, we should have hope of salvation in the image of a pagan king?* Furthermore, the three-headed depiction of the head on that gold coin approaches the figure of Hecate <note>A goddess in Greek mythology associated with crossroads, magic, and the underworld.</note>, whom Minutius Felix <note>A 2nd-3rd century Christian apologist.</note> depicts in very few words along with the forms of many gods. *Diana*, he says, *is meanwhile a huntress with her robes tucked up high, and the Ephesian Diana is built with many breasts and protrusions, while* TRIVIA <note>A Roman name for Hecate, meaning "of the three ways."</note> IS TERRIFYING WITH THREE HEADS AND MANY HANDS. Moreover, on that coin, besides the three heads, there are six hands. Truly, Trivia is Hecate, although Natalis Comes <note>Noel Conti (1520–1582), an Italian mythologist.</note> says in the third book of his *Mythology*: *Of the three heads of Trivia, the right was a horse's, the left a dog's, and the middle a human's.* They used to invoke this Hecate as a Sorceress. Theocritus <note>A Greek poet of the 3rd century BC.</note> says in his *The Sorceress*:

> <note>original Greek: Τὰ χθονία δ' Ἑκάτᾳ...</note>
> *And to the infernal* HECATE, *at whom the dogs tremble*
> *As she comes through the corpses of the dead and through the black blood.*
> *Hail, terrible* HECATE, *and be our companion even unto the end.*

->Gnosti-<-

# THE UNBELIEVING BELIEVER
<note>original: "APISTOPISTVS" — a Greek-derived title likely referring to the paradoxical nature of Gnostic faith or the author's critique of it.</note>

<margin>18.</margin>
The ancient writers record that the Gnostics were devoted to the magical arts. Jerome, in his 29th Letter to Theodora, states: *“And since we have made mention of heresy, how can our friend Lucinus be praised enough by the trumpet of eloquence? For while the most filthy heresy of* BASILIDES <note>A major Gnostic teacher in Alexandria (2nd century AD) who taught that the world was created by lower powers.</note> *was raging through Spain, and like a plague or disease was wasting the entire province between the Pyrenees and the Ocean, he maintained the purity of the Church’s Faith. He by no means accepted Armagil, Barbelon,* ABRAXAS, *Balsamo, and the ridiculous Leusibora, and other monstrosities rather than names. These they draw, as it were, from Hebrew sources to stir up the minds of the ignorant and of silly women; terrifying even the simple-minded with their foreign sound, so that they might marvel more at what they do not understand.”*

Irenaeus—a man of Apostolic times and a disciple of Papias (who was a listener of John the Evangelist) and Bishop of the Church at Lyons—reports that a certain Marcus, descending from the lineage of BASILIDES THE GNOSTIC, first came to the Gauls. He defiled those regions through which the Rhone and Garonne rivers flow with his teaching. He especially seduced noble women by promising certain hidden mysteries in secret, winning their affection through magical arts and secret bodily pleasures. From there, crossing the Pyrenees, he occupied the Spanish lands. He made it his pursuit to seek out the homes of the wealthy, and especially the women within them, who are led away by various desires, always learning and never arriving at the knowledge of the truth.

We learn from this how widely that heresy wandered, and that it was not contained within narrow boundaries. Thus, it is no wonder that so many Gems belonging to its followers are found. On these gems is found not only the carving of some monstrous animal, or a god, or some other object, but also letters. Sometimes a great abundance of these letters is seen arranged in many lines, which cannot be combined into a single word or utterance, as I have warned before. This method...

<summary>The author cites Jerome and Irenaeus to describe the spread of the Basilidian Gnostic heresy through France and Spain, emphasizing their use of magical incantations and inscribed gems to deceive the wealthy.</summary>
<keywords>Gnosticism, Basilides, Jerome, Irenaeus, Abraxas, magic, Gnostic gems, Spain, Gaul, Marcus the Gnostic</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Gnostic sects, specifically the Basilidians, following the previous page's introduction to their mystical systems.</meta>

-># ABRAXAS<-

<note>catchword: &amp; Gnostics</note>

...must be referred back to their secrets, which they did not wish to be understood by men. Theodoret <note>Theodoret of Cyrus, a 5th-century theologian and church historian</note> says of the Basilidians: *And these men, like the followers of Menander and Simon, use incantations and the magic art of every kind of sorcery, fashioning certain names of Angels; some of whom they say hold the first heaven, others the second, and so on in order up to the three hundred and sixty-fifth. Indeed, they name the Savior Lord as Kaulakauam <note>original: "Kaulakauam" – a mystical name likely derived from a Hebrew phrase in Isaiah 28:10, used by Gnostics to refer to the Savior</note>. They say these are secrets which cannot be understood by everyone, but only by one in a thousand.*

<margin>XIX.</margin> Sometimes letters form a word, such as that which I mentioned, IAO <note>original: "IAO" – a Greek transcription of the Hebrew Tetragrammaton (Yahweh), frequently found on magical gems</note>, which is very frequent in the Abraxas stones, and serves as a sort of password for that sect and evidence of the Abraxas-related madness. Concerning this, Epiphanius <note>Bishop of Salamis, known for his "Panarion" against heresies</note> says in Heresy XXVI, which is that of the Gnostics: *Furthermore, he says, they claim these are the names of the greater Princes, according to them: in the first heaven is the Prince IAO; in the second is Saclan, the Prince of fornication; in the third is the Prince Seth; in the fourth they say is Daden (for they assert there is a fourth and third heaven); and even another fifth, in which they say is Eloaeus, who is also called Adoneus; in the sixth, some say is Ialdabaoth, others Elilaeus; others even establish a seventh heaven, in which is Sabaoth; others deny this and pronounce that Ialdabaoth is in the seventh; and in the eighth is she who is called Barbelo.* Even the Valentinians, the offspring of the Gnostics, usurped this voice in their dreams. Theodoret says in the same Heresy: *All these things they name within the first fullness <note>original: "pleroma" – the totality of divine powers</note>; but those things said to be outside the fullness are these: <term>Epinoian</term> <note>original: ἐπίνοιαν – "thought" or "invention"</note>, that is, the thought of that Wisdom, which they also call the Formless Conception, and by name they call Achamoth; she, having been made outside the fullness, would dwell in a certain shadow and empty space; but he who is called Christ by others, moved by pity, was slain by that which they call <term>Horon</term> <note>original: ὅρον – "Boundary" or "Limit"</note>.*

<summary>This page details the complex Gnostic cosmology of the Basilidians and Valentinians, listing the mystical names of rulers inhabiting various celestial spheres and explaining the origins of the figure Achamoth.</summary>
<keywords>Basilidians, IAO, Gnostics, Kaulakauam, Ialdabaoth, Sabaoth, Barbelo, Achamoth, Epiphanius, Theodoret, celestial spheres, magic names</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Gnostic sects and their use of mystical names, specifically focusing on the followers of Marcus and the origins of the name IAO.</meta>

# THE UNBELIEVING BELIEVER

And they say that he gave form to the Cross according to its substance, but left it void of knowledge; and after these things, he withdrew his own power into himself. When that power perceived the light surrounding it, it ran toward the inquiry of that light; but it was prohibited from proceeding further by Horus <note>In Gnostic mythology, Horus (or Horos) acts as a "Limiter" or "Boundary" between the divine realm and the lower world</note>, when he had spoken the name **IAO**. For they say that this name originated from that event. Epiphanius <note>A 4th-century bishop known for his "Panarion," a massive work refuting various heresies</note> also places this among the Marcosians <note>Followers of Marcus the Gnostic, known for their elaborate rituals and use of numerology</note>: *Others indeed*, he says, *add certain Hebrew names, so that they might be a source of greater wonder to those who are being initiated, in this manner: Basema, Chamosse Baaanora, Mistadia, Ruada, Custa, Babophor, Kalachthei.* The interpretation of these is as follows: *Above every power of the Father, I invoke that which is named Light, and the Good Spirit, and Life, because you have reigned in the body.* Others again add a formula of redemption in these words: *The name hidden from every Deity and Dominion and Truth, which JESUS the Nazarene put on in the life of the light of Christ, of the Christ living through the Holy Spirit for Angelic redemption, the name of restoration, Messiah, Uphareg, Namempsaman, Chaldean, Mosomedaëa, Acphrana, Plaöua, JESUS Nazaria.* And the interpretation of these is: *I do not divide the Spirit, heart, and super-celestial merciful power of Christ. May I enjoy your name, Savior of truth!* And these are the words spoken by those who perform the initiation. *But he who has been initiated responds: I am strengthened, and redeemed, and I redeem my soul from this age, and from all things that are with it, in the name of **IAO**, who redeemed his soul for redemption in the living Christ.* Then all those present shout: *Peace to all upon whom this name rests.* Then they anoint the initiated person with the juice of balsam. Now Marcus, from whom the Marcosians are named, succeeded Secundus, Epiphanius, Ptolemy, and Valentinus, who trace their origin from the Gnostics, whose father was Basilides; therefore it is no wonder if the name **IAO** was handed down to them through succession.

<summary>This page details the mystical rituals and secret Hebrew-sounding formulas used by the Marcosian Gnostics, specifically explaining the significance of the name IAO in their initiation rites. It traces the lineage of these teachings from Basilides and Valentinus down to Marcus.</summary>

<keywords>Gnosticism, IAO, Marcosians, Epiphanius, initiation rituals, Basilides, Valentinus, redemption, mystical names</keywords>

# A B R A X A S

<margin>20.</margin> Frequently, moreover, the name <term>IAO</term> <note>A Greek transliteration of the Hebrew Tetragrammaton (Yahweh)</note> is found on gems alongside <term>SABAO</term>, which I seem to recall seeing carved as <term>SABAOT</term> <note>From the Hebrew 'Sabaoth', meaning 'of Hosts'</note> as well; I previously noted briefly, citing Epiphanius, that this was perversely usurped by the heretics. But the same author <note>Epiphanius of Salamis, a 4th-century bishop known for his 'Panarion' against heresies</note> writes more extensively on this regarding the Nicolaitans: *“And from here (that is, from the Nicolaitans) those who boast of a falsely named knowledge began to arise in the world—I mean the Gnostics, and the Phibionites, and the followers of Epiphanes, the Stratiotics and the Levitics, and many others. For each of these, dragging their own sect toward their own whims, devised innumerable ways of malice. For some of them glorify a certain Barbelo, whom they say is above in the eighth heaven. They say she was produced by the Father, but others say she is the mother of Ialdabaoth, and others say of Sabaoth; and they say the son of the latter obtained dominion over the seventh heaven through a certain audacity and tyranny. He said to those below: 'I am,' he said, 'the first, and he who comes after, and besides me there is no other.' But Barbelo herself heard this speech and wept.”* And further down: *“Furthermore, others from the aforementioned groups glorify Ialdabaoth, and they say he is the first son, as I said, of Barbelo, and for that reason they say honor must be given to him because he revealed many things; whence they fashion and invent certain books in the name of Ialdabaoth himself, and infinite barbaric nomenclatures of Princes and Powers in every single heaven.”*

Saint Jerome says in his *Catalog of Writers* that these terms were invented to cause terror: *“Agrippa, surnamed Castor, a very learned man, argued most strongly against the twenty-four volumes of the heretic <term>BASILIDES</term> which he had composed on the Gospel, exposing all his mysteries and enumerating the prophets Barcabas and Marcob, and certain other barbaric names to terrify the listeners, as well as his greatest god <term>ABRAXAS</term>, who is calculated according to the Greek numbering as if containing the year.”* <note>The Greek letters of Abraxas add up to 365, the number of days in a year.</note> In the same manner, Theodoret, in his work on heresy...

<summary>This page examines the Gnostic appropriation of Hebrew divine names like Sabaoth and the mythological origins of figures like Ialdabaoth and Barbelo, citing early Church Fathers to argue these names were used to intimidate the unlearned.</summary>

# THE UNBELIEVING BELIEVER
<note>original: "APISTOPISTVS"</note>

<insert>Signature: D 3</insert>

the heresy of Marcus: *They also mix in Hebrew names, in order to strike terror into those who are being initiated.* <note>Marcus was a 2nd-century Gnostic teacher known for using numerology and mystical invocations.</note> Theodoret also mentions this regarding the name *Sabaoth* <note>Hebrew: "Lord of Hosts"</note> among the Ascodryti: *They place an anathema upon baptism and the communion of the mysteries, as things performed in the name of* SABAOTH. *For they say that he exercises a tyranny in the seventh heaven. Moreover, they say that the nourishment of the Princes <note>The Archons or planetary rulers in Gnostic belief</note> is souls, and without this they cannot live; but that the devil is the son of* SABAOTH, *and that* SABAOTH *is the God of the Jews, but the son, since he is evil, does not honor him, but opposes him in all things.* Epiphanius records what form they believed this Sabaoth to have in his *Heresy of the Gnostics*: *Some hand down,* he says, *that* SABAOTH *has the form of a donkey, others the form of a pig; for which reason, they say, it was commanded to the Jews that they should not eat pork.* It is a marvel what Constantinus Geoponicus <note>A 10th-century compiler of agricultural lore</note> reports concerning these two words IAO SABAOTH <note>IAO is a Greek rendering of the Tetragrammaton, the four-letter name of God</note>, that certain people use them for catching fish.

<margin>XXI.</margin> I shall not pursue the remaining words of the Abraxas-delusion, even though they are found expressed both on Gems and in the writings of the Fathers, such as ADONEUS <note>A Greek-Gnostic variation of "Adonai" or "Lord"</note>, mentioned by me above from Epiphanius, and the names of Angels familiar from Holy Scripture, with which I have seen mixed others that are not found therein. Nor shall I say anything further regarding the many other remaining words which the Gems indeed display, but the books of the Ancients do not contain; for it is easy to judge from those I have explained that the rest are of the same grain; just as the same must be concluded regarding certain added characters, as I said above regarding letters which, when connected in a certain position, do not produce a word. For I may confirm from Epiphanius's account of the Carpocratians (who called themselves Gnostics, according to the testimony of Irenaeus on Carpocrates) what I pointed out above from Theodoret: *Magic was devised among them,*

<summary>This page examines how Gnostic sects like the Carpocratians and Ascodryti misappropriated Hebrew divine names (Sabaoth, Iao) for magical rituals and distorted their meanings to fit a dualistic cosmology. It highlights the use of these names on ancient "Abraxas" gems and in superstitious practices like fishing charms.</summary>

-># ABRAXAS<-

*They have invented diverse incantations for every kind of scheme, for love and for allurements: indeed, they even draw to themselves an attendant demon <note>original: "assessorem daemonem," a familiar spirit believed to assist the magician</note> for this purpose, so that through many illusions and great power they may dominate all whom they wish, and each may dare to undertake any action, surely deceiving themselves toward the perfection of their own blinded minds.* However, so that they might veil their magical arts, they chose specific stones for individual images, so that the effect might seem to arise from the nature of the stone itself: for instance, for that monster which I described at the beginning <note>referring to the Abraxas figure with a rooster's head and serpent legs</note>, jasper was the proper stone, as I have almost always seen, and as is also testified by Vincent of Beauvais <note>a 13th-century Dominican friar and encyclopedist</note> in his *Natural Mirror*, who describes this monster of ours and says it was recognized by Thetel the Jew <note>an author of a medieval lapidary, or book on the properties of stones</note>. Nor was that serpent with the radiated head carved on any stone other than jasper, as Galen <note>the famous Greek physician of the 2nd century AD</note> asserts in the ninth book of his *On the Power of Simple Medicines*; I will therefore provide his words so that it may be established, even by a Pagan witness, that images provide nothing if the natural virtue is already present in the stone: *Indeed, he says, some testify that a property exists in certain stones of the kind which* GREEN JASPER *truly possesses, aiding both the stomach and the mouth of the stomach if it is tied on: some also insert it into a* RING, *and carve on it a dragon having rays, just as King Nechepsos <note>an Egyptian pharaoh to whom early astrological and medical texts were attributed</note> wrote in his thirteenth book. I have suitable experience of this stone, for I made a necklace of such small stones and hung it from the neck, lowered so that the stones touched the mouth of the stomach. They seemed to help no less than if they had the carving which Nechepsos described.* This passage warns us that even these "unfaithful believers" <note>original: "Apistopistos," a Greek-derived term for those who mix faith with superstition</note> used images taken from the Gentiles <note>non-Christians or Pagans</note>.

<margin>XXII.</margin> But if it is to be considered whether any fruit may be gathered from the inspection of these Gems, it will be this: that we may consider even from these small stones, and not only from the reading of the Fathers, we may learn...

He notes that these "Abraxas" gems were often adapted from much older Egyptian and Pagan traditions.</summary>

# THE UNBELIEVING BELIEVER
<note>original: "APISTOPISTVS" - a Greek-derived title meaning one who is both faithless and faithful, or perhaps a "doubting believer."</note>

...let us examine with what great force the devil attacked the newborn Church of Christ and strove to sow weeds <note>original: "zizania" - referring to the parable of the wheat and the tares in Matthew 13</note> over the good seed. Concerning this matter, Epiphanius <note>A 4th-century Bishop of Salamis known for his "Panarion," a massive work refuting heresies.</note> writes in his chapter on the Heresy of the Sethians <note>A Gnostic sect that revered the biblical Seth as a divine figure.</note>: *“Truly, the devil has planted this entire scheme into the minds of men. One may see and marvel at how he deceived man with many absurdities and distracted him toward iniquity: toward fornications, adulteries, lasciviousness, the insane worship of idols, deceptions, the shedding of blood, robberies, insatiable greeds, gluttonies, and all such things. Yet, never before the coming of Christ did he dare to speak any blasphemous word against his Lord, or to think of rising up against Him. For he awaited the coming of Christ, as if saying: ‘It is written of you, He has given His angels charge over you, and they shall bear you up in their hands.’ <note>A quote from Psalm 91, famously used by the devil during the Temptation of Christ.</note> For he always heard the Prophets announcing the coming of Christ and the future redemption of those who have sinned and perform penance through Christ; and he thought that he himself might obtain some mercy. But when that miserable one saw that Christ had not undertaken any care for his own salvation, he vomited forth blasphemies against the Lord with an open mouth, instilling in men the opinion that they should deny those things which truly exist, and seek after those things which do not exist.”*

Theodoret <note>A 5th-century theologian and Bishop of Cyrrhus.</note> also writes at the beginning of the second book of his *Compendium of Heretical Fables*: *“What sort of branches the most bitter root of Simon <note>Simon Magus, often considered by early church writers as the 'father of all heresies.'</note> produced, and how deadly those fruits were, we have clearly shown in the first book. We recounted the long-windedness of their fables, cutting them as short as possible, for we dreaded to repeat their greatest blasphemies. Regarding their wicked mysteries, we passed over in silence those which were too impious and cursed; for we judged that we would be sinning if we taught them to those who are ignorant of them. For not even those who have entirely enslaved themselves to pleasure and lust would think of the things those men wickedly and criminally dared to do, but would rather turn away from them as from something cursed. These things were instilled into their minds by...”*

->the demon<-
<margin>demon</margin>

<summary>This page cites the Church Fathers Epiphanius and Theodoret to explain how the devil, realizing he would not be saved by Christ, turned to instigating blasphemy and heresy through figures like Simon Magus.</summary>
<keywords>Epiphanius, Sethians, Theodoret, Simon Magus, origins of heresy, devil's rebellion, early church history, blasphemy</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the discussion on the "Abraxas" gems, transitioning from the physical description of the artifacts to a historical and theological defense of early Christianity against Gnostic heresies.</meta>

# ABRAXAS THE UNBELIEVER.

*The most wicked demon, when he had undertaken a war against the herald of truth, attempted to spread a bad reputation and low esteem against those who worship Him rightly. For he thought that a shared name would ensure that the curse fell upon everyone: for both the teachers of accursed doctrines and those who were the preachers of the Gospel Decrees were called "Christians." However, whoever was ignorant of the difference thought that all who shared that one name were impious and wicked. And the inventor and architect of wickedness dared these things as soon as the Light of truth arose and sent out the divine Apostles like rays into the whole world. For while they were still surviving, both Simon <note>Simon Magus, often considered the first heretic</note> arose, and Menander, and other disciples of Simon whom we mentioned before. In nearly the same manner, Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, the "Father of Church History"</note> says in the fourth book of his Ecclesiastical History, after he treated Basilides and Carpocrates: Therefore the devil, who takes joy from evil deeds, used these men as the ministers of his own wickedness; by their persuasion, those who had received the Faith were not only led into deception and broken off as if bound for a bitter and miserable destruction, but they also provided the Gentiles, who were strangers to the Faith, an opportunity and a handle for petulantly and impiously disparaging the most holy Word of God. This was done with the clear plan that the rumor of their shameful way of life, spread everywhere, would burn a foul mark of ignominy upon the entire multitude of Christians. From this it certainly happened that a certain nefarious opinion was stirred up about us—one most foreign to all reason—which persuaded the minds of unbelievers far and wide.* We see, therefore, that even from these gems some material for pious consideration is supplied. I have brought forth what I felt concerning them, and I hope it will not be unpleasing to those studious of Antiquity.

->T H E  E N D.<-

<summary>The author concludes his work by citing Eusebius to explain how early Gnostic heretics like Simon Magus and Basilides tarnished the reputation of all Christians, using the "Abraxas" gems as a starting point for this historical reflection.</summary>
<keywords>Abraxas, Simon Magus, Menander, Eusebius, Basilides, Carpocrates, Christians, Gnostics, Antiquity, Church History</keywords>

->*From where does the multiform <note>original: "Πολύμορφος" (Polymorphos)</note> ABRAXAS come? For whose benefit and for what purpose is it presented to be seen?*<-

<margin>Book 5 of the Ecclesiastical History, chapter 16.</margin>
<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'A' featuring floral motifs, leaves, and a central architectural element.</image-desc>The historian Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century Christian church historian</note> records that when the Emperor Theodosius was consuming the remnants of idolatry with the pen of his most sharp edicts, he rescued the more notable spoils from the Prince of Hell and wished them to be placed on public display. Theophilus, the Bishop of Alexandria, is said to have done this with particular distinction in that city. Indeed, from the countless statues of the gods which he had ordered to be melted down into tripods and other vessels, he preserved one single image—more foul than all the rest—which he fixed in a famous and open place for the memory of posterity. "Lest," he said, "it ever be permitted for the Pagans to deny that they once worshipped such gods." And Socrates writes that the Pagans were so displeased by this deed of his, that Ammonius the Grammarian <note>A pagan priest and scholar who fled Alexandria after the destruction of the Serapeum</note>, who served as Socrates' own teacher, used to say that the worshippers of the gods had been badly treated <note>original: "actum," referring to the catchword</note> by this one thing

# THE PROTEAN ABRAXAS

...it was done so that these monuments might be erected to their perpetual mockery and disgrace. These foul images of demons brought this benefit: that in the shipwreck of idolatry, they were like the prows <note>original: "rostra"</note> torn from enemy ships and hung up in the forum of the Church. Far from judging it a danger for these to be seen publicly, the wisest men instead thought it useful for those idols to be preserved and openly displayed, as their very appearance and condition would betray both the weakness and filthiness of their own kind, as well as the power and glory of Christ. This was elegantly shown in a long and copious dispute by Dionysius Petavius <note>A 17th-century Jesuit theologian known for his work on the history of doctrines.</note>. With the same intent, we now do the same; we are led to this by the subject of our writing, in which we present tablets deformed by monsters, and from these, a book very much like them: the sick dreams of Basilides <note>An early Gnostic teacher in Alexandria.</note>, the portentous Abraxas, a double-natured god, the vain phantoms of the heretics Valentinus and Marcus, the absurdities <note>original: "ἀλογίας" (alogias)</note> of the Egyptians, entangled deities, and *the abominable and impure herds of demons* <note>original: "τὰς βδελυράς τε, καὶ ἀκαθάρτους τῶν δαιμόνων ἀγέλας"</note>, Grylli <note>Grotesque figures where heads are joined directly to legs.</note>, the hobgoblins of the Empusae <note>Shapeshifting female demons from Greek mythology.</note>, and the various and ridiculous—though difficult—trifles of the Gnostics. These are, in short, the sorts of things Horace mocked in the painters and poets of his own age:

<margin>Preface to the Works of Julian.</margin>
<margin>Cyril of Alexandria on Micah, chapter 7.</margin>
<margin>Book on the Art of Poetry, Gems 1, 2, 3.</margin>

> *If a painter chose to join a horse's neck to a human head,*
> *and to spread feathers of many colors*
> *over limbs gathered from everywhere, so that what is*
> *a beautiful woman above ends foully in a black fish:*
> *could you, my friends, if let in to see it, keep from laughing?*
> *Believe me, Pisos, a book will be perfectly like*
> *that picture, whose idle fancies shall be shaped*
> *like a sick man's dreams, so that neither foot nor head*
> *can be assigned to a single shape.*

# THE PROTEAN ABRAXAS

...it was done so that these monuments might be erected to their perpetual mockery and disgrace. These foul images of demons brought this benefit: that in the shipwreck of idolatry, they were like the prows <note>original: "rostra," the decorative beak-like prows of captured enemy ships displayed as trophies in the Roman forum.</note> torn from enemy ships and hung up in the forum of the Church. Far from judging it a danger for these to be seen publicly, the wisest men instead thought it useful for those idols to be preserved and openly displayed, as their very appearance and condition would betray both the weakness and filthiness of their own kind, as well as the power and glory of Christ. This was elegantly shown in a long and copious dispute by Dionysius Petavius <note>A 17th-century Jesuit theologian and historian of dogma.</note>. With the same intent, we now do the same; we are led to this by the subject of our writing, in which we present tablets deformed by monsters, and from these, a book very much like them: the sick dreams of Basilides <note>An early 2nd-century Gnostic teacher in Alexandria who taught that the world was created by inferior angels.</note>, the portentous Abraxas, a double-natured god, the vain phantoms of the heretics Valentinus and Marcus, the absurdities <note>original: "ἀλογίας" (alogias)</note> of the Egyptians, entangled deities, and *the abominable and impure herds of demons* <note>original: "τὰς βδελυράς τε, καὶ ἀκαθάρτους τῶν δαιμόνων ἀγέλας"</note>, <term>Grylli<note>Grotesque hybrid figures common in ancient gems, often consisting of heads joined directly to legs without a torso.</note></term>, the hobgoblins of the Empusae <note>Shapeshifting female demons from Greek mythology, used here to characterize the frightening nature of the heretical images.</note>, and the various and ridiculous—though difficult—trifles of the Gnostics. # A B R A X A S

<margin>Gem 8</margin>
Macrobius graphically depicts a three-headed image of the Sun, and his description does not differ much from the image we represent here. <margin>Book 1 of the *Saturnalia*</margin> "In Egypt," he says, "the city adjacent <note>referring to Alexandria</note> which boasts Alexander the Great of Macedon as its founder, observes the worship of <term>SERAPIS</term> <note>A Greco-Egyptian deity</note> and <term>ISIS</term> with a veneration that is almost ecstatic: yet they testify that they bestow all that veneration upon the <term>SUN</term> under that name, whether when they fix a basket <note>original: *calathum*, a symbol of fertility and abundance</note> upon his head, or when they add to the statue the sign of a <term>THREE-HEADED CREATURE</term>. This expresses in the <term>MIDDLE</term>, and also the <term>LARGEST</term> head, the likeness of a <term>LION</term>. On the right side, the head of a <term>DOG</term> rises up with a gentle, fawning appearance. The left part of the neck, however, ends with the head of a ravenous <term>WOLF</term>. A <term>DRAGON</term> <note>or serpent</note> connects these animal forms with <term>ITS COILS</term>, its head returning to the god's right hand, by which the monster is beheld."

<margin>Gems 9, 10</margin>
Antiquity imagined those Giants, who fought against Jupiter, to have had serpents in place of feet,

<margin>Ovid, Book 4 of the *Tristia*, Elegy 6</margin>
> The Sphinx, and Harpies, and the <term>SNAKE-FOOTED</term> <term>GIANTS</term>.

<margin>M. Aurelius Severinus, *On the Viper*, chapter 1, section 1. Book 1 of the *Saturnalia*.</margin>
Not without a deeper meaning—so that they might mock the wretched insolence of those who insult the gods—they devised the most noble human form ending basely in snakes. Certainly, nothing more incongruous or indecent, nothing more absurd could be said of such a spectacle. Macrobius states: "What else is to be believed of the <term>GIANTS</term> than that they were a certain impious race of men, denying the gods, and therefore thought to have wished to drive the gods from their celestial seat? Their feet ended in <term>DRAGON COILS</term>; which signifies that they thought of nothing upright, nothing heavenly, with the step and progress of their whole life sinking into the lower regions. From this race, the <term>SUN</term> exacted the due punishments by the force of his pestilential heat." The Emperor Commodus emulated the Sun's power, though through a shameful victory,

# PROTEUS

<margin>Found in the writings of Lampridius and Zonaras.</margin>
The Emperor <note>referring to the Roman Emperor Commodus or Elagabalus, known for such cruel spectacles</note> fashioned those who were weak in their feet and unable to walk into the likeness of GIANTS, so that from their knees down, they were arranged with rags and linens AS IF THEY WERE DRAGONS <note>serpent-footed giants were common in mythology</note>, and he finished them off with arrows. Epiphanius, writing against the Gnostics:

<margin>Heresy 26.</margin>
*From the superstitious opinions of the Pagans, having shaped those trifles and fables to suit their own purpose, they mix a lie with the truth, so that by disguising things composed by the Pagans with BARBARIC VOICES <note>strange, foreign-sounding magical formulas</note>, they may more easily mock the men they have deceived.* From these, therefore, and similar images, the monsters of the Basilidians <note>a Gnostic sect founded by Basilides in the 2nd century</note> were mostly composed:

<margin>In the Explanation of the Isiac Table.</margin>
*From this root,* says Pignorius <note>Lorenzo Pignoria, an Italian antiquarian</note>, *sprouted the dreams of the magi, the fabrications of the Manichaeans, the deceptions of enchanters, and the ravings of the Gnostics and Marcionites, who asserted two first principles <note>the dualist belief in opposing gods of good and evil</note>.* But Irenaeus speaks eloquently regarding the Basilidians:

<margin>Book 1, Chapter 23.</margin>
*They use both IMAGES and INCANTATIONS, and all other sorts of trifles: also inventing certain NAMES.* Indeed, that famous decree of Basilides himself is handed down by the same Irenaeus: YOU SHOULD KNOW EVERYONE, BUT LET NO ONE KNOW YOU. Led by this as a principle of his Secrets, he so entangled all his affairs in riddles and enigmas that scholars are not even in agreement regarding the name of the Portentous Abraxas <note>a supreme mystical deity in Gnostic cosmology</note>. Joseph Scaliger <note>a famous Renaissance scholar</note> judged it to be Hebrew, where he mistakes a purely Basilidian Gem for an Arabic Talisman, and in his *Opuscula*:

<margin>In his Minor Works.</margin>
He says it is מגן דוד <note>original: "Maghen Dauid"</note> *Magen David*, or the *Shield of David*. Our most learned Wendelin composed the word ABRACAE from Hebrew and Greek, and elicited a meaning—a pious one indeed, but too pious, too Christian, and of a higher wisdom than could enter into those malevolent souls of the Gnostics. Therefore, in untying knots of this kind, I have decided to stick to the previously mentioned inquiry of Macarius, which rests on the authority of the Church Fathers; and so, for the sake of illustrating it...

# ABRAXAS

<margin>
Volume 2.
Year of
Christ
120, no. 17
</margin>
...to be shown are the multiform Basilidian Gems <note>Gems associated with Basilides, a 2nd-century Gnostic teacher in Alexandria</note>, for the purpose proposed by the most eminent writer of the Annals, Baronius <note>Cesare Baronius (1538–1607), a Cardinal and ecclesiastical historian</note>; namely, so that you, reader or observer, may clearly see from these *how vastly different the images of Basilides, which are condemned by Irenaeus, are from those which Catholic Christians were accustomed to use religiously and piously.* From this, you may have the calumny of more recent Iconoclasts <note>Those who destroy religious images; here referring to Protestant reformers who accused Catholics of idolatry</note>—*who object against us out of both ignorance and evil deceit*—fully examined and likewise refuted. Irenaeus himself, in the Preface to his books against the Heresies of Valentinus and similar groups who were the successors of the Basilidians, wrote: I have deemed it <term>NECESSARY</term> <note>Latin: necessarium</note>, *since I have read their own Commentaries, to manifest to you, most beloved,* the <term>MOST MONSTROUS MYSTERIES</term> <note>Latin: portentosissima mysteria</note> *of those people, which not everyone understands because not everyone has a brain; so that you, knowing these things, may make them manifest to all those who are with you, and command them to keep themselves from the depth of senselessness and of that blasphemy which is directed against God.*

I have inscribed these multiform gems of the Basilidians as <term>PROTEUS</term> <note>A Greek sea-god capable of changing his shape at will</note>,

<margin>
Ovid,
Book 8 of the
Metamorphoses
</margin>
> Or those who have the right to pass into <term>MANY</term> <term>FORMS</term>,
> *As you do, Proteus, inhabitant of the sea that embraces the earth.*
> *For at times they saw you as a* <term>YOUNG MAN</term>, *at times as a* <term>LION</term>:
> *Now a violent* <term>BOAR</term>, *now a* <term>SNAKE</term> *whom they would fear to touch*
> *You were: at times horns made you a* <term>BULL</term>:
> *Often you could appear as a* <term>STONE</term>, *and often as a* <term>TREE</term>.
> *Sometimes, imitating the appearance of flowing waters,*
> *You were a* <term>RIVER</term>; *sometimes* <term>FIRE</term>, *the opposite of the waves.*

Thus, for the Basilidians—that multiform race of heretics—I myself judge that Circe <note>The enchantress from the Odyssey who turned men into beasts</note> was their mother and Proteus their father; since they each made just as many gods out of themselves, or rather, they held animals as gods, and not just of one beast...

# PROTEUS

...those who bore the likeness of a beast upon their rings, who were like many beasts in their moral conduct.

<margin>Ephesians 5</margin>
The Apostle <note>Paul</note> warns, *It is a shame even to speak of those things which are done by them* (namely the Gnostics). Nor would I have sought out their amulets so diligently, had not Saint Epiphanius—and Baronius following him—not blushed to speak of the impure rites of Basilides <note>A 2nd-century Gnostic teacher in Alexandria</note>, however shameful they may be; through these, he showed that Basilides was a master of lust and of the most foul embraces. Consult Epiphanius himself, if you please, who, when about to enter this swamp of filthy dogma, says: <margin>Heresy 26</margin> *I fear lest by chance I reveal this great poison in its entirety, like the* FACE OF A BASILISK SERPENT <note>A mythical king of serpents said to kill with a glance</note>, *to the ruin of the readers rather than to their correction.* Yet he revealed it nonetheless, judging that the heads of the poppies could not be struck down unless they stood out; he gathered the tares <note>weeds</note> to cast them into the fire, with which the Prince of Darkness corrupted the first harvest of the Gospel.

Irenaeus <note>An early Church Father and heresy-hunter</note> explicitly describes Gnostic gems of this kind, whether of Valentinus or Basilides, when he says: *Just as if someone took a well-crafted image of a king, diligently made from* PRECIOUS GEMS *by a wise artisan, and dissolving the underlying figure of the man, should transfer those* GEMS, *and reforming them, should make from them the* FORM OF A DOG, *or of a* LITTLE FOX, *and that* POORLY ARRANGED; *and should then shape it and say that this is that good image of the king which the wise artisan made, pointing to the* GEMS *which were indeed well-composed by the first artisan into the king's image, but were badly transferred by the later one into the* FIGURE OF A DOG; *and through the* ILLUSION OF THE GEMS <note>original: "gemmarum phantasiam"</note> *he might deceive the simple-minded who have no understanding of the royal form, and persuade them that this* FOUL FIGURE OF A LITTLE FOX *is that good image of the king. In the same way, these men, adopting* OLD WIVES' TALES...

<summary>The author cites Church Fathers like Epiphanius and Irenaeus to condemn Gnostic sects, comparing their misuse of sacred truths to an artisan who breaks down a gemstone mosaic of a king to create the image of a dog or fox.</summary>
<keywords>Gnosticism, Basilides, Epiphanius, Irenaeus, amulets, gemstones, heresy, deception, symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion on the origins of the Basilidian sect; this page continues by examining their use of physical artifacts and secret rituals to justify their doctrines.</meta>

# ABRAXAS

Then, bringing forward speeches, sayings, and parables from here and there, they wish to adapt the oracles of God to **THEIR OWN FABLES**.

But why were their mysteries expressed in such various ways upon Gems? First, indeed, because they used them as Amulets, which Pliny <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, a Roman author and naturalist</note>—mocking them throughout his work—attributes to the vanity of the magi, because they brought forth so many claims about **GEMS**, having overstepped the bounds of Medicine into a kind of charmingly monstrous superstition. <margin>Pliny, Natural History, Book 37, Chapter 3</margin> Secondly, they did this so that they might imitate the Pagans, who were accustomed to wear upon the stones of their rings:

<margin>Prudentius, Crowns of Martyrdom, Hymn 10</margin>
> Fauns, Priapi, the Guardians of the pipes,
> Swimming Nymphs, and aquatic inhabitants
> Situated, in the manner of frogs, beneath the lake itself.

One can see Gems of this kind even today in the cabinets of Antiquarians in such great abundance that they almost equal the infinite quantity of ancient coins. Other heretics who followed Simon Magus <note>Often considered the first heretic, who attempted to buy spiritual power from the Apostles</note> had preceded the Basilidians even in this form of worship. <margin>Irenaeus, Against Heresies, Book 1, Chapter 20</margin> They possessed his **IMAGE** made in the likeness of JUPITER, and that of SELENE <note>Also known as Helen of Tyre, Simon's companion</note>—a most impure prostitute—in the likeness of MINERVA, and these they worshipped. From here also came the Carpocratian Gnostics: <margin>Ibid. Chapter 24</margin> they possessed certain **IMAGES**, some painted, and others fabricated from other materials, and they established the rituals of the Pagans toward them. They did this, however, in such a way that they commanded a singular silence from the Initiates <term>Mystis: those initiated into secret religious rites</term>, saying that Jesus spoke in mystery to his disciples and Apostles privately, and that he demanded they hand these things down separately to those who were worthy and consenting. Hence, committing their secrets to the privacy of the ears alone, they would cauterize <note>To brand or mark with heat</note> their disciples on the upper parts of the right **EAR**. Thus Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher and opponent of early Christianity</note> vehemently demands that no one announce to the common people the things he is about to say: <margin>Found in Eusebius, Preparation for the Gospel, Book 4, Chapter 8</margin> *“You,”* he says, *“take care above all else that you do not report these matters openly.”* And after a few things are interjected: *“See to it that you keep these secrets more secret and hidden than the mysteries themselves, for the reason that nothing...”*

<summary>This page discusses how Gnostic sects like the Basilidians and Carpocratians used engraved gems and images as amulets, imitating pagan customs and maintaining strict secrecy through physical branding and private initiations.</summary>
<keywords>Gnosticism, amulets, engraved gems, Simon Magus, religious secrecy, branding, pagan influence, Irenaeus, Pliny the Elder</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of divine secrecy and the "Protean" nature of ancient mysteries, specifically focusing on the deliberate obscurity of the Gnostics and magical practitioners.</meta>

# PROTEUS

The gods uttered nothing about themselves that was plain or clear, but spoke everything through their oracles in an obscure manner. The reason for this silence was that these rites were either shameful or cruel, such as the sacred rites of the Gnostics <note>A diverse group of early Christian-era religious movements that emphasized secret "gnosis" or knowledge.</note>, in which promiscuous intercourse and the killing of a human being in a wild and savage ritual were considered most religious acts, and the eating of the flesh even most beneficial for health. There follows <margin>Lorenzo Pignoria in his *Explanation of the Isiac Table* <note>A famous bronze tablet from antiquity depicting Egyptian motifs, much studied by Renaissance antiquarians.</note>.</margin> THE DUTY OF SILENCE; as Tertullian says against the Valentinians <note>Followers of Valentinus, a prominent Gnostic teacher.</note>, *that they are silent is a matter of SHAME. They care for nothing more than to hide what they preach; if indeed they can be said to preach, who hide their message.* He says again in the same book: *That which is hidden at such great cost is destroyed by the mere act of revealing it.*

In the inscriptions found on gems, there is for the most part a magical obscurity and an affected barbarism; nowhere else is there greater impunity for divinations or conjectures. These are, <margin>α In the *Life of Hilarion*.</margin> according to α Saint Jerome, *certain torments of words;* <margin>β Book 1 of the *Stromata*.</margin> according to β Clement of Alexandria, *Ephesian letters* <note>original: Ἐφέσια γράμματα; these were ancient Greek magical formulas or "power words" believed to have protective properties.</note>; <margin>γ Book 11 of the *Metamorphoses*.</margin> according to γ Apuleius, *unknowable letters and twisted flourishes;* <margin>δ In the *Necyomantia*.</margin> according to δ Lucian and Porphyry, *certain barbaric and meaningless names;* or the incantations of magicians, who are accustomed to using words used by no nation and signifying nothing. By this folly, they have persuaded others that those words are beneficial. Thus the Basilidians <note>Followers of the Gnostic teacher Basilides.</note>, attributing greater power to barbaric and misunderstood voices, corrupted even those that were known. For example, they wrote IMCHAL instead of MICHAEL, and AIO, OAI, IOA, in place of IAO <note>A Greek rendering of the Hebrew Divine Name.</note>: IEOUOH for JEHOVAH: <margin>ε Found in Eusebius, Book 4 of *Preparation for the Gospel*.</margin> <margin>ζ Rabbi Moses Maimonides on *Idolatry*.</margin> ζ MOADONAI for ADONAI <note>Hebrew for "Lord".</note>, and—a practice which also belongs to magicians η—by frequently uttering the same sounds; by repeating the same voices often, such as KRAMMA, KRAMMA; only thus did they think they would achieve anything. <margin>η Aristides, *On the Concord of the Rhodians*.</margin> θ Irenaeus says: *The rest, who are falsely called GNOSTICS, say that the Prophets made their prophecies from different gods. That the sayings were placed diversely in the writ—* <margin>θ Book 2, chapter 67.</margin>

<summary>This page examines the deliberate use of obscure language, "barbaric" names, and secret rituals by Gnostic sects and ancient magicians to maintain an air of mystery and power. It cites various Church Fathers and classical authors to argue that such secrecy often masked shameful practices or empty superstitions.</summary>

<keywords>Gnosticism, magic gems, secret names, Tertullian, Basilidians, divine names, incantations, religious secrecy, Isiac Table</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Gnostic amulets and the mystical names found upon them, specifically addressing the linguistic origins of the "Abraxas" gems and the difficulty of their interpretation.</meta>

# ABRAXAS

They set forth various names, such as **SABAOTH** <note>Hebrew: "Hosts," often used in the title "Lord of Hosts"</note>, and **ELOA** <note>Hebrew: "God"</note>, and **ADONAI** <note>Hebrew: "Lord"</note>, and whatever others are of this kind; laboring to show from these **DIVERSE POWERS** and **GODS**. Indeed, more recent magicians were so foolish that they did not hesitate to relate the word **AIO**—which is nothing other than **IAO** <note>A common Gnostic name for the Divine, derived from the Hebrew Yahweh</note>—to the cry of a Peacock.

<margin>Book 3, On Sculptures and the Virtues of Stones.</margin>
A parchment manuscript from the library of the Most Serene Queen **CHRISTINA** <note>Queen Christina of Sweden, a famous 17th-century patron of learning and collector of manuscripts</note> states: *If a Thaitis stone has the sculpture of a* **PEACOCK** *holding a sea-turtle fish, and beneath it, on the stone itself, it has the* **CRY OF THE PEACOCK**, *namely* **AIO**, *let the root of a trefoil be placed under it. For this is a great and admirable thing, worn for victory, peace, and stability.* For **IAO**, when shortened by a small 'o' <note>original: "per o paruum corripitur," referring to the Greek letter omicron</note>, *signifies him who gives* **FLIGHT FROM EVILS**. But Macarius did not wish to waste effort in interpreting such trifles, which Scaliger judged to be given in vain. Pignorius thought it better to confess ignorance than to be a source of loathing to the learned Reader. In these same Inscriptions and Gems, Baronius recognized that certain things remain utterly obscure, because not all the secrets of Basilides <note>An early Gnostic teacher in Alexandria</note> became known to the Catholics. Petauius calls them **PRODIGIES OF WORDS**, *in which it is worth neither the leisure nor the effort to struggle*. Who, after such great men, would think anything further should be sought in these, or dare to promise that he will explain what none of them understood? Salmasius contends that most inscriptions of this kind are Egyptian, and nearly all of those found written in Greek characters. *Because*, he says, *the Egyptians, after the rule of the Macedonians, adopted the Greek Alphabet in its entirety, to which they added seven letters from their own ancient script, retained so that they might express the sounds of their own dialect, for the pronouncing and writing of which the characters of the Greek letters were not sufficient.* The interpretation of these as if they were Hebrew has succeeded no better for learned men than the names of the Aeons <note>Divine emanations in Gnostic cosmology</note> of Valentinus.

<margin>Irenaeus, Book 2, Chapter 67.</margin>

<margin>On Epiphanius, Heresy 34.</margin>

<margin>Book on Climacteric Years.</margin>

<summary>This page examines the linguistic confusion surrounding Gnostic amulets, specifically debating whether mystical names like IAO are Hebrew, Egyptian, or even imitations of animal sounds like the peacock's cry. It cites several 17th-century scholars who concluded that many of these "magical" inscriptions are intentionally obscure or linguistically hybrid.</summary>

<keywords>Gnosticism, Abraxas gems, IAO, Sabaoth, Queen Christina of Sweden, Basilides, Egyptian linguistics, Greek alphabet, magical names, Salmasius</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's discussion on the origins of mystical names and the interpretation of ancient inscriptions, following the previous analysis of Hebrew and Egyptian etymologies.</meta>

# PROTEUS

derived from the same Hebrew source by force. For all those words are purely Egyptian. Regarding these, I later discovered that the Reverend Father Athanasius Kircher <note>A famous 17th-century Jesuit scholar and polymath known for his attempts to decipher Egyptian hieroglyphs.</note> brought some light to the subject in that work where, truly acting as a Christian Oedipus, he rendered the Egyptian Sphinx docile, making it forget its wildness. And indeed, it must be admitted—nor do I deny what I have learned at another's risk—that many meanings for these inscriptions can be imagined by someone strong in imagination and meditation. However, I do not consider it worthwhile to thrust such divinations upon the reader, as I have no wish to waste time so poorly, mindful of Tertullian’s warning regarding the Marcosians: <margin>Book on Prescriptions, chapter 5.</margin> *They calculate*, he says, *groups of eight and ten <note>original: "ogdoadas & decadas"</note>, so that to report all their vanities would be foolish and idle; yet it is not only vain now, but even dangerous.* For characters are sometimes heaped into a pile from which you cannot form even a single syllable; they are often numerical letters, which to count on one's fingers would be a task of almost insane leisure, or at the very least of troublesome subtlety—which, when tempered, is pleasing, but when immoderate, is offensive. These things are almost entirely undiscovered and inextricable, and should not be presented otherwise than they were presented by the Fathers themselves. But Latin writers (these are the words of Salmasius) <margin>Book on Climacteric Years.</margin> call ABRAXAS with a *fault* <note>meaning a corruption of the spelling</note>, as do *Tertullian and others*; but the Greeks call it ABRAXAX <note>original: ΑΒΡΑΣΑΞ</note>, and so it is read on the talismanic gems and rings of antiquarians. Both *Abraxas* and *Abraxax* consist of the same number of letters, which produce that number which a full year has: 365. However, *Abraxas* has a Greek ending, while *Abrafax* <note>Abraxax</note> has an Egyptian one. This name, which he thinks is not without fault, I use without harm alongside Macarius. For what evil is there if, with the most ancient writers, we prefer the Greek ending to the Egyptian? Especially where...

<summary>The author discusses the etymology of the mystical name Abraxas, citing Athanasius Kircher and Salmasius while cautioning against the "insane leisure" of over-interpreting numerical symbols and Gnostic vanities.</summary>
<keywords>Abraxas, Athanasius Kircher, Salmasius, Tertullian, Gnosticism, numerology, Egyptian etymology, talismans, 365</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's justification for his study of the Abraxas gems and Gnostic heresies, following the introduction of the term "Abraxas" and its numerical significance.</meta>

-># A B R A X A S<-

In both names <note>referring to "Abraxas" and "Mithras," which both total 365 in Greek numerals</note> there are just as many letters, and the same sum is established. I certainly would not wish to be faulted for this, nor if I explain those things which I shall borrow from the various readings of the Church Fathers in almost the same words with which they were narrated by them. Because <margin>Book 1 of the *Saturnalia*</margin> *the present work—as Macrobius says in a similar context—promises not a display of eloquence, but a collection of things worth knowing.* Of things worth knowing, I say; since the first step of wisdom is to have understood what is false; of which kind are those things represented here, decorated, so to speak, only with the lies of the sun. They say that a face is more becoming in which some mole <note>original: *næuus*, a birthmark or blemish</note> is present: this book of ours, as great as it is, is entirely a mole. And if perhaps to someone the diligence seems too light which I have applied in searching out these absurdities <note>original: *affanijs*, trifles or nonsense</note> and in making public the trifles of the heretics; I shall add here and there another light thing to a light argument, so that if by chance the individual parts have too little weight, when joined and gathered together they might acquire something to tip the scales. Although I do not hang ivy over this flat wine <note>original: *vappæ*; a reference to the custom of hanging ivy outside a tavern to show wine was for sale</note> to make it more salable; nor do I take such pleasure in my own merchandise that I do not warn again and again that the dogma of the Basilidians <note>Followers of the 2nd-century Gnostic teacher Basilides</note> is a treasury of evils, in which there are monsters instead of figures, discordant and unpolished voices instead of a procession of speeches, clandestine arts, and disciplines far removed from more refined literature. Regarding these matters, as error easily creeps in while discussing them, so one should not care for the glory which must be sought from more serious subjects. And even if those things were lacking, we are not permitted to be such accurate or eloquent writers, nor even to be occupied with the flowers of words, which the cry of duty calls away toward groans and prayers. Irenaeus <note>A 2nd-century bishop and theologian who wrote against Gnosticism</note>, when about to write against the Gnostics, said: *You will not require from us, who dwell among the Celts and are mostly called away into a barbarian tongue, the art of speech, which we have not learned; nor the power*

<summary>The author defends his unpolished writing style and his focus on "heretical trifles," arguing that exposing falsehood is the first step to wisdom. He cites Macrobius and Irenaeus to justify prioritizing factual collection over eloquent oratory.</summary>
<keywords>Abraxas, Basilidians, Gnosticism, Irenaeus, Macrobius, rhetoric, heresy, Church Fathers</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of his writing style and introduces the central thesis regarding the syncretic nature of the deity Abraxas.</meta>

# PROTEUS

of this composition, which we have not sought after; neither the ornament of words, nor the art of persuasion, which we do not know. If the Bishop of Lyon <note>Irenaeus (c. 130–202 AD), an early Church Father who wrote against heresy.</note>, that great defender of Ecclesiastical peace—and called by Tertullian <margin>Book against the Valentinians, chapter 5.</margin> the most curious explorer of all doctrines—demands that this indulgence be granted to him, then I, conscious of my own insignificance, should certainly expect the same by a better right, or rather, expect it freely from my readers. But now I prepare to introduce the various forms,

<margin>Prudentius, Crowns of Martyrdom, Hymn 10.</margin>
> *And if there is anywhere any* MYSTICAL VANITY,
> *of* LATIN GODS, *and* EGYPTIAN GODS,
> *To whom* ROME *offers sacrifice, to whom* CANOPUS <note>A city in ancient Egypt near Alexandria, used here to represent Egyptian religion.</note> *supplicates.*

When I have performed this, I will further show that it was not without cause that Macarius wrote that this ABRAXAS <note>A word of mystic significance in Gnosticism, often representing the Lord of the 365 heavens.</note> was like the Trojan horse, embracing the entire family of gods, whether they were Egyptian, Greek, Latin, or Persian.

<margin>Tertullian, Against Marcion, book 1, chapter 7.</margin>
> BASILIDES <note>An early Gnostic religious teacher in Alexandria.</note> *commanded us to believe there are as many* GODS
> *As the year has days, and just as many worlds.*

<summary>The author humbles himself by comparing his writing to the Church Father Irenaeus before arguing that the Gnostic deity Abraxas serves as a "Trojan horse" containing all ancient pagan pantheons.</summary>
<keywords>Abraxas, Gnosticism, Irenaeus of Lyon, Tertullian, Basilides, syncretism, Trojan horse, Egyptian mythology, Roman religion</keywords>

# CHAPTER I.

### TABLES I. II. III.

### ABRAXAS, Mithras, Helios, The Rooster-headed. <note>original: "Gallinaceus"; refers to the rooster-headed figure often found on Gnostic gems.</note>

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'F' featuring floral motifs and a potted plant.</image-desc>

IN VAIN were the **SUNS** sought by Basilides <note>A prominent Gnostic teacher in Alexandria during the 2nd century.</note>, so long as he refused to gaze upon the opposing rays of the rising Gospel,

<margin>Juvenal, Satire 15</margin>
> ——— *while he believes only those*
> *to be GODS, which he himself worships.*

He ought to be cast out from the nest with his chicks like a bastard and a degenerate. In vain might the owl boast itself a friend of the **SUN**, of which St. Ambrose says: <margin>Sermon 43.</margin> *That animal,* he says, *is a figure of heretics and of the Gentiles, who embrace the darkness of the devil, shudder at the light of the Savior, and with the large eyes of their disputations they discern vain things, but do not look upon eternal things.* Of these the Lord says: *They have eyes, and they shall not see; they walk in darkness.* For they are sharp-witted toward superstitious things, but dull toward the Divine; who, while they think to fly away on subtle discourses, are—like owls—thrown into confusion by the splendor of the true Light.

Among those seekers of shadows <note>original: "tenebriones"; literally "dwellers in darkness," used here as a derogatory term for heretics.</note> who, having undertaken a work of darkness, tore the seamless robe of Christ, and who (as Tertullian speaks) wished to be heretics derived from the Gospel, Simon Magus was the first of all to break forth. After him came Menander, from whom <margin>Book on Prescriptions, chapter 46.</margin> <margin>Eusebius, Book 4 of History, chapter 7.</margin> <margin>Nicephorus, Book 4, chapter 2.</margin> *a certain crafty and two-headed poison of error, like a serpent breaking forth as if from a pestilential spring, produced two authors and leaders of two sects differing between themselves: Saturnilus, an Antiochian by birth, and* **BASILIDES** *of Alexandria. The former of these [spread his errors] in Syria, the latter indeed in Egypt,*

<summary>This page introduces the Gnostic leader Basilides, framing his teachings as a rejection of the true Christian "Light" in favor of superstitious darkness. It traces the lineage of heresy from Simon Magus through Menander to the establishment of Gnostic sects in Syria and Egypt.</summary>
<keywords>Basilides, Abraxas, Gnosticism, heresy, Simon Magus, Menander, Saturnilus, Alexandria, early church history</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Gnostic sects and their symbolic imagery, specifically focusing on the origins of the Abraxas gems and the influence of Basilides.</meta>

# ON THE ABRAXAS GEMS

*...he established schools of error, which were repugnant to God and piety. Irenaeus <note>Saint Irenaeus (c. 130–202 AD), a bishop and theologian known for his writings against heresy.</note> declares that Saturnilus, for the most part, falsely devised the same things as Menander; but that BASILIDES, under the pretense of deeper secrets, stretched his efforts to an immense degree while he himself was inventing* MONSTROUS FABLES, *and those most full of impious error.*

His principal idol was ABRAXAS, who is the same as <margin>Book 1 of the Commentary on Amos. Gem 1 & 2. Book 1 of the Saturnalia. Gem 3, 4, & 5.</margin> MITHRAS or HELIOS <note>The Greek god of the Sun.</note>, according to the testimony of Saint Jerome; now fawning upon his sister the Moon, now winged and sitting upon a horse, on account of his swift and rapid movement through all things. *The Egyptians*, says Macrobius <note>A Roman provincial of the early 5th century, author of the Saturnalia.</note>, *fashion the images of the* SUN *itself as* WINGED: now just as he appears in the same Macrobius, and on the coins of Heliogabalus, *in a beardless form, with the right hand raised with a whip in the manner of a charioteer.* Prudentius <note>A Roman Christian poet (c. 348–413 AD).</note> writes:

> *They invented that this* STAR *drives a chariot and rapid teams,*
> *and they devised the* RAYS OF THE HEAD, and the LASHES *of the right hand.*

<margin>Pliny, Book 10 of Natural History, chapter 21.</margin> And just as the rooster commands his own kind and exercises kingship in whatever house he may be; since Basilides aspired to that glory, and as a corrupter of the Christian Religion—or rather a deserter—had crossed over to the rites of Mithras and the Sun, he also frequently used the symbols of those same rites on Gems: especially the rooster, the herald of the coming light, whom nature brought forth for the purpose of rousing mortals to work and breaking their sleep. Pausanias <note>A Greek traveler and geographer of the 2nd century AD.</note> in the first of his books on Elis: *They celebrate the* ROOSTER AS A BIRD SACRED TO THE SUN, *because it announces the return of the* SUN *with its song.* For indeed, it knows the stars; it goes to bed with the Sun, nor does it allow his rising to steal upon the unwary; it announces the coming day with song, and the song itself with the clapping of its wings; finally, it is the only bird that frequently looks up at the sky.

Furthermore, concerning *Iao* <note>original: "Iάω" - a Greek transcription of the Hebrew Tetragrammaton (YHWH), often used in Gnostic magic.</note>, a most frequent word on the Abraxas stones, the Gnostics...

<summary>This page discusses the Gnostic leader Basilides and his syncretic worship of Abraxas, identifying the figure with the sun deities Mithras and Helios. It explains the solar symbolism found on Gnostic gems, particularly the use of the rooster as a herald of light and divine authority.</summary>
<keywords>Basilides, Abraxas, Mithras, Helios, Gnosticism, solar symbolism, rooster, Iao, Gnostic gems, Macrobius, Irenaeus</keywords>

# COMMENTARY. CHAPTER I.

The Gnostics themselves, according to Epiphanius <note>A 4th-century Church Father known for his "Panarion," a refutation of various heresies.</note>, say: *In the highest sphere resides IAO.* <note>original: "ἰάω" — a Greek rendering of the Hebrew Tetragrammaton, the four-letter name of God.</note> And in the works of Macrobius, when the Clarian Apollo <note>An oracle of the god Apollo at Claros.</note> was consulted as to which of the gods should be considered the one called IAO, he spoke thus:

<margin>Book I of the Saturnalia.</margin>
> *Declare that IAO is the supreme god of all,*
> *In winter he is Hades, and Zeus at the beginning of spring,*
> *The Sun in summer, and in autumn the delicate (or festive) IAO.*
<note>The author provides both the original Greek verse and a Latin translation, here rendered into English. This passage attempts to synchronize the Hebrew God with the changing seasons and Greek deities.</note>

In reality, IAO is nothing other than the Tetragrammaton <term>יהוה; the four-letter Hebrew name of God, often vocalized as Yahweh</term>, from which also comes *Ieuo*, and *Jove* in the nominative case, naturally by adding a Latin termination. Eusebius <note>An early Christian historian and bishop.</note> says: *IAO is SALVATION, that is, the saving power of God.* <margin>Book 4 of the Preparation for the Gospel, chapter 29.</margin> But we see from these things how the priest of Apollo deceived those who consulted him, by leading them away from the true God of Israel, the author of nature, toward nature itself—the handiwork of God. It is as if the Jews (whose God is IAO, as Diodorus acknowledges) worshipped the Sun. <margin>Gerard John Vossius, Book 2 on Idolatry, chapter 14.</margin> We also recognize those whom we now, along with Tertullian, designate as heretics: they fashion the most vain and **FOUL FICTIONS** using **HOLY NAMES**, titles, and arguments of the true religion. <margin>Book against the Valentinians.</margin> These are tokens truly worthy of the pride of Basilides <note>An early Gnostic teacher in Alexandria.</note>, who used to say that he and his followers alone were men, and all others were pigs and dogs; and for that reason, his mysteries were not to be made public to others, because it was commanded: *Do not cast pearls before swine.* <margin>Epiphanius, Heresy 24.</margin> On the contrary, as Baronius <note>Cesare Baronius, a 16th-century Cardinal and historian.</note> wisely and prudently noted: *To put these things on display is nothing other than to raise up the most beautiful trophies of the Church, a victory obtained from the spoils of the enemy; and to proclaim the glory of God regarding the protection of the Church in every inscribed mark and fixed sign.* <margin>Year of Christ 120, number 17.</margin>

But, he whom Sirmondus <note>Jacques Sirmond, a Jesuit scholar and editor of ancient texts.</note> inscribed as THE PREDESTINED,

<summary>This page discusses the origins of the divine name 'IAO' as a Greek corruption of the Hebrew Tetragrammaton and critiques how pagan oracles and Gnostic heretics like Basilides misappropriated holy names for their own systems.</summary>
<keywords>IAO, Tetragrammaton, Gnosticism, Basilides, Apollo Clarius, Macrobius, divine names, Church Fathers, heresy</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of early Christian heresies, specifically focusing on the followers of Basilides and Carpocrates during the reign of Emperor Hadrian.</meta>

# ON THE ABRAXAS GEMS

An ancient author, speaking of the **Basilidians** <note>Followers of Basilides, a 2nd-century Gnostic teacher in Alexandria</note>, says: *The Church, regarding these men as if they were possessed by demons, condemned them not by arguing with them, but by casting them out.* This is indeed true; however, at that time—

<margin>
Book 4 of the History of the Church, chapter 2.
</margin>

—many men of the Church (these are the words of **Nicephorus** <note>Nicephorus Callistus Xanthopulus, a 14th-century Byzantine church historian</note>) confronted them with arguments drawn from right reason, upholding the Apostolic decrees. There were also those among them who left behind in their books preservative instructions against the snares of such heresies. Among these, **Castor Agrippa** published a Refutation of the insolence of **Basilides**. Exposing his deceptions and **Secret Mysteries**, Agrippa says that Basilides composed twenty-four books on the Gospel. He also claims that Basilides invented prophets named **Barcabbas** and **Barcoph**—who never existed in the nature of things—to strike terror into the hearts of barbarian men. Furthermore, he introduced a **Pythagorean Silence** <note>A vow of silence required of initiates, lasting several years</note> and taught that things should be held in no distinction: as if, when the time required it, one might precisely abjure the Faith without any exception and be permitted to taste things sacrificed to idols. Indeed, **Irenaeus** <note>Bishop of Lugdunum and early Christian apologist</note>, recounting similar things about **Basilides**, beautifully brought his shameful error into the open. He reports that **Carpocrates** was a contemporary of his, the author and leader of those who are called **Gnostics**. Carpocrates was not ashamed to openly practice the magic art, which his father **Simon** <note>Likely referring to Simon Magus</note> had professed secretly and craftily. He hunted for glory and exalted himself through "curious arts" <note>A common historical euphemism for magic or the occult</note> by means of love potions, which he prepared through demons who sent visions in dreams and acted as their assistants. He also taught that all those who wished to reach the perfection of their sacred **Secret Mysteries** must commit every kind of foulness; for it was impossible for anyone to escape those who were called the "Princes of the World" among them, unless he had satisfied them with every unspeakable filth. And indeed, these men are said to have existed under the Empire of **Hadrian**. Since he was a man of such immense ambition—

<margin>
Zonaras and Xiphilinus on Hadrian.
</margin>

<summary>This page details the early Church's rejection of the Basilidian and Carpocratian heresies, highlighting their use of invented prophets, magic arts, and secretive rituals during the reign of Emperor Hadrian.</summary>
<keywords>Basilidians, Castor Agrippa, Basilides, Gnosticism, Carpocrates, Simon Magus, magic arts, Emperor Hadrian, Church History, heresy</keywords>

# COMMENTARY. CHAPTER I.

In his desire to grasp at everything and professing to be ignorant of nothing, being most inquisitive even in these matters, he frequently made use of **DIVINATIONS** and **MAGIC RITES**. We represent here his silver ring with a rotating seal, from the *Cabinet of Rings* <note>original: "Dactyliotheca"</note> of Gorlaeus, which he undoubtedly wore as a protective charm <note>original: "alexitélon" (Greek: ἀλεξίτηλον), a ward against evil or poison</note>:

<image-desc>An engraving containing four circular/spherical illustrations within a single rectangular frame. One view shows the ring open/rotated to reveal interior engravings, while the other shows it closed.</image-desc>

<margin>In The Ship.</margin>
For on its side is seen the image of Mercury with his herald's staff <note>term: caduceus (the winged staff entwined with serpents, a symbol of the god Mercury)</note> and winged hat; concerning which Lucian <note>A 2nd-century Syrian-Greek satirist</note> writes: *I would wish for MERCURY to give me such RINGS of power, so that I might always be IN GOOD HEALTH of body and be INVULNERABLE; and another, that I might be INVISIBLE, of the same kind as the RING OF GYGES.* <note>The Ring of Gyges was a mythical artifact mentioned by Plato that granted its owner the power of invisibility.</note> Pliny...
