# ON THE ABRAXAS GEMS

<margin>Pliny, Natural History, Book 1, Chapter 7; Book 30, Chapter 2</margin>
Criticizing those rings fashioned by magical art, Pliny says: *They serve foreign rites, and* CARRY gods on their FINGERS, *and* WORSHIP MONSTERS *as well: they condemn and devise* GLOBES <note>original: "globos" - likely referring to magical spheres or celestial orbs used in divination</note>. Writing on the types of Magic, he also mentions that which promises divine revelations from SPHERES, the air, and the STARS; furthermore, he mentions conversations with shadows and the spirits of the underworld. He adds: *All of these things, in our age, the Emperor* NERO *found to be* EMPTY and FALSE: *for his lust for the lyre and tragic songs was no greater than his desire for the highest fortune of human affairs, while his soul wallowed in deep vices. First, he longed to command the gods, and he wished for nothing more noble. No one ever favored any of the arts more strongly. To this end, he lacked neither wealth, nor strength, nor the talent for learning, with the world enduring his whims. It is an immense and* UNDOUBTED *example of a* FALSE ART, *which* NERO *abandoned.* But leaving aside this monster of a man,

<margin>Salmasius, Book on Climacteric Years</margin>
let us return to the follower of Basilides <note>Basilides was an early Gnostic teacher in Alexandria who taught that Abraxas was the supreme deity</note>. Salmasius says: *This God of Basilides is depicted with the head of a crested poultry cock, holding a shield in one hand and a sword in the other, wearing a breastplate in the manner of a soldier, with serpentine feet and legs. His name was corrupted in two Talismans which were proposed to Scaliger* <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger, a famous 16th-century scholar</note> *to be explained; one from Varetus, the other from the son of Turquet, the most celebrated Physician. On that one was written:* ABRAXAM IAO AKLOSTH <note>original Greek: ΑΒΡΑΣΑΜ ΙΑΩ ΑΚΛΩCΘ</note>. *Where* ABRAXAM *was wrongly written for* ABRAXAS. *Scaliger mistakenly thinks the letters themselves were unique and the entire inscription was Hebrew. The name itself,* ABRAXAM, *which is corrupted, he thinks is the Hebrew* <term>Chavrat-Shem</term> <note>original Hebrew: חברתשם - "Company of the Name"</note>. *Which is a humorous hallucination. On another stone, which Pignorius presented in the Isiac Table, is written:* IAO ABRAZAZ. *It is the same name, namely* Iao Abraxas, *but* Z, *that is* M *turned over, was wrongly placed for* X <note>The author is correcting a transcription error where the Greek letter Xi (Ξ) was mistaken for a Zeta (Ζ) or an inverted Mu (Μ)</note>. *On the other stone of Varetus, the person who sent the* cast <note>original: "ἔκτυπον" (ektypon) - a molded copy or impression of the gem</note> *to Scaliger also described it incorrectly:* ABRASARE, ARIRIE, OITHAB, *and what follows.*

<summary>This page examines historical critiques of magic, citing Pliny the Elder's disdain for Nero's failed occult pursuits, and provides a scholarly analysis of transcription errors found on Gnostic "Abraxas" amulets.</summary>

# COMMENTARY. CHAPTER I.

It should have been **ABRACAE IAO** <note>original: "ABRACAE IAΩ"; IAO is a Greek vocalization of the Hebrew Tetragrammaton, the name of God</note>. The inscription itself is entirely Egyptian, not Jewish; thus that most eminent man <note>referring to the scholar Claude Saumaise, known as Salmasius</note> has tormented himself in vain by trying to extract a meaning as if from Hebrew words.

The same Salmasius does not approve of the stag horns occasionally placed on the head of the cock-headed Abraxas, which he claims are, in the original model, a crest or a feathery plume. I do not know if he is correct: for, so that the Basilidians <note>a Gnostic sect founded by Basilides in the 2nd century</note> might gather the symbols of the **SUN** into one, they seem to have sometimes given the crest—the most notable mark of the cock—even to the eagle; indeed, sometimes even branches like those of a stag, which is a most long-lived animal, and for that reason sacred to the **SUN**, the awakener of the principles of life.

Thetel, in the works of Camillus Leonardus <note>an Italian physician and astrologer, author of "The Mirror of Stones"</note>, promises wonders from this monster Abraxas engraved on a gem: *The image of a man*, he says, *on* **JASPER**, *having a shield in his left hand, and in his right an idol or something warlike: and in place of feet let there be vipers: and in place of a human head let there be the head of a* **COCK** *or a* **LION**, *and with a breastplate. This* **STONE** *thus* **ENGRAVED** *has power against enemies, and makes the wearer victorious, and is effective against poisons: it also binds blood, from whatever place it may flow.*

<margin>Book 3 on Stones and Gems, chapter 16.</margin>
Leonardus himself is of the same opinion: *Since*, he says, *there are diverse virtues in any stone, so also there are figures engraved on the stone from diverse parts of living creatures, which show the effect of the stone through similarity, as appears in my* **JASPER**, *in which there is a figure having the head of a* **COCK**, *the whole body down to the legs covered by a man's breastplate, holding a shield in the left hand, but in the right a whip, and two vipers fill the place of the legs and feet: which parts of the figure, if well considered, indicate the virtue of the stone.*

<margin>In the same book, chapter 6.</margin>
On the ninth Gem, the cock-headed Abraxas is also seen with a radiated head, as a symbol of the **SUN**, whom the Latins used to call the **RADIANT ONE**, sometimes without any further addition.

<margin>Gem 9</margin>
The inscription of Amulet 14: **TALA ARAIO OARLORON TOKON** <note>original Greek: "ΤΑΛΑ ΑΡΑΙΩ ΩΑΡΛΟΡΟΝ ΤΟΚΟΝ"; likely a magical formula or "voces magicae" intended for protection or childbirth</note>

<margin>Gem 14.</margin>

<meta>catchword: TOKON-</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the symbolic iconography of the Gnostic deity Abraxas, specifically the cock-headed figure with serpent legs, and explains the medicinal and protective virtues attributed to jasper gems bearing these images.</summary>
<keywords>Abraxas, Salmasius, Basilidians, Camillus Leonardus, Jasper, Cock, Lion, Sun, Amulet, Gnosticism, magical gems</keywords>

# ON THE ABRAXAS GEMS
<note>original: "IN GEMMAS ABRAXEAS"</note>

<margin>In the second part of Volume 2 of the *Egyptian Oedipus*, class 11, chapter 6. Baronius, Year of Christ 120, number 13.</margin>
Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher, a famous 17th-century Jesuit scholar</note> interprets TOKONBAI as *many-tongued* and *many-lettered*: TO THE HIGHEST MARS WHO CONQUERS ALL.

Because these heretical impostors were accustomed to using these characters and figures, arranged in various ways, to create amulets; you have an excellent example of this in Quintus Serenus Sammonicus, an ancient physician and, as it appears, a follower of that same Basilides <note>A Gnostic teacher in 2nd-century Alexandria</note>. He wrote medical precepts in heroic verse, and for curing a semitertian fever <note>a specific type of recurring malarial fever</note>, he directed an amulet to be made with these characters, to be hung around the neck of the sick person, containing **ABRACA**, joined with other elements that echo something portentous by their very sound.

<margin>The Cone.
ABRACADABRA
ABRACADABR
ABRACADAB
ABRACADA
ABRACAD
ABRACA
ABRAC
ABRA
ABR
AB
A.</margin>
> *Inscribe on paper that which is called* **ABRACADABRA**,
> *Repeat it often, and beneath it, but subtract the end,*
> *And more and more let single elements be missing from the figures,*
> *Which you shall always take away, and fix the rest,*
> *Until the lettering is reduced to a NARROW CONE:*
> *Remember to bind these with linen and wreathe them round the neck.*

And at the end:

> *Such bonds are beneficial for the neck of the languishing,*
> *And (wonderful power!) they shall drive away lethal diseases.*

Magicians devise monstrous words, and the works of Marcellus Empiricus and the "new Dioscorides" abound with such follies. Trallianus <note>Alexander of Tralles, a 6th-century physician</note> also places these among natural remedies, from which even Cato did not shrink; and Constantine Caesar has many things of this kind in the *Geoponica* <note>a collection of agricultural lore</note>, as does Pliny himself. To these I add Julius Africanus in his *Cesti*, in whose work there are also some notes on healing through **ABRACADABRA**. Indeed, it is a festive and ridiculous superstition that the disease and the remedy should end equally in an equal number of letters.

<margin>Robert Constantin in his Notes on Serenus.</margin>
Our distinguished colleague Godefroy Wendelin interpreted these same elements in this way:

It illustrates the "cone" or "triangle" method of writing the word to diminish the power of the disease.</summary>

# COMMENTARY. CHAPTER I.

->ABPA: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit <note>original Hebrew: "Ab, Ben, Ruach Ha-Kodesh"</note><-
->SAD: Salvation from Glory <note>original Greek: "Sōtēria Apo Doxēs"</note><-
->ABPA: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit<-

It is as if a prayer to the most holy Trinity were conceived in this manner: *O Trinity, FATHER, SON, and HOLY SPIRIT! SALVATION proceeds from the GLORY OF THE TRINITY.* This was indeed acutely devised by a most learned man and with an ingenious sense; however, many other similar interpretations could be found (especially when individual letters are adopted), none of which would truly reveal the mind of these men <note>referring to the ancient Gnostics</note>. Clearly these things originated from the Magi, whom heretics once followed in many matters. Basilides <note>a 2nd-century Gnostic teacher in Alexandria</note> did not avert evils with these gods—however much he believed them to be "averters of misfortune"—but rather stirred them up, unless a better God had preferred better things and averted this madness of his. Of the same inventive sort is the interpretation by Joseph Scaliger <note>a famous 16th-century French religious scholar</note>, previously criticized by Salmasio, when he rendered the Egyptian inscription of a Basilidian Gem into Hebrew as follows: ABPA. CAP. H. APIPI. H. OITHAB. <margin>*In the French Letters, which survive at the end of his minor works.*</margin> AAN. ATHA. NA. A. B. A. which means: *Drive away, O Prince of God, those cursed by God. And do good to us, You, I beseech You; forever You are blessed.* Likewise, he renders another: ABPA. CAM. IAO. L. K. L. O. S. TH. into Hebrew as: *Drive away, O Name of God, all the oppressors who afflict us, and the haters of our good—that is, those who envy our well-being.*

In this way, our friend Macarius also arranged the numerical letters of ABRAXAS with those found in the word MITHRAS <note>the Persian sun god</note>, though with little clarity and less success. Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher, a famous Jesuit polymath</note> is far more direct, provided that *i* is changed to *e*. He says: *ABRAXAS, through an arithmetic resolution made according to <term>isopsēphia</term> <note>the practice of adding up the number values of letters in a word to find a sum</note>, is the same as MITHRAS, whom they called the Solar Soul.* <margin>*In the second part of volume 2 of the Oedipus, class II, chapter 6.*</margin> vir- <note>catchword for the next page: "virtue" or "man"</note>

<summary>This page critiques various scholarly attempts to decode the mystical name Abraxas using Hebrew acronyms and Greek numerology, specifically citing the theories of Scaliger and Kircher. The author argues that while these interpretations are ingenious, they likely reflect the "madness" of ancient Gnostics rather than a clear theological truth.</summary>

# CHAPTER II.

### TABLE IV.

->**Abraxas** with the head of a hawk <note>original: "Ἱερακέφαλος"</note>, the *Ibis*, *Niphus*, or **Cnephus** the good spirit <note>original: "ἀγαθὸς δαίμων"</note>, and the "odargazas" <note>A magical name or formula often found on Gnostic gems</note>.<-

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'P' featuring a central pillar or pedestal flanked by two figures, possibly cherubs or mythological beings, with floral and scrollwork ornamentation.</image-desc>

The followers of Basilides <note>A 2nd-century Gnostic teacher in Alexandria</note> believed it consistent with their teachings to attach the head of a hawk to the usual monster, Abraxas. This head is also associated with Mithras <note>The Persian sun god</note>, even though the hawk is said to wage an internal war with the eagle, and they are often found locked in combat. Horapollo <note>An author of a treatise on Egyptian hieroglyphs</note> says of the hawk:

<margin>Book 1 of the Hieroglyphics.</margin>
> It appears to be the IMAGE OF THE SUN more than any other bird, seeing as it looks into the sun's rays with its eyes most intently by some peculiar and hidden power of nature. And from this it follows that Physicians use the herb "Hawk-weed" <note>original: "Hieraceo"</note> to heal defects of the eyes. From this it also follows that they sometimes paint the SUN in the form of a HAWK, as the guardian and master of sight.

<margin>Book 3 of the Preparation for the Gospel, chapter 4.</margin>
Porphyrius <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher</note> as quoted in Eusebius: *Through a singular and extraordinary power of wisdom, as well as a long-standing intimacy with the divine Spirit, they also understood this: that there are certain GODS who take more delight in specific animals than in men themselves, such as the SUN in the HAWK. Since the hawk has a nature consisting entirely of blood and spirit, it feels such compassion toward man that it groans over a fallen corpse and casts earth upon its eyes.* Hence in Egypt, there were CURATORS OF THE SACRED HAWKS, just as there were nourishers of the other sacred animals, whose duties you may read about in Diodorus <note>Diodorus Siculus, a Greek historian</note>.

<margin>Book 2, chapter 4.</margin>
To this belongs the station of HIERACON <note>The "City of Hawks" in Egypt</note> mentioned in the writings of Antoninus, which received its name just as other...

...many others are derived from sacred animals, as Pignorius and others have observed. No more on this for now, since we shall speak further below regarding the names of the Angels inscribed on the back of the gem in the section on the Phaëton Abraxas <note>A reference to a specific type of amulet depicting the sun-god or a celestial charioteer</note>.

<margin>GEMMA 16. In the second part of volume 2 of the *Oedipus Aegyptiacus*, class 11, chapter 6.</margin>
Kircher interprets the inscription of the following small ring as follows, and describes the diagram on the reverse: **ΙΟΥ ΙΟΥ ΩΙΟΥΝΥ ΥΟΟΝΩ ΩΝΙΟΥ**, *that is*, THE ETERNAL STRENGTH OF THE LORD <note>original Latin: DOMINI FORTITVDO ÆTERNA</note>. And beneath the feet is the name of the Angel **ΙΑΩΛΗΛ** *Iaoliël*, *that is* ANGEL OF THE LORD. On the back face, an IBIS is depicted in a flying pose, with a serpent entangled in its spreading feet, beside which stands ANUBIS with a sistrum <note>A musical percussion instrument used in ancient Egyptian religious ceremonies</note>. These denote nothing other than the MERCURIAL POWER, which drives away all harmful things. Pliny says: *The Egyptians also invoke* THEIR IBISES *against the arrival of* SERPENTS. And Cicero teaches that they were consecrated by the Barbarians on account of this benefit, where he says: *The Egyptians themselves, who are mocked, consecrated no beast except for some utility which they might derive from it. For instance, the* IBISES *destroy a vast force of* SERPENTS. *As do the Storks, to whom such great honor is given for the* DESTRUCTION OF SERPENTS *that in Thessaly it was a capital crime to have killed one, and the laws provided the same punishment as for a murderer.* But Juvenal speaks aptly, as if concerning the final Gem of this plate:

# COMMENTARY. CHAPTER II.

<margin>Satire 15</margin>
And thus he reports in that same place, both Cnephus, or the "Good Spirit" <note>original: Ἀγαθοδαίμονα (Agathodaemon)</note> in the form of an Ibis, the form of a serpent, and with the head of a hawk. For even Juvenal in his satires mentions the Ibis as an enemy to serpents; and Sanchuniatho, in the writings of Eusebius, speaks most clearly concerning the serpent CNEPH: <margin>Book 1 of the Preparation for the Gospel, chapter 7.</margin> *Nor is such an animal [the serpent] extinguished by a natural death, unless it has first been struck by some force.* And indeed, the PHOENICIANS called it the GOOD SPIRIT, while the Egyptians similarly named it CNEPH, and they added to it the HEAD OF A HAWK, on account of a certain extraordinary active power of this bird. Furthermore, that famous Epeis—who is named by them as the highest interpreter and scribe of sacred matters, and whose works Arius of Heracleopolis translated into the Greek language—explained this matter allegorically, word for word, as follows: *The most DIVINE of all was that SERPENT which bore the form of a HAWK, and was most pleasant to behold: for indeed, whenever it opened its eyes, it immediately filled all the places of its original region with LIGHT; but if it closed them, darkness followed at once.* And Epeis clearly declared that it was FIREY by nature through the very force of the word "diaugasei," that is, IT ILLUMINATED: *for "to illuminate" <note>original: τὸ διαύγασει</note> is a property of light.* Hence, on a gemstone, you may see the Sun communicating its rays, in a manner of speaking, to the Good Spirit: for the hawk is sacred to it, and is its symbol, as we have already shown. <margin>Book on Isis and Osiris.</margin> In Plutarch: *The inhabitants of the Thebaid <note>A region in ancient Upper Egypt</note> believe in no mortal god, but only in him whom they call the god CNEPH, who has no origin and is not mortal.* At which passage Gerard John Vossius <note>A famous 17th-century Dutch scholar</note> advises that we should read "Cneph the unbegotten" <note>original: Κνήφ ἀγέννητον</note>, and not, as is wrongly found in the <margin>Book 1 on Idolatry, chapter 2. In Eusebius, book 3 of the Preparation, chapter 12.</margin> Aldine, Stephanic, and the latest Parisian editions, "Cnephagonneton." Porphyry teaches that another image of this god depicted him pouring an egg out of his mouth, to signify that he was the maker of the world, or the <term>Demiurge</term> <note>A Greek term for a craftsman or creator of the universe</note>. And perhaps of such a kind was the Mithraic image—

# CHAPTER III.
## PLATE V.
### ABRAXAS The Raven <note>original: "Κόραξ" (Korax)</note>.

Some time ago in Rome, from the printing house of Lafreri, there appeared an image of a clay tablet. It was set into a wall in the house formerly known as the house of Octavius Zenus, near the Theater of Pompey and the Campo de' Fiori. It has been published again and is circulated within a collection of Hieroglyphics by an anonymous author. In both instances, it is presented under the guise of an agricultural scene, but incorrectly so, as Macarius <note>Jean L'Heureux, a 16th-century scholar of Christian archaeology</note> notes; for the image is actually of Mithras <note>The central deity of a Roman mystery religion</note> slaying a struggling bull. Even if other evidence were lacking, the raven appearing as if hovering over the bull's neck shows that this is indeed Mithras, or Apollo. For Aelian says: <margin>In the Supplement to Pierius.</margin> <margin>Book 1, chapter 48.</margin> **THE RAVEN**, he says, **IS SACRED TO APOLLO, and his servant;** and therefore *it possesses the power* **OF DIVINATION.** Pliny states: <margin>Book 10, chapter 12.</margin> *Among the omens, RAVENS alone seem to have an understanding of their own significations. For when the guests of Media were slain, they all flew away from the Peloponnese and the region of Attica. Their worst omen is when they swallow their voice as if they were being strangled.* Clement of Alexandria, quoted in Eusebius, says regarding such ravens delivering oracles: <margin>Book 1 of the Preparation for the Gospel, chapter 3.</margin> *Of the same sort of enchantment, as if partners in the trade, let the goats trained for divination be considered, as well as those* **RAVENS** *which men have instructed to deliver responses.* Hence the **CORACINE** <note>Raven-related</note> rites mentioned in the Commentaries of Hilary the Deacon, which are attributed to Saint Ambrose; on which passage Gerard Johannes Vossius remarks: <margin>On chapter 1 of the Epistle to the Romans. Book 3 on Idolatry, chapter 100.</margin> *I believe, he says, these rites were near Coptos, a city of such great name that from it Egypt derives its appellation. Around it...*

<summary>This page discusses the symbolic role of the raven in the cult of Mithras and its association with Apollo and divination, correcting a previous misidentification of a Roman relief as an agricultural scene.</summary>
<keywords>Abraxas, Raven, Mithras, Apollo, Divination, Roman Archaeology, Coptos, Symbolism</keywords>

# ON THE ABRAXAS GEMS

In that city, only two RAVENS were ever observed; for the Roman soldiers, who guarded the emerald mines in those mountainous regions, never saw more than that. And in that same place, there was a statue of APOLLO, to whom RAVENS are said to be sacred. But *I do not see how it follows from this* that those RAVENS were themselves GODS, rather than a symbol of the DIVINITY. For the sacred rites could have been called *korakina* <note>original: "κοράκινα"</note> or RAVEN-LIKE after the symbol. These are the author's words, to which I would easily subscribe, were it not for the authority of Saint Jerome, who counts the RAVEN among the MONSTROUS IDOLS, which Prudentius <note>A Roman Christian poet (c. 348–413 AD).</note> likewise calls IDOLS OF THE GODS. And ancient inscriptions teach us that the RAVEN RITES were a part of the MITHRAIC MYSTERIES <note>The cult of Mithras, a mystery religion popular among Roman soldiers.</note>, just as there were Lion, Persian, and Solar rites named after the Lion, the Persian, and the Sun. In these inscriptions, Mithras is depicted in exactly the same attire as in the Zeno tablet: with a flowing cloak, tucked up, wearing short trousers and sleeves, with a curved tiara, looking back at a RAVEN, and inscribed: *To the Sun Mithras the Unconquered* <note>original: "Ἡλίῳ Μίθρᾳ ἀνικήτῳ"</note>. Hence Ausonius says of the raven:

<margin>
In Gruter, page 1087.
In Bulenger, book 1 on Magic, chapter 4.
Idyll 11.
</margin>

> *The bronze-footed stags outlive Nestor three times over,*
> *But the PROPHETIC BIRD OF APOLLO <note>The raven.</note> surpasses even three of their lifetimes.*

<margin>
Book 3, Ode 27.
</margin>

Thence comes the prayer of Horace to Apollo, who is the rising Sun:
> I shall summon the PROPHETIC RAVEN with prayer
> from the rising of the SUN.

<margin>
GEM 22.
GEMS 19, 20, & 23.
</margin>

The star sometimes placed next to the Basilidian Raven <note>Basilidians were a Gnostic sect founded by Basilides in the 2nd century.</note> indicates this very thing, as does the laurel branch also dedicated to Apollo. These trifles and Gentile superstitions were surely used even in the symbols of those wicked disciples, the Basilidians, who came from a wicked teacher: "bad RAVENS from bad EGGS" <note>A play on the Latin proverb "bad crow, bad egg" (mala corvus, mala ova), meaning a bad father produces a bad son.</note>.

# CHAPTER IV.

### TABLE VI.

#### ABRAXAS Phaëthon, Phanes, Priapus, Protogenus, the Star of Venus.

<margin>Book 2 on Idolatry, chapter 7.</margin>
Gerard John Vossius <note>A celebrated 17th-century Dutch scholar of classical mythology and theology.</note> proves with many arguments that Priapus was naturally regarded as the SUN. First, he draws from the Hymns of Orpheus—or rather those of Onomacritus <note>An ancient Greek poet to whom many Orphic hymns were attributed.</note>—where he celebrates the "First-Born" <note>original: "Protogenon"</note> and calls him: *The Great One wandering in the upper air*; likewise: *The All-shining Branch*; and shortly after adds concerning the same:

-> ——— through the world <-
-> Leading the pure, SHINING LIGHT, from which I call you PHANES, <-
-> And PRIAPUS the KING. ——— <-

Later he is called *having diverse seeds*, or *rich in seed*. And what is more manifest from these things, says Vossius, than that PRIAPUS is the primordial LIGHT OF THE SUN: which is also called Phanes, because nothing is more *phanon*, or BRIGHT, than the SUN. And indeed, you may read in Macrobius <margin>Book 1 of the Saturnalia.</margin> that Phanes is the same as the SUN, where he says he was also called *Phanaeus* <note>The "Shining One."</note> for that reason. In the writings of Suidas, Phaëthon is the Sun, and Phanes is introduced as the Presiding Power of the generative faculty.

Macarius <note>Likely referring to Jean L'Heureux, known as Macarius, an author on ancient gems.</note> describes this god of the Basilidians <note>A Gnostic sect founded by Basilides in the 2nd century.</note>, and says that he represents him as he saw him, just as we might lead forth a captive from the impure and abominable herd of demons:

<summary>This page explores the syncretism of the deity Priapus with solar figures like Phanes and Phaëthon, arguing through classical and Orphic sources that these figures represent the primordial light of the sun. It connects these pagan concepts to the "Abraxas" gems used by the Basilidian Gnostics.</summary>

# ON THE ABRAXAS GEMS

<margin>Gems 24 &amp; 25</margin>
I believe, he says, that a certain wondrous monster belongs to the category of ABRAXAS <note>Abraxas was a deity in the Gnostic system, often depicted on amulets as a figure with a rooster's head and snakes for legs</note>; it is a naked human figure with four wings, carrying a crab in its right hand. The head is human, yet horrific. Beneath its feet is the word *Iao* <note>original: "Iαω" - a Greek rendering of the Hebrew Divine Name, Yahweh, frequently used in magical papyri</note>. For this reason, a similar monster with only two wings also belongs to this class; it has a phallus attached, and beneath its feet various small animals are depicted within a short space. I would gladly give this monster the name of Phaethon or Protogonus <note>Protogonus means "First-Born," an early creator deity in Orphic mystery traditions</note>, especially the one to whom Orpheus addressed a hymn. The wings are perhaps to be referred to the seasons of the year which fly past; in the primary division there are two, Summer and Winter; in the secondary division there are four: Spring, Summer, Autumn, and Winter. The phallus is added to denote generation. For God and the Sun, as the Philosopher <note>likely referring to Aristotle</note> testifies, generate all things; and those small animals are added to indicate the things produced by the Sun. From the inverted and scattered letters on the penultimate gem of this plate, Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a famous Jesuit scholar and polymath</note> gathers the word: *MNESTHEA* <note>original: "ΜΝΗCΤΗΑ"</note>, which signifies the same as REMEMBER; as if those wearing the amulet were asking the Deity to be mindful of what they requested.

<margin>Gem 26</margin>
<margin>In the second part of volume 2 of the *Egyptian Oedipus*, class 11, chapter 6.</margin>
On the final gem, where Phanes is shown in a portly form with other small animals surrounding their deity, there is also placed the *Star of Venus*, wandering in its alternating path, and rivaling the Sun and Moon in its very nicknames. Of this star, Pliny says: *It is greater in size than all other stars: indeed, of such brightness that shadows are cast by the rays of this one STAR alone. Therefore, it has a great range of names. For some have called it the star of Juno, others of Isis, others of the Mother of the Gods. By its nature* ALL THINGS ARE GENERATED *on earth: for at either of its risings, sprinkling a* GENITAL DEW, *it not only fills the conceptions of the earth, but also stimulates those of* ALL LIVING CREATURES.

<margin>Book 2, chapter 8.</margin>
Concerning the heresy of the Basilidians <note>A Gnostic sect founded by Basilides in the 2nd century AD</note> regarding the Angels, in addition to Tertullian (praised by Macarius), Irenaeus says: *The names also wha—* <note>The text cuts off here due to the page break</note>

# COMMENTARY. CHAPTER IV.

They strive to explain that some are in the first heaven, others in the second, and so on through three hundred and sixty-five fabricated heavens, providing their names, origins, **ANGELS**, and Powers. Saturnilus, <margin>Heresy 23.</margin> a fellow student of Basilides <note>Basilides was an early Gnostic teacher in Alexandria who taught a complex cosmology of 365 heavens.</note>, used to say, according to Epiphanius: that the **ANGELS** revolted from a higher Power; and that **SEVEN** of them created the world and the things contained within it, and that each **ANGEL** of that world was allotted a part by division. Hence, in the names of the Angels themselves, and in that inscription <margin>GEM 15. &amp; 24.</margin> which I provided above from the reverse side of the hawk-headed Abraxas <note>Abraxas is a Gnostic solar deity often depicted with a rooster's head and snake legs.</note>, I see that the number seven can be observed. By this, the Basilidians understood the supreme God as containing seven Angels, the Presidents of the seven heavens, from whom three hundred and sixty-five others proceeded, corresponding to the days of the year. Furthermore, the Magi called them *bird-shaped Angels of birds* <note>original: "Ἀγγέλους ὀρνίθων ὀρνιθομόρφους"</note>; nor did they pronounce the names of the Angels while slaughtering birds, lest they might by chance violate a hidden demon. In the book of Botrys, King of the Persians, cited in Bulenger: <margin>Book 2 on Magic, chapter 60.</margin> *I killed a vulture with a sharp reed; he who kills it shall be alone, nor shall he be seen by anyone, provided that he speaks the names of* **THREE ANGELS**.

Furthermore, they believed they could perform wonders with these angelic names, which were often foreign and unusual. In Arnobius: <margin>Book 1 against the Pagans.</margin> *He will perhaps encounter again, along with many others, those slanderous and childish claims: he was a magician, he accomplished all those things by secret arts; he stole the* **NAMES OF POWERFUL ANGELS** *and remote teachings from the inner shrines of the Egyptians.* Later, in the Roman Council, it was decreed by Pope Zachary <margin>Year of Christ 745.</margin> that no other **NAMES** of Angels should be recited except those of **MICHAEL**, **GABRIEL**, and **RAPHAEL**. # ON THE ABRAXAS GEMS

<margin>Chapter 16.</margin> In the Capitularies of Aachen <note>A set of legal and religious decrees issued by Charlemagne in 789 AD</note> it is stated: *It is commanded that* UNKNOWN NAMES OF ANGELS *should neither be invented nor named, except for those whom we hold in authority: these are* MICHAEL, GABRIEL, and RAPHAEL. Regarding these unknown names of angels and their invocations for magical incantations and superstitious uses, <margin>Book 2, chapter 58.</margin> the words of Irenaeus <note>A 2nd-century bishop and theologian who wrote against Gnosticism</note> are to be understood: *Neither should one do anything by* ANGELIC *invocations, nor by incantations, nor by any wicked* <margin>Book 10 of the Confessions, chapter 42.</margin> *curiosity.* Likewise, the words of Augustine: *Should I have gone to the* ANGELS? *By what prayer? By what Sacraments? Many attempting to return to you [God], and not being strong enough through themselves, as I hear, have tried these things, and have fallen into a desire for* CURIOUS VISIONS. For if the Magi <note>Practitioners of magic or sorcery</note> invoke evil spirits, and sometimes mix the names of good spirits into their wicked deeds—indeed, even the God of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, as is found in Origen <margin>Book 1 against Celsus.</margin>—then all the more usefully do true Christians implore the help of the good Angels against the race of demons, who are corrupt by their own will and condemned by God along with their prince, Satan.

# CHAPTER V.

## TABLES VII. VIII.

### ABRAXAS Lion-formed, Lion-headed. <note>original: "Λεοντόμορφος, Λεοντοκέφαλος"</note>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'G' featuring floral motifs, vines, and a small potted plant within the letter's frame.</image-desc>

Philostratus <note>A Greek scholar of the Roman imperial period</note> is the authority for the claim that the Griffin is dedicated to the SUN by the Indians; it is indeed a fictional animal, but frequent enough in Roman Hieroglyphics and depicted in various ways, though usually composed of an EAGLE and a LION.

<margin>Pighius, book on the parts of the year.</margin>
So great is the harmony of both animals with the SUN, and so fiery is their nature, that the feathers of the former <note>the eagle</note>, when mixed with the plumage of other birds, consume them with their heat; while from the bones of the latter <note>the lion</note>, when struck together, fire is ignited, as Aelian writes.

<margin>Pliny, book 10, chapter 3.</margin>
Hence ABRAXAS MITHRAS <note>Mithras was a Persian sun god popular in the Roman Empire; here linked to the Gnostic Abraxas</note> is shown not only with an Eagle's head, but also as Lion-formed and Lion-headed.

<margin>In the Octavius.</margin>
Minutius Felix <note>An early Christian apologist</note> says: *You dedicate GODS with the faces of LIONS and dogs.*

<margin>Apologetic, chapter 16.</margin>
According to Tertullian, they are TWO-FORMED DEITIES, mixed with canine and LION-like heads.

<margin>Book 6, Against the Heathens.</margin>
According to Arnobius, they are GODS with MOST GRIM faces. Concerning these, Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher</note> writes: *You may see among them certain IMAGES, human in appearance as far as the neck, but in the face representing now a bird, now a LION, or some other animal; and others again similar to humans in the head, but in the remaining parts of the body, now the lower, now the upper, showing the form of other living creatures. By these they wish to signify that it is by the will and counsel of the gods themselves that these creatures live among one another, and it does not happen without their divine power that wild and monstrous beasts are not only tamed, but even joined with us in some fellowship of life.*

<margin>Eusebius, book 3 on the Preparation [for the Gospel], chapter 4.</margin>
Therefore, the LION is worshipped by them as a GOD; and a certain part of Egypt (they call it a *Prefecture* <note>A Roman administrative district</note>) is named LEONTOPOLITES <note>City of Lions</note> after it; another...

# ON THE ABRAXAS GEMS

<margin>Horapollo, *Hieroglyphics*, Book 1, Chapter 17.</margin>
*The district of Busiris is named after the ox, and another, Cynopolis, after the dog.* <note>The author is comparing how Egyptian regions were named after sacred animals.</note> Horapollo says regarding the Lion: *This animal has a large head and* FIERY *pupils, a round face, and hair scattered all around in the manner of* RAYS, *resembling the* SUN. *Whence they place* LIONS *beneath the throne of* HORUS <note>Horus was the Egyptian god of the sky and sun.</note>, *that is, the* SUN, *demonstrating the greatest similarity of this living creature with the* GOD, *inasmuch as he is the* SUN.

<margin>Macrobius, *Saturnalia*, Book 1.</margin>
To this, the Egyptians consecrated the animal in the ZODIAC, in that part of the heaven where the SUN, in its annual course, seethes most greatly with powerful heat; and they call the sign of the LION therein the DOMICILE OF THE SUN, because that animal is seen to derive its substance from the nature of the SUN. First, the animal excels in impulse and heat, just as the SUN excels the stars; and the LION is strong in its chest and the forward part of its body, but diminishes in its hind limbs. Equally, the power of the SUN increases in the first part toward midday, or in the first part of the year, from spring into summer; soon, languishing, it is drawn down either toward the Sunset, which is the lower part of the day, or toward winter, which is seen as the lower part of the year. Likewise, the lion is always seen with open and fiery eyes, just as the SUN beholds the earth with an open and fiery eye in a perpetual and indefatigable gaze.

<margin>GEM 29. Year of Christ 120, number 13.</margin>
Furthermore, because on the reverse of Gem 29 there is an ABRAXAS in human form <note>original: "ἀντεϱπόμοϱφ<unclear>ος</unclear>" (anthropomorphos)</note>, inscribed all the way to the soles of the feet with portentous names; from this, says Baronius <note>Cesare Baronio, a 16th-century Cardinal and historian.</note>, a more evident argument is deduced: that this is the same genus of images which the followers of Basilides <note>A 2nd-century Gnostic teacher in Alexandria.</note> were accustomed to fashion. For Epiphanius <note>A Church Father who wrote against heresies.</note> says this concerning the same Basilides and the number of the name ABRASAX, according to his opinion: *Thence, he says, man also has three hundred and sixty-five members, and to each of the Virtues* <note>In Gnosticism, "Virtues" or "Powers" are divine emanations.</note> *one member is attributed.* Since Basilides said these things, it appears by the same reasoning that different names of these same Virtues were placed on the different body parts of the same image.

<summary>This page explains the solar symbolism of the lion in Egyptian and Roman thought, linking it to the Gnostic "Abraxas" figures which featured divine names inscribed on specific body parts.</summary>

# COMMENTARY. CHAPTER V.

...be considered as inscribed; those specifically, of course, which the person wearing the image seemed to have need of: since otherwise it would be impossible to engrave all three hundred and sixty-five names in such a narrow space. <note>The author refers to the Gnostic belief that there were 365 heavens, each with a presiding name, corresponding to the days of the year.</note> As for the rest, such as the fact that the image appears to hold a shield in one hand and a two-pronged fork <note>original: "bidentem"</note> in the other, and that a rod stands positioned above the head, these remain entirely obscure, because not all the secrets of Basilides <note>A 2nd-century Gnostic teacher in Alexandria</note> were known to the Catholics. <note>Here "Catholics" refers to the mainstream early Church writers who recorded Gnostic beliefs primarily to refute them.</note>

The gem on which a man with a lion's head is shown carrying a pipe was attributed by Macarius to the Isiac Abraxas, in whose sacred rites the pipe was carried. <note>Referring to the goddess Isis; the pipe or flute was used in her processions.</note> An image not much different exists in the collection of the eminent Albert Rubens, carved on a magnet stone; and the word **THOYTH** <note>original: "ΘΩΥΘ"</note>, which is the first part of the inscription on the back, seems to suggest that it should be ascribed to Mercury. Cicero, when discussing the *Nature of the Gods* and the multiple forms of Mercury, writes: <margin>GEM 33.</margin> <margin>Book 3.</margin> *The fifth is worshipped by the Pheneatae; he is also said to have slain Argus, and for that reason fled to Egypt, where he gave laws and letters to the Egyptians. Him the Egyptians call* **THOYTH**, *and by that same name the first month of the year is called among them.* Sanchuniatho, a most ancient author—either older than or contemporary with the times of the Trojan War—whose works Philo (not the famous Hebrew, but the one from Byblos) translated from the Phoenician idiom into the Greek language, applied a certain singular diligence in investigating the affairs of **TAAUTUS**. <note>original: "TAAVTI"</note> This was because he had placed great effort in the study of many things and desired intensely to know the history of all nations from the very first construction of the universe; he understood well enough that of all who had looked upon this light, this man was the first to discover letters and was the author of writing commentaries. He began his account with him whom the Egyptians call **Thoyth**, the Alexandrians **Thoth**, and whom the Greeks rendered as **Hermes**, that is, **MERCURY**. <margin>In Eusebius, Book 1 of the *Preparation for the Gospel*, chapter 6.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the origins of divine names, specifically linking the Egyptian and Germanic traditions to a universal concept of God, before transitioning into a critique of the Basilidian sect.</meta>

# ON THE ABRAXAS GEMS

<margin>Book 1 of *Ancient Germany*, chapter 9.</margin>
Philippus Cluverius <note>A celebrated 17th-century geographer and historian</note> proves with many arguments that the Germanic Teutons derived their entire collective name from this name **THOYTH**, or **THEVTH**. He demonstrates that this was the same **GOD** as the Germanic **TUISTO**, and the **TEUTATES** of the Spaniards and Gauls. Having discussed the origin of this name, he further concludes: This was the first common god of all nations, because his name reached not only the Egyptians and the Celts (who are situated very far from them), but also the Greeks and Latins. For the Greeks, in their own language, called this same word *THEOS* <note>original: ΘΕΟΣ</note>, by a slight change of the letters *T-H* into *TH-S*; this is best understood by those who are sufficiently familiar with the true and authentic power of the letter Theta (Θ). From this, the Dorians and Laconians first changed it into *S*, saying *SEOS* instead of *THEOS*. Yet these same Greeks, in other dialects, called the same word *THEUTH* as *ZEUS* and *DEUS*. From this, the Latins also derived their word **DEUS** <note>God</note>. Therefore, since this was the common and first God of all mortals, he was certainly none other than the one whom the sacred monuments of Moses describe as the Creator of heaven and earth and of all things established within them: whom we acknowledge and worship as the one true and eternal God under the three persons of **FATHER**, **SON**, and **HOLY SPIRIT**. Thus far Cluverius. We do not compare the trifles of the Basilidians <note>A Gnostic sect founded by Basilides in the 2nd century</note> to the supreme and unique majesty of this same great God, but according to our purpose, we proceed to slaughter them like sacrificial victims. And so as not to wander too far afield, **ABRAXAS** is inscribed on the back of a lion-headed figure as: *GIGANTORHYKTA* <note>original: ΓΙΓΑΝΤΟΡΗΚΤΑ</note>, that is, **BREAKER OF GIANTS**.

<margin>Sixtus ab Hemminga, Preface to *Astrology Refuted*.</margin>
If we seek the truth from lies, it is in a sense plainly contrary to the Basilidian doctrine, by which those men (like a great many astrologers today) fashioned for themselves—in contempt of nature and of divinity itself—false, spurious, and adulterated arts, by whose help and support they trust—

<summary>This page explores the etymological links between the Egyptian Thoth, the Germanic Tuisto, and the Greek/Latin words for God, ultimately contrasting Christian monotheism with the "trifles" of the Basilidian Gnostics. It identifies the epithet "Breaker of Giants" on a lion-headed Abraxas gem.</summary>

<keywords>Philippus Cluverius, Teutons, Thoth, Teutates, etymology of God, Zeus, Holy Trinity, Basilidians, Abraxas, Gigantorhykta, Sixtus ab Hemminga, Gnosticism</keywords>

# COMMENTARY. CHAPTER V.

They placed their trust in these things, and boasted that they possessed knowledge of the secrets <note>original: "arcana"</note> not only of this lower and elemental world—even summoning the spirits of the underworld—but also, following the example of the giants, they claimed to scale heaven itself, becoming confidants and participants in all celestial counsels and divine matters.

It is pleasing to take this opportunity to also refute the vanity of magic here, since they have claimed that images of lions of this sort, engraved on gemstones and set in a ring, can cure those suffering from stones <note>The "stone" refers to kidney or bladder stones, a common and painful ailment in the early modern period.</note>. A <term>manuscript</term> <note>abbreviated as "ms." in the original</note> codex concerning the Sculptures and Virtues of Stones, in which the seals of Cehel <note>Likely referring to Zahel or Sahl ibn Bishr, a famous 9th-century Jewish astrologer and magician</note> are recorded, from the library of the Most Serene <margin>Book 3.</margin> **CHRISTINA**, Queen of Sweden: *If a stone is found having the engraving of a <term>LION</term>, and above it the <term>MOON</term> and <term>STARS</term>; let a <term>RING</term> be made, not of bronze, with the craftsman remaining silent while the aforementioned stone is enclosed within it. For whoever wears this <term>RING</term> shall have no <term>STONE</term> in the kidneys or bladder, and shall in no way feel pain in the side or kidney.* The very emptiness of the promise makes us disbelieve this, and our religion forbids us from even trying it; although, otherwise, we are already sufficiently taught by our own experience that the torments of stones are most bitter.

## TABLES IX. X.

->ABRAXAS Harpocrates.<-

<margin>
Gem 35 and those following.
</margin>
That the <term>Basilidians</term><note>A Gnostic sect founded by Basilides in the 2nd century AD, known for their complex cosmology and use of amulets.</note> referred all their mysteries to the SUN is demonstrated by the Lotus flower, which appears quite frequently on their monstrous amulets with Harpocrates <note>The Greek god of silence, adapted from the Egyptian child-Horus</note> seated upon it; sometimes even with a lion and a lizard, symbols of the SUN, which Porphyrius <note>A Neoplatonic philosopher</note> records is called a LIZARD by some and a LION by others. 

<margin>
Book 4 on Abstinence.
Book 13, chapters 17 & 18.
</margin>
Pliny, having weighed the nature of the lotus tree, says: <note>original: "Est eodem, inquit, nomine & herba..."</note> *“There is a plant of the same name, and in Egypt, a stalk of the marsh-growing kind. For when the irrigating waters of the Nile recede, it comes forth like a bean, with a stalk and leaves packed in a dense mass, only shorter and more slender; it has a fruit on its head similar to a poppy in its notches and in every other way, containing grains like millet inside. The inhabitants let the heads rot in heaps, then separate them by washing, and having dried them, they grind them and use them for bread. There is a marvelous thing reported besides these: that when the SUN sets, those POPPIES close up and are covered by leaves. At sunrise, however, they open until they ripen and the flower, which is white, falls off.”* 

Then, concerning the stalk itself and the root of the lotus: *“They report this further regarding the Euphrates: that both the stalk itself and the flower are submerged in the evening until midnight, and the whole thing goes so deep into the water that it cannot be found even by reaching down with the hand. Then it turns and gradually raises itself, and at the rising of the SUN it emerges out of the water, opens its flower, and continues to rise so that it stands quite high above the water.”*

<margin>
Book 4.
</margin>
Theophrastus: *“That which is called the Lotus grows for the most part in the plains...”*

# COMMENTARY. CHAPTER VI.

...comes from the soil when the fields are flooded: its nature is that of a stalk similar to a bean, and the fruit grows in the same manner, though smaller and more slender. The fruit is born at the head; the flower is white, and its leaves are very close in narrowness to those of lilies; many fruits emerge, densely packed together. At the setting of the SUN they compress themselves and cover their head with wonderful industry; at sunrise they open and rise above the water; and it continues this until the head is fully formed and the flowers fall away. Anyone who has not observed through daily experience how the herb <term>Heliotrope</term> <note>literally "sun-turner"; likely referring here to the sunflower or a similar solar-tracking plant</note> always gazes at the departing SUN, and turns with it at all hours, even when shaded by clouds, would marvel at this. <margin>Pliny, Book 2, Chapter 41.</margin>

Harpocrates <note>The Greek adaptation of the Egyptian god Horus-the-Child</note> sits upon a lotus with a finger pressed to his lips; regarding him, Plutarch, Varro, and others are cited by Angelo Poliziano in his *Miscellanies*, whose lot I shall here make my own with interest: *One must not think of this GOD as imperfect,* says Plutarch, *or* <margin>Chapter 83.</margin> *as an INFANT, or finally as some kind of LEGUME <note>original: "legumen"; a reference to the bean-like lotus fruit</note>, but rather as a certain Presider and moderator of raw, imperfect, and minimally articulated speech.* FOR WHICH REASON HE ALSO COMPRESSES HIS LIPS WITH HIS FINGER, a sign of TACITURNITY and SILENCE. The same author, in his *Oracles of the Pythia*, saw that the Egyptians represent the beginning of the SUN'S rising with a PAINTED LITTLE BOY *who* SITS UPON A LOTUS. Moreover, Marcus Varro, in his book *On the Latin Language*, writes thus: *Heaven and earth, these are the same gods as the Egyptian Serapis and Isis, even if HARPOCRATES signifies with his FINGER that you should be SILENT.* Ovid must also be understood as referring to this: <margin>Book 9, Metamorphoses.</margin>

> *And holy Bubastis, and Apis of various colors,*
> *And he who suppresses his voice, and with a FINGER urges SILENCE.*

Therefore, Basilides <note>A 2nd-century Gnostic teacher in Alexandria</note> transferred this god of silence from the Egyptians into his own sacred rites; introducing a Pythagorean silence, he wished his followers to be "Harpocrates" (as the common proverb goes), while he fashioned portentous fables and of secret and ineffable...

# ON THE ABRAXAS GEMS

<margin>Nicephorus, Book 4 of Ecclesiastical History, chapter 2; Book 1, chapter 23</margin>And he extends the knowledge of things, their hidden meaning, and their discovery into infinity; he teaches that these things must be shrouded in silence, according to Irenaeus <note>Irenaeus (c. 130–202 AD) was a bishop and theologian who wrote extensively against Gnosticism.</note>; and that not many can know these things, but only one in a thousand, and two in ten thousand—holy things kept in great silence, heavenly things kept by secrecy alone. <margin>Tertullian, Against the Valentinians, chapter 1</margin> So too the Priscillianists (who followed a dogma forged from the ancient plague of Marcion and Basilides) taught their disciples *that when questioned and examined under oath, they should never reveal anything of their doctrine: and for the concealment of that doctrine, they said that perjury was permitted.* <margin>Augustine, Book on Heresies</margin> For this reason, they always had this maxim on their lips:

> *Swear, forswear, but do not betray the secret.*

<margin>GEM 40. In the second part of volume 2 of the Egyptian Oedipus, class 11, chapter 6.</margin> The most learned Kircher <note>Athanasius Kircher (1602–1680), a Jesuit scholar and polymath who attempted to decipher Egyptian hieroglyphs.</note> provides this explanation of the penultimate and last Harpocratic amulet: *On the first face,* he says, *is* HARPOCRATES <note>The Greek god of silence, adapted from the Egyptian child-god Horus.</note> *seated upon a lotus; he holds a whip in his left hand and commands silence with his right. At the root of the lotus, a* LION *is seen running with this inscription:* ABRA-CAE CICIPIM, *that is,* ABRAXAS STIRRING ALL THINGS. *By the* LION, *they understand Momphta, the Guardian of the Nile's rise, which they aptly denote by the star* SIOTHIS <note>Also known as Sirius, the Dog Star.</note> *or the* LITTLE DOG *placed upon the head of the* LION; *for at the rising of the Dog Star, when the* SUN *enters* LEO, *the flooding of the Nile occurs, while the Solar deity Harpocrates repels all the scorching power of Bebon <note>A reference to Set or Typhon, the Egyptian deity of chaos and heat.</note> with his whip. Note here that* CICIPIM, *read in reverse order as* MIPICIC, *is the same as the mother Isis, or the Moon, who is easily indicated in this amulet as the Genius of the Nile and the co-operator of* HARPOCRATES, *the solar deity. On the back part of the amulet, it holds these names inscribed:* <note>original Greek: APXEO IAΩ MEPMENNH</note> BEGINNING IAO <note>A Greek transcription of the Hebrew Tetragrammaton (Yahweh), often used in Gnostic magic.</note> OF ALL FERTILITY.*

<margin>GEM 41.</margin> Furthermore, in another amulet, there is a figure of HARPOCRATES without any letters; however, on the back part, there is an Egyptian inscription which is the same as THE GREAT IAO.

COMMENTARY. CHAPTER VI.

<sig>L 3</sig>

<margin>Book on the Climacteric Years.</margin>
Furthermore, Salmasius <note>Claude Saumaise (1588–1653), a renowned French classical scholar</note> is mistaken when he claims that the Basilidians <note>A Gnostic sect founded by Basilides in Alexandria during the 2nd century</note> depicted that god whom they called Sabaoth <note>original: Σαβαώθ; a Hebrew title for God meaning "of Hosts"</note> in the likeness of a youth, sitting atop a certain herb, with a whip in his left hand and a finger of the other hand pressed to his lips. It is clear from what has been said that this is none other than the Abraxas-Harpocrates <note>Harpocrates was the Greek adaptation of the Egyptian child-god Horus; Abraxas was a supreme deity in Gnostic cosmology</note> sitting on a lotus; nor does the inscription "SABAO" <note>original: ΣΑΒΑΩ</note> change anything, as it is also found on other Abraxas stones alongside the image of Hecate and the usual monster with a rooster's head. In what form the Gnostics declared Sabaoth—whom they called the GOD OF THE JEWS—to be, Epiphanius teaches in these words:

<margin>Heresy 26</margin>
> *As for* SABAOTH, *some believe he exists in the form of a* DONKEY, *others in the form of a* PIG. *And for this reason, they say, he had forbidden the Jews from eating swine's flesh. They claim he is the founder of heaven and earth, as well as those celestial spheres that follow, and of his own Angels.*

And after a few lines:
> *Furthermore, those unhappy people added to the fable that the same* SABAOTH *has womanly hair. This is because they think that the word* SABAOTH *is the name of some Prince, and they do not understand that wherever it is said: "Thus says the Lord God* SABAOTH," *it is not the proper name of anyone, but is rather applied to the Deity as a term of praise in preaching. Indeed, Lord* SABAOTH *means the same in Hebrew as Lord of* ARMIES <note>original: EXERCITVVM</note>. *But these people, driven by fury and madness, to the injury of their Lord, search for him who is nowhere, and have lost him who truly exists, or rather, they have lost themselves.*

Of the same origin among the Gentiles is the nomenclature of Jehovah Sabaoth <note>original: יהוה צבאות</note>, or SABAZIAN JUPITER; those who were initiated into his rites would pass a snake (into which Jupiter had been transformed) or a dragon through the fold of their robes. Clement of Alexandria says:

<margin>In Eusebius, Book 2 of the Preparation, chapter 3. Book 5.</margin>
> *The symbol of the* SABAZIAN *mysteries is surely the God passing through the breast.*

Arnobius <note>An Early Christian apologist of the 4th century</note> says: *Finally, the sacred rites themselves, and the initiation of the ritual—*

The very rites of initiation, which bear the name of the SABADIANS <note>original: "Sabadiis"; likely referring to the Sabazian mysteries</note>, can serve as a testimony to the truth; in these rites, a golden serpent is lowered into the robes of the consecrated. # CHAPTER VII.
## PLATE XI.
### ABRAXAS as Father Bromius, The Cup of the Marcosians, Horus, Isis.

<margin>GEM 42.</margin>
I have no doubt that Bacchus is represented by the first gem of this plate. Indeed, on it is a man with his head encircled by a radiant crown, wearing a long flowing robe; he extends his left hand with the index finger toward the letter X, while with his right hand he raises a cup. Below is also the mark $\Theta$ <note>The Greek letter Theta</note>, which signifies condemnation, just as X signifies absolution. And first, indeed, he is crowned with rays because Father Liber <note>An Italian deity identified with Bacchus/Dionysus</note> and Apollo are one and the same. Whence Macrobius says: <margin>Book 1 of the Saturnalia</margin> *What we have said concerning APOLLO can also be considered as said concerning LIBER. For Aristotle, who wrote the "Theologumena," asserts that APOLLO and FATHER LIBER are one and the same GOD, supporting this with many other arguments; he also says that among the Ligyreans in Thrace there is a sanctuary consecrated to LIBER from which oracles are given. But in this sanctuary, they prophesy after having taken much undiluted wine, just as at the temple of Clarius they utter oracles after water has been drunk. Also among the Lacedæmonians <note>Spartans</note>, in the rites which they celebrate for APOLLO called the Hyacinthia, they are crowned with ivy in the BACCHIC RITE. Likewise, the Boeotians, mentioning that Mount Parnassus is consecrated to APOLLO, yet at the same time in that same place both an ORACLE...*

<summary>This page explores the syncretism between Bacchus and Apollo in Gnostic thought, using a specific gem (Gem 42) to illustrate how solar and wine deities were merged. It also references the "Sabazian" serpent initiation rites as a parallel to Gnostic practices.</summary>

# COMMENTARY. CHAPTER VII.

They worship the DELPHIC shrine and the BACCHIC CAVERN as being consecrated to *one God*. For this reason, divine rites are celebrated for both APOLLO and LIBER PATER <note>The Roman name for Bacchus, the god of wine and freedom</note> on the same mountain. He then proceeds and demonstrates with many arguments, even clearer ones, that the SUN and LIBER PATER are to be considered as the same deity.

Bacchus extends his index finger toward the mark of absolution, "X"; because, as Servius <note>A 4th-century grammarian and commentator on Virgil</note> explains rather ridiculously, <margin>In the 1st book of the *Georgics*</margin> the *sacred rites of LIBER PATER pertained to the PURIFICATION OF THE SOUL: and thus men were PURIFIED by his mysteries, just as grain is purified by winnowing fans. From this, he is called LIBER, because he LIBERATES.*

At first glance, I also referred the other Gem—with the inscription IAO <note>A Greek transcription of the Hebrew Divine Name, often used in magical gems</note> on the reverse—to the drinking cup <term>cantharus: a large, two-handled wine cup associated with Bacchus</term> of the same Bacchus, which is also seen on the silver coins of the Boeotians. For, as Cornelius Labeo says in Macrobius: <margin>Book 1 of the *Saturnalia*</margin> LIBER PATER and the SUN *are signified by the name IAO*. And Epiphanius says of the Gnostics: <margin>Heresy 26</margin> *They drench themselves in ointment, and wash, and celebrate FEASTS, and indulge in DRUNKENNESS.* But the rays placed above the cup in the manner of a thunderbolt, and the horns of plenty added on either side, reminded me of the chalice of the Marcosians <note>A 2nd-century Gnostic sect founded by Marcus</note>, and of that "grace" and abundance of all things which they promised from it. Through their magical vanity, they performed their deceptions even at the sacred table, mocking those reclining there; just as Pliny describes Anaxilaus, <margin>Book 35, chapter 15</margin> *who, carrying around burning sulfur in a new cup with a coal placed beneath it, would cast a dire paleness like that of the dead over the guests by the reflection of the flame.* Irenaeus and Epiphanius say of the heretic Marcus, <margin>Irenaeus Book 1, ch. 8; also in Epiphanius, Heresy 34</margin> from whom the sect of the Marcosians sprouted: *Combining the dreams of Anaxilaus with the craftiness of those they call Magi, and mocking through these deceptions and arts, he turned all his spectators and supporters into a state of stupor and admiration: which his followers continue to do to this very day.* CUPS WITH WINE

TEMPERED WITH WINE, *feigning an act of thanksgiving and employing a longer, solemn formula of prayer, he causes the liquids to appear purple and red; so much so that you would think that supreme and celestial Grace, at his invocation, was instilling her own blood into those vessels. There is no one among the bystanders who does not vehemently desire to taste this liquor, so that the divine Grace invoked by the Magician might most abundantly drench their soul. Furthermore, handing those tempered* VESSELS *to women, he commands them to offer a blessing in his presence. Then, bringing forward another* CUP *far larger than the one upon which the woman bestowed the blessing, and pouring from that smaller one into the larger, he speaks thus:* May that Grace, which is before all things, unthinkable and ineffable, fill your inner man, and multiply her knowledge within you, sowing the grain of mustard seed into the good ground. <note>This ritual describes the "Marcosian" Eucharist, where the heretic Marcus used chemical tricks to make wine appear to turn into blood to deceive his followers.</note> *Having said these things, he immediately turns the unhappy woman toward madness and proves himself a master of illusions. Horus is wrapped in bandages, just as infants are in their cradles; Heliodorus <margin>Book 3.</margin> writes that the statues of the gods among the Egyptians were fashioned in this state. Apuleius testifies that the priests of Isis used this style of dress when he says: *The high priests, those leaders of the sacred rites, who were tightly draped in a pectoral of white linen cinched down to their very footsteps.* <margin>Book 11 of the Metamorphoses.</margin>

This same Horus is also the SUN, as are almost all the other Basilidian <note>Followers of Basilides, a 2nd-century Gnostic teacher in Alexandria</note> gods: for among the Egyptians, says Macrobius, <margin>Book 1 of the Saturnalia.</margin> APOLLO, *who is* the SUN, *is called* HORUS: *from whom the twenty-four HOURS, by which the day and night are completed, received their name; and the four seasons, by which the annual circuit is filled, are called HOURS.* <note>The author is drawing an etymological link between the god Horus and the Latin word "Hora" (Hour).</note>

The last Gem of this Table, a most precious treasure of the Most Serene Archduke LEOPOLD WILLIAM, depicts the Apotheosis <note>Elevation to divine status</note> of Isis, and her triumph over the rejected Typhon <note>The Greek name for the Egyptian god Set</note>. Julius Firmicus writes: *In their sacred rites they add tragic and deadly struggles of fearful calamity. They say that incest and adultery were committed with a sister, and that this crime was punished by the severe observations of a husband.* <margin>Book on the Error of Profane Religions.</margin> ISIS *is the sister*, OSIRIS *the brother*, TYPHON *the husband*. *When he discovered that* ISIS *his wife had been corrupted by the incestuous desires of her brother, he killed* OSIRIS, *tore him limb from limb, and cast the throbbing members of the wretched body along all the banks of the river.* ISIS, *having rejected* TYPHON, *so that she might bury both her brother and her spouse, took her sister Nephthys as a companion, and* ANUBIS *the hunter, upon whom a* DOG'S HEAD *was placed for this reason: because by the skill of a* TRACKING DOG *he found the parts of the mangled body.* Lactantius and Minutius Felix write that this same Osiris was the son of Isis (as seen below in the Abraxas Anubis), while Eusebius writes that he was her husband, brother, and son.

<margin>Apuleius, in the same place.</margin>
To the right of those reclining at the table is a chest—not that one capable of holding secrets and deeply concealing the hidden things of a magnificent religion, but the one in which Osiris was enclosed by the treachery of Typhon and cast into the river. For they relate that TYPHON, having secretly measured <margin>Plutarch, Book on Isis and Osiris</margin> the body of OSIRIS, *prepared an elegant and exquisitely adorned* CHEST *to fit his size, and brought it into the* BANQUET. *When the guests had viewed it with pleasure and admiration, Typhon promised in jest that he would give the* CHEST *as a gift to the one who, upon getting inside, matched its dimensions. When everyone had tried it and it fit no one, OSIRIS entered it and lay down. Then those who were present ran forward and slammed the lid of the* CHEST; *and when they had fastened it, partly with bolts from the outside and partly by pouring in liquid lead, they carried it to the Nile and let it down into the sea through the Tanitic mouth, where he was tasted by the* LEPIDOTUS <note>original: "lepidoto, sive squameo" - a scaly fish</note>, *the* PHAGRUS, *and the* OXYRHYNCHUS fish—

# COMMENTARY. CHAPTER VII.

*...fish, which the Egyptians detest above all others.* For this reason, a fish is seen to the left of those reclining at the table, as well as a lion, which was rejected by Horus during the search for Osiris. Plutarch says: <margin>In the same work <note>Referring to Plutarch's "On Isis and Osiris."</note></margin> **OSIRIS appeared to HORUS from the underworld, and prepared and trained him for battle; he also asked him what he considered the most beautiful thing. When HORUS replied: "To avenge one's father and mother who have been treated unjustly," Osiris further asked which animal he thought most useful for one going into battle. When HORUS replied "the HORSE," Osiris was surprised and questioned why he had not said the LION rather than the HORSE. HORUS replied that the LION is useful to one needing help, but the HORSE is suited for pursuing and cutting down a fleeing enemy. Upon hearing this, OSIRIS was delighted, feeling that HORUS was sufficiently prepared for the fight.** Apuleius also mentions a *handled cup* <note>original: "Cymbij ansati"</note> when discussing the Procession of Isis, <margin>Book 11 of the Metamorphoses</margin> and a *small vessel rounded in the manner of a breast, from which they poured libations of milk*; both of these can also be seen on the Gem.

Beneath the table are an asp, a tripod, and a rooster. Regarding the ASP, the same Apuleius says: **The ASP rose up, lifting its head high, with its neck swelling out broadly.** Through the tripod, however, Suidas <note>A 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedist.</note> says is signified the threefold distinction of time: *past, present, and future.* <margin>Under the entry "Tripod" <note>original: "Τρίπυς"</note></margin> This is a most beautiful symbol for Isis herself, whom Apuleius calls the **Mother of the stars, Parent of seasons, and the primordial offspring of the Ages.** Finally, I believe the rooster was added to this symbolic emblem because these joyful ceremonies and festive spectacles began at the rising of the Sun, to whom the rooster is sacred. Again, Apuleius writes: **The clouds of dark night having been put to flight, the golden SUN RISES, and behold, with religious and truly triumphal movement, the crowds fill all the streets.**

<margin>Book 1.</margin>
Finally, to commend the vanity of this Isiac amulet, Diodorus <note>Diodorus Siculus, a Greek historian</note> writes that **ISIS**, as the inventor of many medicines for diseases, contributed greatly to the art of medicine; and that she, having also attained **IMMORTALITY**, delights in the worship of men and is especially occupied with their **HEALTH**. Indeed, she openly assists in dreams those whom she deems worthy, showing both her **DIVINE POWER** and her generosity toward **HEALING THE NATIONS**. They say the evidence for these things is found not in Greek fables, but in **CERTAIN WORKS**: for nearly the entire world worships **THIS GODDESS** because of her manifest **CURES FOR DISEASES**. For they say that in dreams she provides **AID FOR THE ILLNESSES** of those not in good health; and those who obey her warnings are cured beyond expectation, even those whose safety the physicians had despaired of. Also, those weak in sight or in another part of the body, by imploring the power of this goddess, are restored to their former health. To these things Pignorius <note>Lorenzo Pignoria, a 17th-century antiquarian</note> adds a bronze coin

# C H A P T E R VIII.

### T A B L E S XII. XIII.

**Abraxas** *the Dog-headed, Anubis, Hermanubis, or Mercury: a Mithraic sacred object, representing the Sun beginning to pass through Aries.* <note>The author connects the Egyptian Anubis with the Persian mysteries of Mithras and the zodiacal sign of the Ram.</note>

<margin>In the Sea Dialogues.</margin>
Lucian writes concerning Mercury <note>original: "Mercurio"</note> that after he had gone before Isis as she crossed over into Egypt, he was changed from a youth into one with a <term>dog's face</term> <note>original: "κυνοπρόσωπον" (kynoprosonpon)</note>. Moreover, they relate (these are the words of Diodorus <margin>Book 1, ch. 2.</margin>) *that Osiris, King of Egypt, having established his kingdom and left the care of all matters to his wife Isis, gave to her* **Mercury**, *whose counsel she was to use, since he seemed to excel among his friends in wisdom.* The same author later says: *The god, who among them is called* **Anubis**, *they <term>fashion with a canine head</term>, signifying that he was the <term>guardian</term> of the bodies of Osiris and Isis.* <margin>Book 2, ch. 4.</margin> Some assert that the dogs of Isis, at the time when Osiris was being sought, drove away both wild beasts and all who stood in the way, and together they tracked down the body of Osiris. And for that reason, in the festivals of Isis, dogs lead the procession, showing ancient gratitude toward dogs according to the old custom. Eusebius, concerning the consecration of animals among the Egyptians, says: There are those who bring forward a third cause for this kind of worship <margin>Book 2 of the Preparation for the Gospel, ch. 1.</margin> namely, the utility which they perceived from this genus of living creatures; that dogs are of use to men both for hunting and for protection; for which reason they say that god, whom they name **Anubis**, **is of a canine head**, to signify that he was the Praetorian soldier <note>A member of the elite imperial guard.</note> of Osiris and Isis. Although there are those who wish to claim that Isis, at the time she sought Osiris, took dogs with her to lead the way, who [drove off] wild beasts, and

<margin>Book 8 of the Aeneid</margin>
> MONSTROUS GODS OF EVERY KIND, AND THE BARKING ANUBIS:
<note>original: "OMNIGENUMQ; DEVM MONSTRA, & LATRATOR ANVBIS"</note>

Because, he says, he is depicted with a **DOG'S** head, they wish him to be MERCURY, *for the reason that nothing is more keen-scented than a dog*. Hence in Plu-
<margin>Book on Isis and Osiris</margin>
tarch he is called *Anubis* and *Hermanubis* <note>original: "Ἄνουβις, & Ἑρμάνουβις"</note>, although he distinguishes one from the other. Furthermore, that Mercury is the same as Apol-
<margin>Book 1 of the Saturnalia</margin>
lo or the Sun is apparent from this, says Macrobius: *that among many nations the star of* MERCURY *is referred to by the name of* APOLLO. *And because* APOLLO *presides over the Muses*, MER-CURY *imparts speech, which is the gift of the muses. Besides these things, there are many proofs that* MERCURY *is considered to be the* SUN. *First, because the statues of* MERCURY *are adorned with feathered wings; which thing demonstrates the velocity of the* SUN. *For because we believe* MERCURY *to be the master of the mind, and we understand him to be so named from the Greek word for "interpreting"* <note>original: "ἀπὸ τοῦ ἑρμηνεύειν" (apo tou hermeneuein)</note>, *and the* SUN *is the mind of the world (moreover, the speed of the mind is the greatest, as Homer says, "like a wing or a thought")* <note>original: "ὡσεὶ πτερὸν ἠὲ νόημα" (hosei pteron ee noema)</note> *therefore* MERCURY *is adorned with feathers, as if by the very nature of the* SUN. *The Egyptians complete this argument more clearly, fashioning winged statues of the* SUN *himself, for which they use more than one color. For they fashion one with a blue appearance, and the other bright. Of these, they call the bright one the "Upper" and the blue one the "Lower." Moreover, the name of the "Lower"* SUN *is given when he completes his course in the lower hemisphere, that is, in the wintry signs; the "Upper," when he circles the summer part of the Zodiac.* There is a similar fiction regarding MERCURY under another fable, when he is regarded as the administrator and messenger between the upper and lower gods.

But now let us approach the individual amulets more closely, of which kind Prudentius teaches that Cleopatra possessed several, specifically in Gems and women's finery, where he sings:
<margin>Book 2 against Symmachus</margin>
> *The defeated Cleopatra gave up certain*
> *Images of a* **DOG'S** *HEAD.*
<note>original: "Quasdam victa dedit CAPITIS Cleopatra CANINI Effigies."</note>

# COMMENTARY. CHAPTER VIII.

<margin>
GEM 46.
Coptic Forerunner, last chapter.
Book on Climacteric Years.
</margin>
First, although it appears to belong to the Gnostics <note>A diverse group of early religious thinkers who sought "gnosis" or secret knowledge</note>, Kircher says, *the Coptic letters, which are found everywhere similar to the Hieroglyphics, clearly show it to be Egyptian:* add to this that the Gnostics took everything from the Egyptians. Indeed, as Salmasius notes, two languages in particular were seen as sacred and legitimate for these talismans <note>original: τελέσματα (telesmata), consecrated objects or charms</note>, through which it was believed some efficacy was imprinted on the consecrations composed and written in their speech: namely, the Egyptian and the Assyrian (or Chaldean), as Iamblichus teaches in his *Letter to Porphyry*. He adds the reason for this: because they wanted the sacred dialect of the "Sacred Nations" (as the Egyptian and Assyrian were once considered) to be suitable for sacred rites. Therefore, their speech was pleasing to the gods and "kindred" <note>original: συγγενὴς (syngenes)</note> to them, because the first nations to use that speech revealed the names and worship of the gods to men.

<margin>
GEM 47.
</margin>
Another gem of immense value is a wheel-shaped Chalcedony, featuring the image of the Isiac Cynocephalus <note>A baboon-headed figure associated with the Egyptian goddess Isis and the god Thoth</note>. Its form is hexagonal, with the angles ending in a point, which is the way crystals always arise from their origin and by nature itself; Andreas Cesalpinus teaches this at length in his book *On Metals*. He shows that the sides bend into a point when material is lacking; since this material is also purer, it is more constricted and appears clearer. It is exactly as in this gem, which Pliny might seem to have had before his eyes when he wrote of the Indian diamond: <margin>Book 37, ch. 4</margin> *Certain ones*, he says, *are not different from* CRYSTAL *in their kinship, having a translucent color, and with the* SIX-SIDED SMOOTHNESS *of the sides, it is* WHIRLED *into a* POINT. Hence, in Solinus too <margin>Chapter 36</margin>, *Crystal is* SIX-ANGLED. Now, among the poets, there is frequent mention of the "rhombus" or "whirling wheel" <note>A tool used in ancient magic, often spun on a string to cast spells</note> which was revolved by sorcerers in magic rites. Horace writes regarding Canidia <note>A famous witch in Roman poetry</note>:

> Now, now I yield to your effective science
> As a suppliant, and pray by the realms of Proserpina,

> *By the powers of Diana that must not be moved,*
> *And by the books of incantations, which are able*
> *To call down the stars unfastened from the sky,*
> *Canidia, spare me at last from your SACRED WORDS,*
> *And release, release the WHIRLIGIG, letting it spin backward.*
<note>original: "Per & Dianæ non mouenda numina..." from Horace, Epodes 17. Canidia was a famous sorceress in Roman literature.</note>

<margin>Book 9, Epigram 30</margin>
And Martial writes:
> *What madam now will know how to draw down the Moon with a THESSALIAN MAGIC WHEEL,*
> *Or how to sell these or those marriage beds?*
<note>The "Thessalian magic wheel" refers to a device used by witches to cast spells, often associated with drawing the moon from the sky.</note>

The commentator on Theocritus says that a magic wheel of this kind is also called a <term>TROCHUS</term> <note>A hoop or spinning top, used here in a magical context.</note>. However, I am led to believe that this gem was used for play, and was spun with the fingers like a roll of the dice, by the image impressed on one of its sides. This image shows the <term>Cynocephalus</term> <note>A dog-headed baboon, often associated with the Egyptian god Thoth or the attendants of Isis.</note> of Isis, with characters inscribed below. It is sitting on the ground in the posture of one imploring ultimate help, with its chest bared for mourning and its head veiled as if in grief. Regarding this, Minucius Felix writes in his *Octavius*: *Isis, with her <term>CYNOCEPHALUS</term> and her bald-headed priests, mourns, laments, and searches for her lost son. The wretched followers of Isis beat their breasts and imitate the grief of the most unhappy mother. Soon, when the little boy is found, Isis rejoices, the priests exult, and the <term>CYNOCEPHALUS</term> glories in being the finder. Nor do they cease every year to either lose what they find, or find what they lose. Is it not ridiculous to either mourn what you worship, or worship what you mourn?* 

<margin>Book 1, Chapter 21</margin>
Lactantius also writes: *The Egyptian rites of Isis consist of her having either lost or found her little son. For first, her priests, having shaved their bodies, beat their breasts and lament, just as she herself had done when she lost him. Then the boy is produced as if found, and that mourning is changed into joy.* Almost the same thing happens in a game of dice, which was also employed in sacred rites, especially Egyptian ones; so that, of course, while the die is cast, you are forced either to mourn because you have lost, or, having obtained the <term>Cynocephalus</term>—or rather, having sought such a face—to exult with him.

<summary>The author discusses the connection between magical spinning wheels (rhombus) and Abraxas gems, suggesting some were used as gaming pieces or dice. He links the imagery of the dog-headed Cynocephalus to the cyclical mourning and rejoicing found in the Egyptian mysteries of Isis.</summary>
