# CHAPTER XVI.

## PLATE XXVIII.

### *Heads of Philosophers, Basilidian or Egyptian perforated Gems: A very rare Abraxas on a coin.*

<margin>GEM 118.</margin>
In this final section, it seemed appropriate to record these perforated gems, which further prove that such items were occasionally tied to the body in a superstitious manner, or suspended from the neck, to ward off evil spells. The first, which is solid agate, depicts the head of some philosopher covered with a small skullcap, with a long beard and flowing hair. Epiphanius <note>A 4th-century Church Father known for his writings against heresies.</note> says of the Carpocratian Gnostics and the followers of Basilides: <margin>Heresy 27, from Book 1 of Irenaeus, chapter 24.</margin> *They possess images painted in colors, some even made of gold or silver, or other materials, which they claim are likenesses of JESUS; they say these were fashioned by Pontius Pilate in the likeness of Christ during the time he lived among men. But they keep such IMAGES HIDDEN, as well as those of certain PHILOSOPHERS, such as Pythagoras, Plato, Aristotle, and others; they mix these with the images of Christ I mentioned, and they worship them all together, set upright, performing the rites of the Gentiles before them.* <margin>Licetus, On Ring Gems, chapter 115. Cicero, On the Ends of Good and Evil.</note> Thus the Stoics used to wear Zeno, the Academics Plato, and the Peripatetics wore Aristotle engraved on their signet rings, and many wore Epicurus; of whom Tully <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero.</note> says, *the image of Epicurus was commonly seen in Rome, not only in paintings, but even on drinking cups and RINGS.* <margin>GEM 119.</margin> On the obverse side of the second Gem is the head of an Egyptian deity,

# ON ABRAXAS GEMS

<margin>Book 1, chapter 18.</margin>
On the reverse side is engraved a small branch of balsam. Dioscorides <note>A Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD.</note> relates that it grows in *a certain valley of Judea, and in Egypt*; which Claudian also indicates:

<margin>In the Epithalamium of Palladius and Celerina.</margin>
> *And others poured BALSAM from jeweled jars*
> *Throughout the whole house, which the BARK OF THE NILE,*
> *Wounded by a hard nail, sweats out from its rich wound.*
> <note>original: "Gemmatisque alij per totum BALSAMA tectum / Effudêre cadis, duro quae faucius ungue / NILIACVS pingui desudat vulnere CORTEX."</note>

Prosper Alpinus <note>A 16th-century Italian physician and botanist known for his study of Egyptian plants.</note> describes its seeds, such as those seen here, as having *the shape and size of terebinth fruits, POINTED AT THE ENDS and THICK IN THE MIDDLE.* He adds that *there is no medicine which the Egyptians use more frequently, applying it to almost all diseases, especially for restoring strength to the stomach.* For this reason, the kings of Egypt held nothing more precious to give as gifts to foreign kings and Emperors, whether to initiate or preserve friendship with them. Hence, it is likely that they used gems of this kind, marked with the image of balsam, in place of amulets.

<margin>GEM 120.</margin>
The third gem, likewise solid and made of agate, the size of a medium pigeon's egg, appears to be a military amulet. On it, a Crab and a Butterfly are symbols of military Prudence <term>Prudentia: the virtue of foresight and practical wisdom</term>, which is also indicated on both sides by crowned figures girded with swords, as if debating some stratagem. Oppianus <note>A Greco-Roman poet known for his works on fishing and hunting.</note> writes:

<margin>Book 2.</margin>
> *Skillful PRUDENCE was granted from on high to the CRAB,*
> *For whom sweet prey is obtained without great labor.*
> *The oyster extends its shells, and opens the bars*
> *Of its gates, feeding on the mud, and spread out on the rocks*
> *It longs for the waters: the CRAB, moving sideways,*
> *Lifts a small stone from the wet cliff, and with curved claws*
> *Holds it, and drops the stone into the open shells.*
> *He sits by, and his side is filled by the friendly feast.*
> <note>original: "Concessa ex alto solers PRVDENTIA CANCRO..." This describes the crab's cleverness in preventing an oyster from closing its shell.</note>

# COMMENTARY. CHAPTER XVI.

> *The oyster is unable to close its twin shells with a firm bond,*
> *For it gapes open, separated by wide gates,*
> *And dying, it leaves behind the sated and broad CRAB.*
<note>original Latin: "Non potis est nexu geminas occludere lances / Ostrea..." This verse describes the crab's habit of wedging a stone into an oyster's shell to prevent it from closing, allowing the crab to feast on the meat inside.</note>

From this, the crab is said to be as eager for food as it is watchful of danger. And they say that the Emperor Octavian Augustus was so delighted by this proverb, **Hasten slowly**, <note>original: "Festina lentè"</note> that he used it not only in common conversation but also in his letters; he even had a butterfly and a crab engraved on his seal. With these two small creatures, he depicted the meaning of the proverb in the manner of a Hieroglyphic: by the butterfly, a winged and swift little worm, he suggested speed; but by the crab, which moves with a backward and slow motion, he suggested delay and hesitation. Regarding the crab appearing alone, Macarius says: *Whenever we see the CRAB alone on Gems along with LETTERS and WORDS, it will be a symbol of ABRAXAS.* <note>Abraxas was a mystical word and deity in Gnostic systems, often represented on amulets to signify the 365 days of the year.</note>

And so much for the Gems engraved with Basilidian or Egyptian characters, to which the Ancients attributed great power, especially those on which the image of a Cynocephalus <note>A dog-headed deity, usually representing the Egyptian god Anubis or Thoth.</note> or Harpocrates <note>The Greek interpretation of the Egyptian child-god Horus, often depicted with a finger to his lips as a sign of silence.</note> was carved.

<margin>Book 2, chapter 7.</margin>
Hence Pliny says: *They are enslaved to foreign rites, and THEY WEAR GODS ON THEIR FINGERS, and they even worship MONSTERS.*

<margin>Book 33, chapter 3.</margin>
And elsewhere the same author complains that *even men are beginning to wear HARPOCRATES and statues of EGYPTIAN DEITIES on their FINGERS.*

<margin>In the Apology.</margin>
Apuleius states: *It is my custom to carry the IMAGE of some GOD with me wherever I go.* To keep this superstition away from his followers, Pythagoras forbade them <term>to carry the IMAGE of a GOD in a RING</term> <note>original Greek: "ἐν δακτυλίῳ εἰκόνα Θεοῦ περιφέρειν"</note>.

Macarius points out a single coin marked on the other side with the usual monster of Abraxas. But Gassendi also writes,

<margin>In his Life.</margin>
that in the year 1600, Peiresc <note>Nicolas-Claude Fabri de Peiresc (1580–1637), a famous French scholar and antiquarian.</note> showed to Cardinals Baronius and Bellarmine **VARIOUS GEMS AND COINS** upon which the **BASILIDIANS, VALENTINIANS**, and other heretics used to in-
->scribe<-

<meta>The text breaks mid-word "inscribe" (inscribere) to be continued on the following page.</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the symbolism of the crab in ancient emblems, specifically the "Hasten Slowly" motto of Augustus, and critiques the ancient practice of wearing Gnostic and Egyptian deities on finger rings.</summary>
<keywords>Festina Lente, Abraxas, Gnosticism, Basilidians, Harpocrates, Amulets, Augustus, Peiresc, Ancient Jewelry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Gnostic gems and the mystical use of alphabets, following the previous analysis of Abraxas stones.</meta>

# ON THE ABRAXAS GEMS

<margin>In the Notes to Poem 26 by Paulinus. Tertullian, *On the Prescription of Heretics*, chapter 50.</margin> they were accustomed to use BARBARIC WORDS <note>original: "VOCABVLA BARBARA"; these were non-Greek or non-Latin sounds used in magic to invoke power</note> to conceal their opinions. Heribert Rosweyde <note>A Jesuit hagiographer and scholar (1569–1629)</note> also cites a Basilidian coin in the collection of the Duke of Aarschot, which was inscribed with the ALPHABET; he attributes it to the Marcosians <note>A Gnostic sect founded by Marcus, who believed the universe was created through the sounds of the alphabet</note>, who, composing a new heresy from the Greek ALPHABET, denied that the truth could be found without these letters. Indeed, they claimed that the entire fullness and perfection of truth was arranged within these letters; for this reason, they say Christ said: *I am the Alpha and the Omega.*

As for me, I have never seen "Abraxas" on coins, and I think it is very rare. For those coins found in bronze and silver, often divided by grids with Arabic numerals or Hebrew letters, and frequently with celestial signs added to the edges—and even more often the names of the Angels ANAEL, SATOVIEL, MICHAEL, GABRIEL, HAGIEL, SATQUIEL, RAPHAEL, ZADRIEL—these coins, I say, are far more recent than the Basilidian monsters. They should be categorized as magical incantations, or what they call "characters of a high name" <note>original: "characteres alti nominis"; likely referring to the "Shem HaMephorash" or the explicit name of God used in later Renaissance magic</note>; I have seen a great many of this kind.

->[Image: A woodcut illustration of a decorative footed bowl or tazza overflowing with various fruits, including grapes, apples, and pears.]<-

<summary>The author discusses the Gnostic use of alphabets and "barbaric words" to hide secret truths, specifically refuting the antiquity of certain magical coins by attributing them to later medieval or Renaissance occult practices rather than early Gnosticism.</summary>
<keywords>Abraxas, Basilidians, Marcosians, magic coins, Angel names, alphabets, Heribert Rosweyde, Gnosticism, talismans</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the commentary, focusing on the distinction between the heretical use of images by Gnostics and the orthodox veneration of sacred icons.</meta>

# CONCLUSION OF THE WORK
## CHAPTER XVII

*The "Transformation into God" <note>original: "Θεομόρφωσις" (Theomorphosis)</note> practiced by the followers of Basilides, and not the worship and veneration of sacred images, was what Irenaeus criticized.*

In describing these things, surely the Cynic Varros <note>Marcus Terentius Varro, a Roman scholar often cited by Church Fathers</note> would not have done enough, nor the Lentuli, nor the Roman mime-writers like Hostilius, of whom Tertullian speaks in his *Apology*: *The Roman Cynic Varro introduces THREE HUNDRED JUPITERS (or "Jupiters" as one must say) without heads.* Other witty talents also work for your pleasures through the DISGRACE OF THE GODS. Consider the charms of the Lentuli and Hostilii: whether you are laughing at the MIMES or at YOUR GODS in your jokes and tricks. Were these things not acts of madness—and great madness at that—with everyone inventing whatever happened to fall into his mind? These are the "pearls" of the followers of Basilides; these are those seeds of a monstrous art that survive even today. These are surely the images that Irenaeus condemned, which the followers of Basilides used, as did the Marcosians and Valentinians <note>various Gnostic sects of the early Christian era</note>, who pressed everything INTO IMAGES, being themselves clearly IMAGINARY Christians.

<margin>Tertullian against the Valentinians.</margin>
This is a total casting out of Divinity rather than a Theology; it is so infamous that it seems shameful to spend more words tearing it apart. *That god of yours*, says Firmicus, *is not TWOFOLD, but MULTIFORM: for the form of the old deceiver is changed into many appearances.*

<margin>Book on the Error of Profane Religions.</margin>
Believe me, the devil has passed over nothing that might weaken or [debilitate] <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "debilita-" indicates the next page begins with "debilitaret" (might weaken/debilitate) miserable man.</note>

<summary>The author argues that early Church condemnations of images, such as those by Irenaeus, were directed at Gnostic "monstrous art" and pagan parodies rather than legitimate Christian icons. He cites early Christian apologists like Tertullian and Firmicus Maternus to illustrate the absurdity of these heretical and pagan representations.</summary>
<keywords>Basilidians, Irenaeus, Tertullian, Varro, Valentinians, Firmicus Maternus, Gnosticism, idolatry vs. icons, Roman mimes</keywords>

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on Abraxas gems, transitioning from a critique of Gnostic "monsters" to a defense of orthodox Christian iconography.</meta>

# ON THE ABRAXAS GEMS

...might weaken or destroy them. Therefore, he <note>The author refers here to the Devil or the "enemy of mankind."</note> transforms himself into **all forms** with manifold diversity; for this reason, he has composed himself with the cunning of various kinds, so that he might destroy men ensnared by his various and multiple frauds. Thus, it was his pleasure, even from the very beginning of the nascent Church, to corrupt the excellent fruit of truth with the seed of adultery.

<margin>St. Ignatius, Epistle to the Trallians.</margin>
But you, whoever you are who shall either read of these monsters or look upon them with your eyes, flee indeed from Basilides <note>A Gnostic teacher in 2nd-century Alexandria who claimed that "Abraxas" was the supreme deity.</note> and the whole collection of his unworthiness. But also, let a compassion for the most unhappy blindness and madness of men come over you: keep in mind God—not a foul and portentous thing under the shameful and mute image of a demon—but that one great, living, and true God, the fountain of all good, in whom we hope, who does not despise us, nor remove His salvation from our race. By His great kindness, it must be believed that it has happened that we have before our eyes the darkness into which those destitute of the light of divine things have fallen. When you gaze upon their masks and portentous idols, remember how blinded by darkness they were, so that you may value more highly the benefit of Him by whom you were rescued, and love Christ all the more fervently, who is "beautiful in form beyond the sons of men." Revere His most holy Mother in sacred images—that woman clothed with the Sun, whom we know to have been entirely beautiful. Finally, to end with the words of Pope Gregory II to the Emperor Leo the Isaurian <note>Leo III, the Byzantine Emperor who initiated Iconoclasm, the destruction of religious images.</note> when he promulgated edicts against sacred images: *Bestow honor upon, and glorify our holy and glorious Fathers and doctors, who, according to God, dispelled the blindness from our hearts and eyes and restored sight to them. But you have written: "How is it that in the six Councils nothing was said about Images?" Truly, Emperor, nothing was said about bread and water either, whether they should be eat-*

<margin>Found in Baronius <note>Cesare Baronius, the great Catholic historian.</note> for the year of Christ 726.</margin>

<summary>The author warns against the deceptive "monsters" of Gnostic imagery, contrasting the grotesque Abraxas figures with the beauty of Christ and the Virgin Mary while defending the use of holy images against Iconoclasm.</summary>
<keywords>Basilides, Gnosticism, Abraxas gems, Pope Gregory II, Leo the Isaurian, Iconoclasm, sacred images, Church Fathers, Baronius</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes Chapter 17 of the commentary, continuing the defense of religious imagery against the accusations of contemporary reformers by contrasting orthodox icons with Gnostic idols.</meta>

### COMMENTARY. CHAPTER XVII.

*...to be considered, whether one should eat or not eat; drink or not drink; since these things were handed down from the beginning for the preservation of human life: so also were images handed down, and the Bishops themselves used to bring images to the Church Councils. Nor did any of those who love Christ and are religious men undertake a journey or pilgrimage without images; seeing as they were endowed with virtue and upright before God.* <note>The author argues that using religious images is as natural and ancient a tradition as the basic rules of survival.</note> I believe it has been sufficiently demonstrated that the images condemned by Irenaeus <note>Irenaeus (c. 130–202 AD) was a Bishop and theologian who wrote against heresies.</note> were not of this kind, and how different our images are from those of the followers of Basilides <note>Basilides was an early Gnostic teacher in Alexandria; his followers were accused of using magical amulets and idols.</note> which we have displayed—regardless of how the modern innovators <note>original: "novatores" – a common 16th/17th-century term for Protestant Reformers.</note> foolishly babble today, being themselves <term>all-changing</term> <note>original: "παντόμορφοι" (pantomorphoi) – meaning multiform or taking many shapes; here used to insult the inconsistency of his opponents.</note> Basilidians.

# APPROVAL OF THE CENSOR.

The *Abraxas Apistopistus* <note>The title translates roughly to "Abraxas the Unbelievable" or "Faithless," referring to the deceptive nature of the amulets.</note> of Jean Macaire <gloss>the deceased</gloss> <note>original: "μακαρίτου" (makaritou) - a Greek term for the late or blessed</note>, Canon of Aire, and the *Abraxas Proteus* of the noble and very Reverend Lord Jean Chiflet, Canon of Tournai, reveal the monsters of the "Abraxas Gems" and the "Basilidian Suns." These works expose the horrors nurtured by the first heretics <note>The Basilidians were a 2nd-century Gnostic sect in Alexandria followed by the teacher Basilides</note> with such a vast number and such a wealth of most recondite <term>recondite: hidden or obscure knowledge</term> learning, that I cannot sufficiently praise or value the labors undertaken in tracking these down from every side. Through these, the thirst of every most learned man can be quenched; especially on a subject so ancient and yet so new, which has remained hidden until now from the most eager minds of our age. It could not have been handled with more subtlety or elegance than by the hand and pen of these two most learned Canons. I have so judged, at Brussels, the 24th of December, 1656.

->*Antoon Sanders, Priest,*<-
->*Licentiate of Sacred Theology, Canon and Penitentiary*<-
->*of Ypres, Censor of Books.*<-

---

# SUMMARY OF THE PRIVILEGE.

Philip IV, *Catholic King of the Spains and the Indies, as well as the most powerful Prince of the Belgians and Burgundians, has sanctioned by his diploma that no one, without the will of Balthasar Moretus <note>The Moretus family were the successors to the famous Plantin Press in Antwerp</note>, son of Jan the younger, shall in any way print the book entitled: The Abraxas, or Apistopistus, of Jean Macaire, Canon of Aire; which is an antiquarian disquisition on the Basilidian Gems: to which is added the Abraxas Proteus, or the portentous variety of the multiform Basilidian Gem; presented and illustrated with a Commentary by Jean Chiflet, Canon of Tournai, etc. Nor shall anyone import into these provinces of Lower Germany <note>The Spanish Netherlands, roughly modern-day Belgium</note> copies printed elsewhere, or hold them for sale. Whoever does otherwise shall be punished by the confiscation of the books and other heavy penalties; as is more broadly evident in the letters given at Brussels on the 15th day of March, 1657.*

->Signed,<-
->Loyens.<-

<summary>This page features the formal ecclesiastical endorsement by Antoon Sanders and the royal copyright (privilege) granted by King Philip IV of Spain to the printer Balthasar Moretus for the publication of a study on Gnostic amulets.</summary>

# TO JEAN-JACQUES CHIFFLET, KNIGHT,
### AND COUNT OF THE ROYAL PHYSICIANS,

->HIS SON, JEAN CHIFFLET, SENDS GREETINGS.<-

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'V' featuring intricate floral scrollwork, foliage, and a central grotesque or cherubic face within the ornamentation.</image-desc>

THERE COMES to you, most loving Father, the Prince of Ethical Philosophy <note>A reference to Socrates, often considered the founder of moral philosophy</note>; and with a rare novelty of subject matter, this Old Man of ancient integrity and wisdom comes forth into the public eye. He appears even in a mask, as he once did upon the Stage; and wearing the buskin of Tragedy <note>The "cothurnus" or high boot worn by ancient Greek tragic actors</note>, as if ordered to go toward the cold hemlock <note>The poison used in Socrates' execution</note>. He is foul in appearance, shaggy, and entangled in an obscure image with monstrous additions, which it seems no one could reveal—not even Oedipus <note>The mythical figure famous for solving the riddle of the Sphinx</note>—unless he wished to wrestle with ghosts. In which endeavor, I ask for indulgence, if

<summary>Jean Chifflet dedicates this work on Socratic moral philosophy to his father, Jean-Jacques Chifflet, using the metaphor of a masked tragic actor to describe the difficult and "monstrous" nature of the ancient text he is presenting.</summary>
<keywords>Jean-Jacques Chifflet, Jean Chifflet, Socrates, Ethics, Philosophy, Dedication, Classical Allusions</keywords>

anywhere, easily found by those who wander. But there are even today, and will be hereafter, as you yourself know, certain unskilled Sophists <note>In this context, "Sophists" refers to pedantic or superficial scholars who prioritize rhetoric over true wisdom.</note> who, since they are themselves devoid of all humanity and liberal disciplines, condemn the study of these things in others. They take offense that many scholars scatter references to poets, historians, and orators throughout their writings, using them as a kind of seasoning to bring a certain sweetness to learned disputations. Dionysius Petavius <note>A prominent 17th-century Jesuit theologian and scholar.</note> notes that men of this same ilk even envied Bishop Synesius <note>A 4th-century philosopher-bishop known for blending Neoplatonism with Christianity.</note> for this very quality.

<margin>

</margin>

To their palates, not even Attic dainties <note>original: "Attica bellaria"; a metaphor for refined, elegant Greek wit or literature.</note> would give satisfaction; nothing is less credible to them than the idea that these studies hold something grander in their depths than they promise on the surface. These men,

<margin>
*Nicolaus Alemannus, Preface to "The Walls of the Lateran".*
*Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, Letter to Ermolao Barbaro.*
</margin>

it seems, would think little of the truly admirable Sileni of Alcibiades <note>A famous metaphor from Plato’s "Symposium" describing Socrates: like statues of the satyr Silenus that look ugly on the outside but contain golden images of gods within.</note>—statues with a shaggy, foul, and contemptible exterior, but filled within with gems and rare, precious treasures. Indeed, I do not deny that among those enslaved to the glory of the philosopher's cloak <note>original: "palliati"; referring to those who wear the 'pallium', the traditional garment of Greek philosophers.</note>, beneath an unkempt head, a neglected beard, and that harsh style of the philosophers, there lies the greatest

<summary>The author defends the use of classical literature and "sweet" rhetoric in scholarly writing against "Sophists" who prefer a dry, unrefined style. He uses the metaphor of Alcibiades' Sileni to argue that a rough exterior can hide profound inner wisdom.</summary>

as the Poet sings, designating <term>Socrates</term> <note>The famous Athenian philosopher (c. 470–399 BC).</note> and his homeland by Mount Hymettus in the land of Attica; in which city, renowned for wisdom, he performed those deeds of heroic virtue before the Gospel was even given,
<margin>Pierre Halloix, On the Writers of the Eastern Church, in the life of Saint Justin.</margin>
which we ourselves fail to perform even after the Gospel and after the examples of Christ our Lord have been placed before our very eyes. For he neither avenged injuries, nor wished them to be avenged, but rather forbade it: kicked with a heel, he greeted his striker; struck on one cheek, he offered the other; he bore a most unjust death with a most composed mind, and before that death, he predicted with certainty what would happen to his slanderers after he was gone; correcting that well-worn and commonly accepted proverb: *That one ought to do well by friends, and ill by enemies*; he praised and left the first part as it was, *To do well by friends*

<margin>Themistius, Oration 4 to the Emperor Valens.</margin>

# ON
# JEAN CHIFLET’S
# SOCRATES,
### BY NICASIUS FABIUS
### CANON AND OFFICIAL
### OF TOURNAI
## A POEM IN IAMBIC SENARIUS

The human mind is the handiwork of the BEING OF BEINGS <note>original: "ENTIVM ENTIS"; a philosophical term for God as the ultimate source of all existence</note>,
An image of the Divine Light, a portion of the Deity,
Ready to climb to the heights above, if only it wishes
To know itself, and to contemplate from whence it came.
But the body is a heavy and earthy thing for the mind,
And all too clinging to its own lowly origin;
The outward form offers no protection, whether it be
Remarkable for its beauty or remarkable for its ugliness.
Who was that great SOCRATES, the glory of Wisdom <note>original: "Sophiæ"</note>?
With what sort of face, or what brow was he endowed?
He was unlovely of mouth, and with a swollen lip,
With a balding head, smooth-pated yet with a bristly beard,
With shaggy hair and skin rough to the touch,
With watery eyes and cheeks full of bumps,
With wide nostrils, and a nose utterly flat:
By Pollux, if you saw such a man, you would think him a Master-Thersites <note>Thersites was a figure from Homer's Iliad known as the ugliest man in the Greek army; "Archi-Thersiten" implies Socrates was even more unsightly.</note>.
But what a soul he had! What strength of heart!

<summary>A commendatory poem by Nicasius Fabius of Tournai reflecting on the contrast between Socrates' famously ugly physical appearance and his divine, wisdom-seeking soul.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Jean Chiflet, Nicasius Fabius, Tournai, Neoplatonism, physical vs spiritual, Thersites, philosophy</keywords>

*And now I set forth how great his virtue was.*
*A sublime soul, greater than his own age,*
*And deeply conscious of his own origin,*
<margin>He was accustomed to frequenting the halls of Wisdom</margin>
*He was accustomed to enter the halls of* WISDOM <term>Sapientia: the personification of philosophical insight</term>,
*And the temples of* JUSTICE <term>Themis: the Greek goddess of divine law and order</term>, *and the shrines of* HONOR
*To examine them with the piercing eye of the mind.*
*Furthermore, even toward the beginnings of the first light,*
*With his gaze turned and his body unmoving,*
*As if he had in very deed migrated from the body,*
*Both through the night and through the long day with steady foot,*
*He sought to drink in those glories which nourishing virtue prepares for the soul,*
*Glories sweeter than Attic honey* <note>Attica, the region around Athens, was famous for the high quality of its honey from Mount Hymettus</note>.
*If malicious calumny grazed him,*
*If a bold little pettifogger* <term>rabula: a brawling or unskillful lawyer</term> *tripped his heel,*
*Or a petulant buffoon offered an insult,*
*Or if his wife Xantippe—the worst of the worst—*
*Drenched him with Patroclian wash-water* <note>A reference to a famous anecdote where Socrates' wife Xantippe poured a chamber pot over his head; "Patroclian" refers to Patroclus, a man noted for his filthiness</note>;
*He corrupted his constancy with no grief,*
*Nor could a man born for greatness be moved by such vanities.*
*So that this wisdom of* SOCRATES *might be made plain,*
*The Pythian priestess, bringing forth fates from the golden cauldron,*
*Wished to give this testimony to his virtue:*
Of all men, Socrates is the wisest. <note>original Greek: Ἀνδρῶν ἁπάντων Σωκράτης σοφώτατος</note>
*What then, when the wicked Anytus and Meletus,*
*And the rabid Lycon, architects of frauds,*
*Blew together their lies against the innocent man?*

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>The catchword "TABV-" suggests the following page begins with a table or a word like "Tabula." This page concludes a poetic tribute to Socrates and the scholar Chiflet.</meta>

What happened when the judges delivered their sentence?
When the cold hemlock <note>A poisonous plant used in ancient Greek executions; Socrates was famously forced to drink it.</note> settled in his vitals?
The bronze-like composure of his **MIND** persisted,
And he laughed amidst the dark clouds of death.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;After the fates, virtue shines forth with a greater light;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Late-arriving truth conquers calamity.
Therefore, repentance took hold of the Senate,
And they established a monument to his sacred **NAME**,
A statue carved by the hand of Lysippus; <note>A famous Greek sculptor of the 4th century BCE.</note>
But this, the lapse of great time has consumed.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But you, CHIFLET, <note>Jean-Jacques Chiflet (1588–1660), a physician and antiquarian.</note> light of the Sequanian people, <note>Referring to the Franche-Comté region of France.</note>
And further, the illustrious glory of our Belgium,
Behold, you establish a monument more lasting than bronze
To the **GREAT VIRTUE OF THE WISE SOCRATES.**
Come then, bring forth the Gems carved with the face of SOCRATES,
And explain all the emblems that lie hidden within:
This is your triumph, and the honor due to you;
Socrates exults, restored by your Gems.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Blessed is the **VIRTUE OF SOCRATES** with you as its herald,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Blessed are the Gems; indeed, blessed is your skill,
CHIFLET, the unrivaled judge of Gems,
And the unrivaled lover of true **WISDOM.**
While you return all these well-known things to the world,
And do not allow these ancient Gems to perish,
With the reciprocating love of the Prince of Sages, <note>original: "Principis Sophi"</note>
WE proclaim you to be a second SOCRATES.

# SECOND PLATE

<note>original: "TABVLA SECVNDA"</note>

->5<-

->6<-

->7<-

->8<-

<note>This plate illustrates four examples of a <term>gryllus|a fantastic composite figure or grotesque head, commonly found on ancient Roman gems, where multiple faces are blended into a single image</term>. These engravings (Figures 5 through 8) demonstrate the ancient fascination with Janus-like duality, often pairing the weathered, bearded face of an elderly man—frequently representing a philosopher or the god Silenus—with the smooth, idealized features of a young woman. 

In Figure 5, the elderly man’s helmet is cleverly shaped like a bird's head, while Figure 6 incorporates smaller, distorted faces into the very base of the bust. Figure 8 is particularly ornate, using the woman’s braided hair to create a decorative border between the two opposing profiles. Such gems were often used as personal seals and were thought to possess protective or "apotropaic" qualities to ward off bad luck.</note>

<summary>The second plate of the collection featuring four numbered engravings of "grylli," or composite heads, which merge elderly and youthful profiles into singular, multifaceted portraits.</summary>

<keywords>Second Plate, gryllus, composite heads, ancient gems, Janus, Roman engravings, grotesque art, Jean-Jacques Chiflet</keywords>

# THIRD PLATE

<note>original: "TABVLA TERTIA"</note>

->9<-

->10<-

->11<-

->12<-

<note>This third plate continues the study of the <term>gryllos|a fantastic composite figure or grotesque head, commonly found on ancient Roman gems, where multiple faces and animal parts are blended into a single image</term>. These four engravings (Figures 9 through 12) illustrate how ancient gem-cutters moved beyond simple human pairings—like the two-faced Roman god <term>Janus|the god of beginnings, gates, transitions, time, duality, and endings, usually depicted with two faces</term>—to create more complex, symbolic hybrids.

In **Figure 9**, a double-headed bust contrasts a front-facing, bearded elderly man with a clean-shaven youth in profile. **Figure 10** adds a zoomorphic element, where the joined heads of a soldier and an elder are topped by a curving swan’s neck. **Figure 11** shows a seamless merger between a bearded man and an eagle, while **Figure 12** features a profile adorned with both a swan's neck and a wing. These combinations often held layered meanings; the eagle might represent power or Jupiter, while the swan could symbolize music, poetry, or the soul's journey.</note>

<summary>The third plate of the series depicting four engraved gems that combine human faces with animal features, specifically swans and eagles, to create complex hybrid figures.</summary>

->16<-

<note>This fourth plate presents a series of highly intricate <term>grylli|fantastical composite heads or figures found on ancient engraved gems, often intended as protective amulets or symbols of various virtues</term>. These engravings (Figures 13 through 16) demonstrate the Roman fascination with blending human and animal forms to create layered meanings.

In **Figure 13**, a bearded man's profile is joined with an elephant's trunk. In the ancient world, the elephant was a symbol of strength, piety, and memory. **Figure 14** shows a "composite" head wearing a helmet adorned with a ram's horn—a symbol of the god Jupiter Ammon—and a palm branch, representing victory and peace.

**Figure 15** is a sophisticated <term>cameo|a piece of jewelry or ornament carved in relief from a material like stone or shell</term> or intaglio showing several faces merged together. An elephant's trunk emerges from the top, this time holding a key, which may symbolize the unlocking of wisdom or access to a religious mystery. Below it, the Greek name Eupatistos is inscribed. Finally, **Figure 16** illustrates a central bearded face surrounded by seven smaller heads, a design choice often meant to represent a collective or to provide comprehensive protection from all directions.</note>

# FIFTH PLATE
<note>original: "TABVLA QVINTA"</note>

->17<-

->18<-

->19<-

->20<-

<note>This fifth plate shifts focus toward the mythological retinue of Bacchus (the Greek Dionysus) and the continued use of composite "Janus-style" heads in ancient gem engraving. These figures represent the wilder side of nature and the transformative power of the divine.

**Figure 17** presents a portrait of a bearded man crowned with vine leaves and clusters of grapes. This is likely a depiction of Silenus, the elderly, wise, and often drunken tutor of the wine god, or perhaps Bacchus himself in his older, "bearded" form. **Figure 18** shows a lean, muscular follower of Bacchus, likely a satyr, captured in a rhythmic stride. He carries a <term>thyrsus|a ritual staff topped with a pine cone and wrapped in ivy, a primary symbol of Dionysian power</term>. Over his shoulder, he wears a <term>nebris|a skin of a fawn, panther, or goat, worn by those participating in Bacchic mysteries</term>.

In **Figure 19**, we see a classic Hellenistic motif: the elderly Silenus leaning on a long staff while interacting with a small, youthful figure—most likely the infant Bacchus. This highlights Silenus's role as a protector and teacher. Finally, **Figure 20** returns to the theme of "grylli" or composite heads. This engraving features a Janus-like head where multiple profiles are merged into one. While Janus traditionally had two faces looking into the past and future, these complex gems often combined male and female, or old and young profiles to represent the <term>totality|the concept that a single deity or spirit encompasses all opposites and stages of life</term>.</note>

<summary>The fifth plate illustrates four engraved gems depicting figures from Dionysian mythology, including Silenus and satyrs, as well as a complex multi-faced composite head.</summary>

# SIXTH PLATE
<note>original: "TABVLA SEXTA"</note>

->21<-

->22<-

->23<-

->24<-

<note>The sixth plate illustrates the Roman fascination with the strange, the intellectual, and the theatrical. The first two figures belong to a category of gems known as <term>grylli|grotesque composite figures popular in Roman gem-cutting, intended to serve as protective charms or to poke fun at various human traits</term>. 

**Figure 21** displays a complex composite creature. It stands on the legs of a bird, but its body is a surreal fusion of a bearded human face in profile, a ram’s head, and a horse’s head. The horse holds a green branch in its mouth while a small bird perches on its neck. Such images were often used as protective amulets, designed to distract the "Evil Eye" with their bizarre and nonsensical appearance.

**Figure 22** features another variation of the <term>gryllus</term>. This creature also possesses bird legs but combines a human head with those of a ram and a rooster. The inclusion of two spears and a <term>caduceus|the winged staff of the messenger god Mercury, often entwined with two snakes</term> suggests a connection to trade, travel, or perhaps a specific military protection.

**Figure 23** shifts toward the portraiture of the intellectual elite. It depicts two bearded male heads in a <term>jugate|an overlapping arrangement where one profile is placed directly in front of the other</term> position. The foremost figure, with his high, balding forehead and thick beard, strongly resembles the traditional iconography of the philosopher Socrates. Owning such a gem allowed a Roman citizen to signal their education and philosophical interests.

Finally, **Figure 24** presents a more complex narrative scene, likely carved into a larger <term>cameo|a gem carved in relief, where the figure stands out from the background</term>. The scene is split by a central column. On the left, a man engages with a veiled woman, while on the right, figures are gathered in a state of reflection or mourning. This may depict a scene from a Greek tragedy or a significant moment of civic or religious life, captured in miniature for a wealthy patron.</note>

<summary>This plate documents the variety of Roman engraved gems, ranging from protective "gryllus" amulets to philosophical portraits and detailed narrative scenes.</summary>
<keywords>gryllus, protective amulets, Socrates, philosophy, Roman gems, caduceus, jugate portraits, narrative cameos</keywords>

# JEAN CHIFLET,
## CANON OF TOURNAI
# SOCRATES,
### OR
# A JUDGMENT
### ON GEMS
# ENGRAVED WITH HIS IMAGE.

<margin>
Book 4.
Tusculan
Disputations.
</margin>
Cicero <note>original: "Tullius"</note> is the authority for the story that Zopyrus—who claimed to discern every person's character from their physical form—once gathered a list of many vices in Socrates while in a public assembly. He was mocked by the others present, who did not recognize those vices in Socrates; however, he was supported by Socrates himself, who said that those vices were indeed present within him, but had been cast out by the use of reason. In treating this same subject—namely, a **Judgment on the image** of the Philosopher whom all Antiquity looked up to—if I more than once interpose a modest conjecture rather than presuming to offer a firm assertion in all matters, I would wish above all for those who delight in these studies, and who value such knowledge so highly, to remember this: *in Riddles or Enigmas (of which there are many here), praise for intellect belongs to him who conjectures or divines acutely and appropriately, not only to him who never wanders, is deceived, or deceives.* <note>The author is setting a humble tone for his physiognomic and iconographic analysis of Socrates.</note>

<summary>Jean Chiflet introduces his study of Socratic engraved gems by citing Cicero's anecdote about Zopyrus, arguing that Socrates' noble character was a triumph of reason over his naturally "vicious" physical appearance.</summary>
<keywords>Jean Chiflet, Socrates, Zopyrus, Cicero, Tusculan Disputations, engraved gems, physiognomy, reason vs. nature</keywords>

--- Page 220 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Jean Chiflet’s study of Socrates, focusing on the physical representation of philosophers in antiquity and the scholarly defense of their reputations.</meta>

# THE SOCRATES OF JEAN CHIFLET

<margin>Commentary on the Gold Coins of the Duke of Aarschot.</margin>
...might deceive. With these words, the most learned Jean Hemelarius <note>A 17th-century Flemish numismatist and canon at Antwerp Cathedral</note>, a member of our Order and Canon of Antwerp, requested that the same indulgence be granted to him in the explanation of ancient coins.

It was so common among the ancients to carry the likenesses and faces of philosophers engraved on gems in their rings that they even regarded them as amulets, through which they believed themselves to be happy and fortunate. This custom was adopted by Muretus <note>Marc-Antoine Muret (1526–1585), a renowned French humanist scholar</note>, a man most eloquent by universal 
<margin>Lipsius, *On the Right Pronunciation of the Latin Language*.</margin>
consent, who loved SOCRATES so much that he wore him on a ring and sealed documents with his ancient image. Just as ZENO was worn by the Stoics, PLATO by the Academics, ARISTOTEL by the Peripatetics on their signet rings, and EPICURUS by almost everyone else, *believing this to be a favorable omen for their family and name*. Such was the fame of that 
<margin>Alexander ab Alexandro, Book 2, Chapter 19.</margin>
great man <note>referring to Epicurus</note>, though he might have seemed to have fallen from it, had not Pierre Gassendi <note>A French philosopher and scientist (1592–1655) who sought to reconcile Epicureanism with Christianity</note>, Provost of the Church of Digne, shown in a most learned commentary of eight books that he had taught holy and upright things. He showed that he was not of *distended skin* <note>original: "cute distenta," implying a bloated or gluttonous appearance</note>, as 
<margin>Book 9, Epistle 9.</margin>
Sidonius Apollinaris depicted him, but of such innocence, purity, and austerity of life that no other philosopher surpassed him; and of such sharpness of wit and maturity of judgment that he does not easily yield to others. In short, he was a master of leisure, but of an honorable kind.

As for SOCRATES, the Prince of Ethical Philosophy, although images of him of every kind are found everywhere, and Plinius <note>Pliny the Elder, Roman naturalist</note> considers that there is no greater 
<margin>Book 34, Chapter 2.</margin>
sign of success than that everyone always desires to know WHAT SORT of person someone was; nevertheless, WHAT SORT of person SOCRATES *is*, says 
<margin>In the *Symposium*.</margin>
Plato, *and what his marvelous manner of speaking is like, almost no one approaches his likeness, neither of the ancients nor of those who exist now, except as I have described and compared. Indeed,* TO NO MAN...

<summary>This page discusses the ancient tradition of wearing philosopher's portraits on rings as lucky charms and highlights the scholarly efforts of figures like Gassendi to rehabilitate the moral reputation of Epicurus. It introduces Socrates as the supreme figure of ethical philosophy whose true nature remains unique and difficult to capture.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Epicurus, signet rings, amulets, Jean Hemelarius, Pierre Gassendi, ancient portraiture, ethical philosophy, numismatics</keywords>

--- Page 221 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory discourse on the character of Socrates, transitioning from his physical appearance to the hidden depth of his philosophy and the description of engraved gems.</meta>

# THE SOCRATES OF JEAN CHIFFLET

<sig>A 2</sig>

He is not only like the Sileni <note>Silenus was the tutor of Bacchus, often depicted as a snub-nosed, elderly satyr who hid great wisdom behind a grotesque exterior</note>, but he himself and his discourses are like the Satyrs. For I had previously overlooked this point: that his speeches are very much like **OPEN SILENI**. If anyone were to hear his discourses, they seem at first glance, as they say, excessively absurd. Indeed, the names and words, in their outward appearance, take on the character of some insulting Satyr. For he always has on his lips stories of donkeys, blacksmiths, shoemakers, and tanners, and he always seems to say the same things about the same people, so that almost everyone who is less experienced or skilled laughs at his words. But if one looks inside his discourses, he will first find that they alone, out of all others, possess a depth of meaning; moreover, they are most divine, containing many beautiful images of the virtues, and extending to so many things—indeed, they encompass all the precepts by which a person who intends to live honestly and well ought to be instructed.

It is certainly just as Plato says: and, if I may speak my mind, it seems that nothing can be found on the shelves of Antiquarians <note>term: Antiquariorum; refers to collectors and scholars of ancient artifacts</note> more obscure, or more illustrious, than those Gems. Handling them with admiration, and exhausted by a long examination, I discovered that they all relate to the image of Socrates, and to his life and character. In explaining these, nothing pleased me as much as brevity; for the materials brought forward to illustrate them should be carried in the hand, not in a full cloak, lest these precious and traditional faces produce boredom by lingering too long. I call them precious, especially if you consider the "sweet madness" of the ancients, among whom the likeness of a man on a Gem, however small, sometimes surpassed the price of living, breathing men. They would be worthy of praise if they had held the middle ground, and if the valuation of such luxuries had not been so great.

<margin>
GEM I.
Pliny, Book 37, chapter 5.
</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Socrates' physical representation in antiquity, specifically focusing on the symbolic and medicinal use of emeralds engraved with his likeness.</meta>

# THE SOCRATES OF JEAN CHIFFLET

...we gaze greedily upon the branches and leaves. Indeed, we look upon emeralds <note>original: "Smaragdos"</note> all the more willingly because nothing else grows green with a deeper hue than they. Furthermore, they are the only gems that fill the eyes with their sight without ever causing satiety. Even when the vision is dimmed by other intense efforts, the sharpness of the sight is restored by looking at an emerald; for those who engrave gems, there is no more pleasant refreshment for the eyes: so much do they soothe weariness with their green gentleness. And so, people used to wear the image of Socrates carved from emerald not merely for the sake of ornament, nor only for the purpose of a seal, but also for their own instruction and for the remedies of both mind and body, so that by gazing upon it they might fill not only their eyes, but their soul. MARCUS ANTONINUS <note>The Roman Emperor Marcus Aurelius</note> himself applied the value of the emerald <margin>Book 7, *Meditations*</margin> to moral character: *Whatever others may say or do,* he says, *it is necessary for me to be good: just as if gold, or an <term>EMERALD</term> <note>Smaragdus: a term used in antiquity for various green stones, most notably the emerald.</note>, or purple cloth were always to say: "Whatever others say or do, I must be an <term>EMERALD</term> <margin>Book 4.</margin> and preserve my own color."* He said previously: *Does an <term>EMERALD</term> lose any of its goodness if it is not praised?* Then elsewhere concerning Socrates: *Alexander, Gaius, and Pompey— <margin>Book 8.</margin> what are they compared to Diogenes, Heraclitus, or <term>SOCRATES</term>? For these men knew the nature of things, their causes, and their materials; these were their principalities and kingdoms. But in the case of the former, there was as much slavery as there was ignorance.* Epictetus says in his *Enchiridion* <note>A "Handbook" of Stoic ethical advice.</note>: *When you are to have some business with someone, especially one of the high-ranking officials, propose to yourself what <term>SOCRATES</term> or Zeno would have done in that situation. Thus it will happen that reason will not fail you in managing whatever has been put before you in the proper way.*

<margin>GEMS II. III.</margin> Moreover, these very gems, and those that follow, represent Socrates in that form described by Plato and Xenophon in the *Symposium*: resembling Silenus <note>A tutor of Dionysus, often depicted as a snub-nosed, balding satyr-like figure.</note>, bald, with a lumpy forehead, a flat nose, a bull-like gaze, protruding eyes, and prominent eyebrows—the sort of men called "Silones" after Silenus, who was hairy—

# JEAN CHIFFLET’S SOCRATES

He was depicted with shaggy eyebrows: for regarding the **IMAGE OF SOCRATES** <note>original: "IMAGINEM SOCRATIS"</note>, unless the sculpted likeness shows the baldness, the snub nose, and the prominent eyes of **SOCRATES**, it should by no means be called his portrait.

<margin>Hugh Eteriano, *On Heresies*, Book 3, chapter 18.</margin>
Synesius writes: That son of Sophronicus, **SOCRATES**, though otherwise thinking modestly of himself and being the most sparing of all men in praising his own gifts, could least of all restrain himself from boasting about his resemblance **TO SILENUS** <note>Silenus was a tutor to Dionysus, typically depicted as a snub-nosed, pot-bellied, and bald satyr-like figure.</note>.

<margin>In the *Praise of Baldness*.</margin>
He certainly used to mock his wives <note>Referring to the tradition that Socrates had two wives, Xanthippe and Myrto.</note> when they were quarreling with one another in a confused state, telling them to abstain from such wickedness and insults; he said he was not worthy of them disputing over him, as he was a man so foul and deformed, with a snub nose and a receding, bald forehead.

<margin>St. Jerome, *Against Vigilantius*.</margin>
And there is that foolish questioner in Athenaeus who asks: *Is anyone more snub-nosed than SOCRATES?* Although these remarks are of the kind that possess more wit and less bitterness—as when you speak against someone’s baldness, Macrobius says, *or against a nose, whether it be a curved protrusion or a SOCRATIC depression* <note>original: "SOCRATICAM depreffionem"</note>. For the less these things are a true misfortune, the lighter the pain they cause.

<margin>Book 5.</margin>
In **VATINIUS** <note>Publius Vatinius, a Roman politician often mocked for his physical deformities.</note>, the deformity of the body vied with the baseness of his character, so that his soul seemed to be enclosed in a most fitting dwelling.

<margin>Velleius Paterculus, Book 2, chapter 69.</margin>
On the other hand, **ANTIGONUS** had the brow of **SILENUS** but the best of characters; yet his grandson **ALEXANDER**, though he was of a beauty more majestic than a human's, nevertheless hurled a spear at his own guests at a banquet, and of two friends, he threw one to a wild beast and the other to himself <note>Likely referring to Alexander the Great's outbursts of rage, contrasting his beauty with his inner turmoil.</note>.

<margin>Seneca, Book 3, *On Anger*, chapter 22.</margin>
It is so true that the face often lies, as the common proverb says and as Socrates himself teaches. He is depicted by Sidonius with falling hair on account of his baldness; and he himself used to relate that it seemed to him in a dream while sleeping that Plato was changed into a Crow, which, hopping upon his head, scratched his **SMOOTH BALD SCALP** <note>original: "GLABRAM CALVAM"</note> with its beak.

<margin>Book 9, Letter 9.</margin>
Of all the philosophers who ever existed, he calls him the **BALDEST** and the *mildest*.

<margin>In his *Praise of Baldness*.</margin>
Synesius <note>A 4th-century Greek bishop and philosopher</note>, although he argues in favor of baldness and decrees it to be a mark of wisdom, nevertheless most learnedly proves that almost all the ancients held a different opinion.

<margin>In the book *Against the Blow of Calumny*, section 2, series 3, chapter 15.</margin>
Theophilus Raynaud <note>A 17th-century Jesuit theologian</note> notes that baldness—due to the head being uncovered and the brain being more easily compromised—was taken as evidence of a disturbed mind. Indeed, as the same author noted, Nonius Marcellus <note>A 4th-century grammarian</note> extracted from the laws of the Twelve Tables <note>The foundation of Roman law</note> that fools were called *The Bald*; and Persius, dealing not with a man whose hair was merely missing, but with one who was dull and fatuous, says:

<margin>Satire 1.</margin>
> ——— *you are talking nonsense, you* BALD MAN,
> *when your fat paunch sticks out a foot and a half.*

In Petronius: *Eumolpus himself, loosened by wine, wished to hurl insults at the* BALD. From this came so many witty remarks and taunts against the baldness of Socrates; and so that these might pass into late posterity, they were expressed not only in books and letters, but also on Gems, which clearly agree with them. It was as if a smooth head detracted something from the shaggy and hairy wisdom of the soul, for which he is so greatly celebrated by Plato. But

<margin>Marsilio Ficino’s *Commentary on Plato*.</margin>
for a man from whom nothing but the great and incomparable proceeded, his severity of life and frequent correction of others' crimes rendered many great men hostile to him (as truth is wont to do). These included especially Anytus, Meletus, and Lycon, most powerful citizens in the Republic; Thrasymachus, Polus, and Callias, the Orators; and furthermore Aristophanes the comic poet, his most bitter pursuer. The Gems also bear witness to this, as I shall soon discuss.

<margin>Virgil, *Aeneid* Book 6.</margin>
> *On his chin — a mass*
> *of unkempt gray hair lies.*

This was characteristic of the Philosophers, even those who displayed a zeal for virtue in their dress alone, and in the gar-

They placed wisdom in a heavy beard, as Arnobius <note>Arnobius the Elder, a 4th-century Christian apologist who often critiqued pagan philosophical customs.</note> notes. Whence Juvenal, speaking of them, says:
<margin>Satire 14.</margin>
> *Though you should bring a thousand* **bearded masters** *from there.*

And Persius:
> — *Believe that this* **bearded master**
> *is speaking.*

He is covered by a cheap little cloak, the poverty of which the same Juvenal commends, not without sarcasm toward the Athenians:
<margin>Satire 7.</margin>
> — *And you, Athens, saw this man in want,*
> *daring to contribute nothing except cold hemlock.* <note>A reference to the poison Socrates was forced to drink at his execution.</note>

Come now, says Marsilio Ficino <note>A celebrated Italian humanist philosopher of the 15th century.</note>, place the person of **Socrates** before your eyes: *You will see a lean, withered, and squalid man: a man naturally melancholic, as it is said, and hairy; thinned by fasting, dirty from neglect; moreover naked, that is, covered by a simple and old little cloak.* Finally, to conclude with **Marcus Antoninus** <note>The Roman Emperor Marcus Aurelius.</note> in as few words as possible: *When you see a* **Satyr**, *imagine that the sight of* **Socrates** *is given to you.*
<margin>Book 10, Meditations.</margin>

For this reason, Johannes Faber <note>A 17th-century physician and scholar of antiquities.</note> rightly did not doubt that many ring-gems, in which the head of **Silenus** and a **Faun** are seen together, represented the images of **Socrates** and **Alcibiades** <note>Alcibiades was a prominent Athenian statesman and student of Socrates; the comparison to Silenus refers to Socrates' snub-nosed, satyr-like features.</note>. In most of them, the likeness of the face is not the same; but allowance must be made for the diligence of the craftsmen, for whom it was enough to have carved the Socratic baldness and snubbed nose, though not always with one and the same line.
<margin>Gem IV. On the images of Illustrious Men by Fulvio Orsini.</margin>

When these features are found on gems in place of the helmet of Pallas <note>The goddess Athena.</note>, they are not mere ornaments or "Grylli" <note>A type of grotesque or caricature in ancient art.</note>—which was a ridiculous kind of painting, according to Antiphilus. Indeed, on the coins of the Athenians, one may see everywhere the helmeted head of Pallas, because that city, by the judgment of the gods (as Apollodorus is the authority) or of Cecrops (as others assert), was consecrated to Pallas...
<margin>Gem V. Pliny book 35, ch. 10. Ludovicus Nonnius on the Greece of Goltzius.</margin>

...it was, and named Athena in honor of the goddess. Nor was this in vain, for no city has become equally famous for the arts of both Peace and War. Furthermore, and what is most relevant to our subject, Pierre Séguin—the Superior of Saint-Germain in Paris <note>original: "Lutetiæ"</note> and Abbot of Femy in the territory of Guise, Diocese of Cambrai—a most refined man, *well known for his understanding of ancient coins and the choice abundance of them which he possesses* (a praise for which, to say nothing of others, he is deservedly honored by Jacques Sirmond in his *Anti-Tristan*), when he had come to Tournai <note>original: "Tornacum"</note> once or twice last year, deigned to share with me an ancient Sardonyx, upon which is the head of Socrates with the inscription ATHENA <note>original: "ΑΘΗΝΗ"</note>. 

This makes it clear that while the Athenians were ungrateful while Socrates was among the living, after his death—their anger having turned, as often happens, into repentance—they so gloried in the education and origin of such a citizen that they adopted him as a Tutelary Spirit <note>The "Genius Tutelaris" was a guardian spirit or protective deity of a person or place.</note> and placed him upon the Helmet of the Presiding Goddess. This was done to indicate the man's lofty character, which could be broken by none of their insults, and could succumb to no sarcasms or mockeries. For he was of an outstanding spirit and of invincible conviction; with great sublimity of soul, he despised those who plucked at or rebuked him. He was brave and constant everywhere, *a victor over himself, which is the greatest victory of all; by twisting himself against the movement of his emotions, he preserved himself without a fall and remained unconquered.* And through that contempt for all praise and for himself, he achieved the status of being judged the wisest, not merely by human eulogy, but even by the voice of the Oracle itself:

<margin>Plutarch, *On the Control of Anger*.</margin>

> Of all men, Socrates is the wisest. <note>original Greek: "Ἀνδρῶν ἁπάντων Σωκράτης σοφώτατος."</note>
> *Of mortals, Socrates alone is truly wise.*

<margin>GEM VI.</margin>

It was indeed quite a sight how, after Meletus <note>One of the three primary accusers of Socrates during his trial in 399 BCE</note> and the slanders he fabricated, Anytus followed, delivering a long and malicious speech; and then, after him, Lycon spared no trickery to ensure that one of you <note>Referring to the jury or the Athenian citizens</note> should die. But, as Seneca so excellently put it: *He who raises himself up against the most cruel misfortunes, and overcomes those evils by which others are crushed, holds those very miseries in the place of* SACRED HEADBANDS <note>original: "INFULARVM". The infula was a woollen bandage worn by priests and victims of sacrifice; Seneca suggests that for the wise man, suffering becomes a badge of honor or sanctity.</note>. *For we are so constituted that nothing occupies our admiration quite as much as a man who is bravely miserable.*
<margin>Book on Consolation, addressed to Helvia.</margin>

Those things are indeed great and worthy of admiration, but Cato the Elder used to say that Socrates seemed more wonderful for nothing else than the fact that, having found a difficult wife, he nevertheless treated her with kindness and humanity. *As far as* XANTHIPPE *is concerned*, Synesius exclaims, *oh, what contempt! How she used to treat* SOCRATES! *Yet none of those things prevented him from living most joyfully in the midst of that contempt.* On an Onyx stone belonging to the Englishman Thomas Trevor—a cast of which was sent to me some time ago by Jean Michael Ritus, a noble painter and a most studious expert in these Antiquities—I remember observing the joined heads of an EAGLE, of SOCRATES, and of XANTHIPPE herself, with the addition of the small vessel with which, after first attacking her husband with insults, she drenched him with a foul flood of liquid. A deed which he himself parried with a joke: namely, that when Xanthippe thundered, she was bound to pour out rain as well.
<margin>GEMS 7, 8, 9.</margin>
<margin>In Dion.</margin>
<margin>Rader, Part 2 of the Garden of Saints.</margin>

When Alcibiades <note>A prominent Athenian statesman and student of Socrates</note> said that such a peevish wife was intolerable, Socrates replied: "And yet, I have long since become as accustomed to these things as if I were constantly hearing the sound of pulleys. Do you not tolerate the noise of cackling GEESE?" When Alcibiades replied, "But they produce eggs and chicks for me," Socrates said, "And Xanthippe bears me sons." Hence Socrates appears on a Gem with the head...
<margin>GEM 10.</margin>
<margin>Laërtius in his Life of Socrates.</margin>

<margin>*Oration for Sextus Roscius.*</margin>
...with the head of a Goose, and his wife Xanthippe, who appears to be making a loud noise with her mouth wide open. As you may find in the writings of Tully <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero</note>, the "Goose" is used to describe an accuser *who merely makes a din, yet causes no real harm.*

<margin>GEMS 11 & 12. *Quintus Curtius book 4; Plutarch's book On the Cleverness of Animals; and Cicero book 1 of the Tusculan Disputations; In the Life of Dion.*</margin>
Socrates is also seen alone with the beak of an eagle, which is undoubtedly a *favorable omen of victory*, and sometimes even with the image of a swan placed upon his head. Regarding this, Plutarch says: *A significant and noble part of divination, both great and ancient, is the* OMEN OF BIRDS. *Therefore, in a general sense, Euripides called* BIRDS *the* HERALDS *of the gods; but specifically,* SOCRATES *made himself a* FELLOW-SERVANT OF SWANS. And Synesius applied the symbols of the Eagle and the Swan to Eloquence and Philosophy: *For the* EAGLE *and the* SWAN *to be together, and to possess the* GIFTS *of both, is denied to birds by nature, but permitted to man by God: to whomever it is granted both to excel in* ELOQUENCE OF TONGUE *and to be a master of* PHILOSOPHY. Socrates himself, when he had seen in a dream a swan's chick grow feathers in his lap, which immediately took flight into the heights on sprouted wings and gave forth the sweetest songs; the next day, when PLATO was introduced to him by his father, he said that this was the SWAN he had seen.

<margin>Diogenes Laërtius *in the Life of Plato.*</margin>
<margin>GEMS 13, 14, & 15. *Pierio Valeriano, Preface to book 2 of Hieroglyphics; Pliny book 8, chapter 1.*</margin>
Greater difficulty is presented by the addition of an elephant's head with its trunk, a palm branch, a caduceus <note>The staff of Mercury, symbol of peace and commerce</note>, and the heads of ANYTUS, MELETUS <note>Socrates' legal accusers</note>, and XANTHIPPE herself; unless we say that the elephant compensates for its deformity with excellent talents, and excels in this: that by its own nature it offers lessons in both character and virtue, so that men have been compelled to take and imitate many things from it, as if from an ideal of morality. *In that animal,* says Pliny, *are found honesty, prudence, and equity—qualities rare even in man.* All of these apply most beautifully to Socrates, by whom, as much as—

# THE SOCRATES OF JEAN CHIFFLET
<margin>Laërtius in his Life of Socrates.</margin>
...though he was of deformed appearance, Ethics took its beginning from him. He was accustomed to urge young men to gaze at themselves frequently in a *mirror*, so that if they were beautiful, they might become worthy of that appearance; but if they were deformed, they might cover their ugliness with learning. Nor would I believe without some hidden meaning <note>original: "sine mysterio"</note> that the latticed skin on the head of the elephant was so accurately depicted; namely, because Socrates compared those who envy the virtue of others to flies. For he says in Seneca: <margin>Book on the Happy Life.</margin> *Do you have leisure to scrutinize the evils of others and pass judgment on anyone? Why does this Philosopher live so spaciously? Why does he dine so sumptuously? You observe the pimples of others—which is the habit of* <term>FLIES</term> <note>The author compares petty critics to flies that seek out sores rather than healthy skin.</note>—*while you yourselves are covered in ulcers.* Pliny indeed says of elephants: <margin>Book 8, chapter 13.</margin> *There is no covering of bristles, nor even protection on the tail for driving away the annoyance of* <term>FLIES</term> (*for even such a vast creature feels them*), *but rather a* <term>LATTICED SKIN</term>, *which invites that kind of animal by its scent. Therefore, when they have received the swarms into the extended folds, they suddenly crush them by tightening the* <term>LATTICES</term> <note>original: "cancellis," referring to the cross-hatched wrinkles of the elephant's hide.</note> *into wrinkles. This serves them in place of a tail, mane, or shaggy hair.* Socrates says of himself in the passage above: *My virtue was made illustrious by the very things through which it was attacked. For it is expedient for virtue to be brought forth and tested; none understand how great it is more than those who have felt its strength by provoking it.* Hence also the palm branch in the hand of the elephant, signifying victory over the irritability of <term>XANTHIPPE</term> <note>Socrates' wife, famous in history for her difficult temper.</note> and the slanders of <term>ANYTUS</term> and <term>MELITUS</term> <note>The primary accusers of Socrates at his trial.</note>. Plato himself, being of a more relaxed virtue and spirit, feared to be accused by these same men; for Justin Martyr relates that he did not dare to confess the true teaching concerning God, lest the same thing happen to him <margin>In his Exhortation to the Greeks.</margin> as happened to Socrates. *But* <term>PLATO</term>, he says, *having received, as it seems, the teaching of* <term>MOSES</term> *and the other Prophets concerning the* <term>ONE AND ONLY GOD</term>, *which he had learned in Egypt, feared—on account of those things which had happened to* <term>SOCRATES</term>—*lest he too should rouse some* <term>ANYTUS</term> *or* <term>MELITUS</term> *against himself among the Athenians, accusing him and saying:* <term>PLATO</term> *acts unjustly, and is over-curious* <note>The text breaks off here; the catchword "riosa" indicates the next page begins with "curiosa."</note>

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of an elephant's "latticed" skin to illustrate how Socrates dealt with his critics and accusers. It also contrasts Socrates' courage with Plato's supposed hesitation to speak the full truth about monotheism for fear of execution.</summary>

he treats of curious matters, and by not accepting the gods which the city receives, and out of **FEAR OF THE HEMLOCK** <note>The poison used in Socrates' execution</note> he established a certain varied and disguised discourse concerning the gods.

Furthermore, there is added to the Socratic Gem a caduceus <note>The staff of Hermes, symbol of messengers and trade</note> with the inscription, *EYELPISTOU* <note>original Greek: ΕΥΕΛΠΙΣΤΟΥ</note>. That is, **CONFIDENTLY**, or <margin>Libanius, Declamation 16.</margin> **WITH TRUST, IN GOOD FAITH, WITHOUT DECEIT.** *For it becomes good and brave men to cling to high hope, and to accept plainly whatever shall seem best to God.* <note>Translated from the Greek: Πρέπει δὲ τοῖς ἀγαθοῖς ἀνδράσι...</note> Which equanimity Socrates, if anyone else, seems to have attained, employing these very words in Plato shortly before his death: *But now it is the hour to depart, for me indeed that I may die, but for you that you may live; which of us, moreover, goes toward the better thing, is uncertain to all, except to God.* <note>Translated from the Greek: Ἀλλὰ γὰρ ἤδη ὥρα ἀπιέναι...</note> The same man, as Synesius witnesses, <margin>In Dion.</margin> *professed that he complied with the will of God, and said that He was not to be* **DISTRUSTED**. Hence Seneca says: *If it pleases you even to live among the Greeks, dwell with* **SOCRATES**, *with* **ZENO**; *the one will teach you how to die if it is necessary, the other how to die before it is necessary.*

Perhaps with greater leisure someone might elicit from these Gems what those words mean which Nonius Marcellus cites from Varro, resembling a riddle: *That he had looked around himself, and found, when he had begun to sleep, as smooth as* **SOCRATES**, *that his bald head had become a hedgehog of white hairs with a* **PROBOSCIS**. <margin>Book 24, Various Readings, ch. 1.</margin> In which most corrupt passage, Petrus Victorius <note>A 16th-century Italian philologist</note> says that one ought to read *calvam* [bald head] instead of *galbam*: and that Varro is speaking of someone whose appearance had been greatly changed in sleep, and whereas he had been bald before he went to sleep, afterward having awakened he found himself (a thing which deservedly caused him great wonder...

<margin>From the *Adages*, Thousand 5, Century 1.</margin>
...brought great [fame]) long-haired. Erasmus, in his *Adages*, reads the phrase as: *As smooth as the callus of Socrates.* He was, Erasmus says, a man of the most enduring body, which had become hardened <note>The Latin *occalluerat* implies skin toughened like a callus from exposure.</note> to every labor; he used to walk barefoot and sleep on the ground. Hence the joke regarding THE CALLUS OF SOCRATES. I am not unaware of what they tell concerning the plucked rooster, which Diogenes—unless I am mistaken—had sent into the school of Plato. But it is more pleasing that we read it as *callus* [hardened skin], for the transition from the letter 'C' to its relative 'G' is easy <note>The author is debating a philological point: whether the original Greek referred to a *gallus* (rooster) or *callus* (callous skin).</note>. These are their views, but neither is fully satisfying. Whatever the case, it was the custom of the Athenians to propose Enigmas of this kind not only on Engraved Gems, but also on Coins, in which one may see in the work of Goltzius <note>Hubert Goltzius (1526–1583), a famous numismatist and printer.</note>, in very small form at the side of an owl, an ELEPHANT, a CAMEL, the head (as it seems) of a RAM, and a CICADA. 

<margin>\* Preface to the *Book on Dreams*. Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, Preface to the *Heptaplus*. Reuchlin, Book 2 of *On the Cabalistic Art*.</margin>
\* Synesius teaches that it was also a Platonic practice to conceal, under the appearance of a cheaper or lowlier subject, those things in Philosophy which are great and excellent. However, this same practice was observed earlier by the Egyptians; those Sphinxes in front of their temples suggested as much, and Pythagoras is believed to have been the first to transfer that method of teaching from them to the Greeks.

<margin>GEM XVI. Aelian, Book 2 of *Various History*. Laërtius, *Life of Socrates*. In *The Reminiscent*.</margin>
Masks on Gems under the image of SOCRATES, like an Old Man of Comedy led onto the stage and torn apart by the insults of Aristophanes. *The common people*, says Lucian, *are for the most part of such a nature that when a matter is conducted with mockery and slander, they especially rejoice, particularly when those things which seem most perfect are seized upon by biting wit*, just as they once rejoiced when Aristophanes and Eupolis, for the sake of derision, introduced SOCRATES onto the stage and devised certain absurd comedies about him. Such is the playfulness and levity of the judgments of the common folk. But when he was being carried about <note>Likely referring to his depiction in the play.</note> and frequently named, having been publicly mocked by the Comic Poet in *The Clouds* <note>original: "ἐν νεφέλαις" (en nephelais)</note>, and when foreigners were asking who that SOCRATES was, he himself stood up,

<margin>* Rader, Part 2 of the *Garden of Saints*.</margin>
...and he voluntarily offered himself to be looked at and mocked by everyone. When warned by his friends not to attend the spectacle <note>referring to the performance of Aristophanes' play *The Clouds*, which lampooned him</note>, he said he would go: for either false things would be charged against him, or true ones; if false, they were to be despised; if true, they were to be corrected. * Truly a noble spirit in a pagan man, which you might rarely find even in a Christian. But to some, he seemed to have caused offense in other matters, and could in no way be counted among the number of just men. Even * Juvenal <margin>Theophilus Raynaud, Book 1 on Virtues, Section 1, Chapter 2. On Moral Discipline, Distinction 3, Question 2, Article 1. * Satire 2.</margin> criticizes the "Socratic sodomites," or soft philosophers, who were secretly devoted to foul lusts. However, there is no shortage of very serious writers <margin>Bessarion, Budé, Pierre Geoffroy, Pierre Halloix, Marsilio Ficino, and others.</margin> who maintain that this shameful filth was pinned on an innocent man through slander, and that he was led into the Areopagus <note>The high court of Athens</note> accused of the same crime as Paul the Apostle, as Saint Justin, philosopher and martyr, testifies. For he writes thus about him in his *Apology to the Senate*, after the middle: <term>Socrates was accused of the same things as we are. For they said that he introduced new demons, and that he did not believe in those whom the city considers to be gods.</term> <note>original Greek: Σωκράτης τὰ αὐτὰ ἡμῖν ἐνεκλήθη...; Chifflet provides both the Greek and a Latin translation, here rendered into English.</note> And a little later, he writes that the same Socrates encouraged his fellow citizens to the knowledge of the UNKNOWN GOD, writing thus: <term>He encouraged them to the knowledge of the God unknown to them through the investigation of reason, saying: It is neither easy to find the Father and Creator of all things, nor, once found, is it safe to announce Him to everyone.</term> <note>original Greek: Πρὸς Θεοῦ τοῦ ἀγνώστου...; Chifflet cites Justin Martyr's interpretation of Socrates as a precursor to Christian monotheism.</note> And soon after he adds that CHRIST was known in part by Socrates in those words: <term>By Christ, who was known in part even by Socrates.</term> <note>original Greek: Χριστοῦ δὲ τοῦ καὶ ὑπὸ Σωκράτους ἀπὸ μέρους γνωσθέντι.</note> Which he says perhaps for this reason: because Socrates seems to have seen the one God, at least in a clouded way...

...recognized him, and thus <term>CHRIST</term> <note>original: "CHRISTVM"</note>, insofar as he is <term>GOD</term>. This was the opinion of <margin>Volume 1 and another of the illustrious writers of the Eastern Church.</margin> Pierre Halloix, the commentator on Justin Martyr, who also examines this question: *Was <term>SOCRATES</term> imbued with such faith concerning <term>GOD</term> and <term>CHRIST</term> that his salvation might be hoped for?* After bringing forward many arguments for both sides, he concludes that the affirmative is not entirely improbable. However, Girolamo Aleandro the Younger, in his booklet on the Creed of the Church from an ancient gem, considers it nearer to the truth that those ethnic <note>pagan</note> philosophers were blind to the light of divine truth; if they dug any gold from the mines of heavenly wisdom, they turned it into dross by adding the vainest opinions, just as heretics do today with the Holy Scriptures.

Otherwise, it would truly be a marvel to see Socrates in the manner of drunkards and banqueters, "wreathing his temples with the green vine-shoot," as he appears on the Onyx of the late <gloss>original: "τῷ μακαρίτῃ"</gloss> Albert Rubens <margin>GEM XVII. Horace, Book 3 of Odes, Ode 25.</margin>: a marvel, I say, to see him in this attire, with his severity of life defeated—he who, even when hungry, never sought to flatter; who, according to Laërtius, *lived so sparingly and temperately that when the plague frequently devastated Athens, he alone never fell ill; who, finally, said that he differed from other men in this: that others live to eat, but he ate to live.* Yet, if we believe Hermippus in the writings of Athenaeus: *While he indulged in constant drinking bouts even unto drunkenness, he lost Acrocorinth—which had been entrusted to him by King Antigone—along with the city <margin>Book 4.</margin> of Corinth itself.* And in the same author: *Plato's <term>SOCRATES</term> does not wish to leave the banquet, even though Eryximachus, Phaedrus, and certain others have already departed; instead, he remains awake with <margin>Book 5.</margin> Agathon and Aristophanes, and drinks from a silver goblet (for one might rightly call them "huge cups"). The same man later drinks skillfully again from a shallow bowl. Of those who were keeping watch, two later began to nod off, though sleep [claimed] Aristophanes first...*

...sleep overcame them; but when it was already dawning, SOCRATES, who had lulled the others to sleep, rose with Agathon and departed for the Lyceum <note>A public gymnasium in Athens where Socrates often taught</note>. Regarding this, Timon says of the same Socrates in the works of Libanius: "Someone DRINKS for whole days: *the man, pretending to philosophize and dressed in a cloak, supports a playful youth and* IS CROWNED." Lucian, in *The Double Indictment*, where he complains about the multitude of Philosophers, says: "*Everywhere there is a* LONG BEARD, *and a* LITTLE BOOK *in the left hand.*"

*Laërtius in his Life.*
