He also danced quite often, judging that exercise to be very conducive to maintaining good health, just as Xenophon also testifies in the *Symposium*. And Plutarch, in *On Preserving Health*, says: *To* SOCRATES, *the bodily exercise of* DANCING *was not unpleasant.* Likewise Lucian: "*SOCRATES himself, the wisest of all men—if faith is to be placed in the Pythian oracle honoring him with this testimony—not only praised* DANCING *with honors, but was also not ashamed or reluctant to learn it, attributing much to its charming harmony and elegance, as well as to fit movement and the form of the one moving. Nor was he afraid, though already* OLDER IN AGE, *to hold dancing among the serious disciplines.*" There is a head of a dog at the back <note>Referring to the image on the engraved gem</note>, by which Socrates used to swear in the *Phaedrus*, as if it were an image of remembrance, when he had committed the speech of Lysias to memory. In the *Phaedo*, however, he likewise swore BY THE DOG, as he thought that faith and obedience should be offered to those who manage the reins of affairs in cities.

<margin>
Pierius, Book 5 of the Hieroglyphics.
</margin>

<margin>
GEM XIX.
*Valerius Maximus, Book 8, Chapter 8.*
</margin>

Leisure, which seems contrary to industry and especially to study, ought to be briefly subjoined: not that by which virtue vanishes, but that by which it is refreshed. For the former must be avoided even by the idle; the latter must sometimes be sought even by the strenuous.

...to those, so that they do not lead a weak life; to these, so that by a timely interruption of labor, they may become more vigorous for working. Seneca, in *On Tranquility of Mind*: *The mind must not be kept equally in the same state of tension: SOCRATES did not blush to play with boys.* Laërtius writes that he was of distinguished character: naked on a Gem <note>original: "Gemma"; referring to an engraved gemstone or cameo</note> he resembles Silenus <note>A tutor of Dionysus, often depicted as a jovial, pot-bellied old man</note> not only in face, but in the appearance of his whole body, whom Lucian depicts as being of such short stature, with a fat and pot-bellied body. And Cassiodorus most eloquently says of the same: <margin>In the Philosophical Pseudo.</margin> <margin>On Psalm 72.</margin> *Many things are gathered together to express persons in a friendly manner, such as this: SOCRATES, son of Sophroniscus, whose mother is Phaenarete, BALD, VERY POT-BELLIED. # THE SOCRATES OF JEAN CHIFFLET

A mocker of life and death, a scourge of both men and gods.

<margin>Gems 21, 22</margin>
One may see Socrates depicted in no less a riddle <note>original: "grypho," referring to a complex or enigmatic symbol</note> with a domestic rooster—which, as he was dying, he ordered to be sacrificed to Aesculapius <note>The god of healing</note>—joined with the heads of a horse and a ram. It is, as it appears, a playful emblem, but one which you would not easily rank below any serious work. Regarding the Rooster, Johann Reuchlin <note>A famous German humanist and scholar of Hebrew (1455–1522)</note> writes in his *On the Art of the Kabbalah* dedicated to Pope Leo X:

<margin>Book 2</margin>
*Socrates, the wisest of men,* he says, *vowed a rooster should be offered to Aesculapius, as is recorded in Plato’s Phaedo; not so that he might heal his body—for he had already been sentenced to death and had drunk the poison—but so that he might return the soul of one dying peacefully, with a shout of joy and a Song of Deliverance <note>original: "Paean," a song of praise or triumph, often associated with Apollo</note>, to his father Apollo: that is, to the first and incorporeal sun, the dispenser of eternal life. For the rooster is sacred to Apollo Phoebus, who is called the Sun, as the joyful herald of the hours and the day. Moreover, Aesculapius was called by the ancients the "Jubilation of Apollo," the victory and triumph which is named Paean, because they named it for the brief cessation and rest after liberation. For in that age, those about to enter battle were accustomed to offer to Mars first, and after the battle, the victors would return a Paean to Apollo. Therefore, let the correct judgments of the most learned men see how aptly Socrates, when he was just about to die, venerated the Sun as the giver of life in the form of Aesculapius (who is the Paean); he surely thought it worthy and dutiful for every best man—once the Python of this world has been conquered like a great serpent and trodden underfoot—to celebrate a Paean to Aesculapius when the journey to true life is swiftly taken through death. Thus Orpheus, the most ancient of poets and a contemporary of those gods (for he is said to have sailed with the sons of Tyndareus and Hercules), sang in his Hymns: Healer of all, Aesculapius, Lord Paean, come blessed savior, granting a good end to life.* <note>original Greek: Ἰητὴρ πάντων ἀσκληπιὲ δέσποτα παιὰν, ἐλθὲ μάκαρ σωτὴρ βιοτῆς τέλος ἐσθλὸν ὀπάζων.</note>

The Ram seems to denote the ungrateful Athenians; or <unclear>xano-</unclear> <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "xantho-" or "xanothrichas" (fair-haired)</note>

<margin><note>original Greek: ἐξανοίαν</note> The service of the Ram</margin> *The service of the Ram*, as it is found in the ancient Proverb, which Suidas and Zenodotus report was usually said of duties bestowed upon the ungrateful; for truly the ram strikes the one who feeds it with its horns, just as the Athenians struck Socrates. Reproaching them for this in Plato’s *Phaedo*, he says: *But I think—and this should not be hidden from you—that no good has yet happened to you in this city as great as this MY SERVICE <note>original Latin: MEVM MINISTERIVM</note>, which I perform to please God.*

<margin>Cicero, Book 2 of *On Duties*.</margin> The horse stands out: *For we must strive with Men and HORSES (as the saying goes) if our intention is to protect and retain our honor.* He carries a vine leaf in his mouth, perhaps to signify the power of supreme judgment in Socrates, by which the guilty and the lazy were struck with the knotted vine-staff <note>The "vitis" or vine-staff was a symbol of authority and a tool for corporal punishment used by Roman centurions.</note>. Juvenal says of Marius:

<margin>Satire 8.</margin>
> *Another man of Arpinum, on the Volscian mountain, used to*
> *Toil for wages, weary behind another’s plow.*
> *After this, he would break the KNOTTED VINE-STAFF over his head,*
> *If he were slow in fortifying the camp with a sluggish axe.*

And for this reason, Jean Macaire notes in his *Carvings of Saints* <term>Hagioglyptis: a work describing sacred carvings or symbols</term> that in Rome, on the Junian Sarcophagus and a certain other one, a leaf of a fig or vine hung below the belt of an attendant, *Which perhaps,* he says, *was then the sign of attendants, so they might be distinguished from others.* There are also javelins, which with an unmoved mind he hurled against the corrupt morals of the Athenians even unto death; and likewise the Caduceus <note>The staff of Mercury (Hermes), symbol of peace and commerce</note>, because it is the gift of MERCURY to make souls tame from a wild state. Pierius Valerianus saw on an onyx gem, with a not very dissimilar theme, a horse's head, a bearded human face on the chest, the feet of a crane, and the tail of a young rooster with the inscription FAB. *From which fiction,* he says, *you might easily guess those virtues for which* FABIUS *the Delayer was famous. For leadership in war from the horse's head; wisdom from the bearded human chest; foresight and hesitation—*

withdrawn, and having the demeanor of a poor sitter <note>original: "mali sessoris." This refers to someone who carries themselves ungracefully or lacks poise</note>, so that Plato would often say to him: *Xenocrates, perform sacrifices to the Graces.* <note>A famous anecdote suggesting Xenocrates was too somber and needed to cultivate charm and elegance</note>

<margin>Gem XXIII.</margin>
Johannes Faber mentions the following gem, in which **Socrates** is shown with **Plato**; he calls it most skillfully carved. It is believable that it was worn by someone devoted to both men—perhaps by the philosopher Seneca, who claims for himself the worship of such images. Indeed, the gem itself receives light from his words. For he says:
<margin>Epistle 64, at the end.</margin>
> *Why should I not possess the images of **great men** as incentives for my soul? Why should I not always call upon them for the sake of honor? The same veneration I owe to my own teachers, I owe to those teachers of the human race from whom the beginnings of such great goods flowed. If I see a Consul or a Praetor, I shall do all things by which honor is usually shown: I shall dismount from my horse, uncover my head, and give way on the path. What then? Shall I receive both Marcus Catos, and Laelius the Wise, and **Socrates** with **Plato**, and Zeno and Cleanthes into my soul without the highest esteem? Truly, I venerate them, and I always rise to such great names.*

<margin>Gem XXIV.</margin>
Lucian, in *The Death of Peregrinus*, suggests that Socrates was depicted with his companions in prison with these words: *Are you waiting until some painter comes along and depicts you, just as the companions were depicted **in prison** next to **Socrates**?* A certain gem seems to represent such a scene, in which the seated Philosopher embraces the right arm of a standing woman with both hands, as if saying a final farewell to his affections. This was performed by Socrates as he was about to die, with the utmost modesty and restraint. Plato says in the *Phaedo*: *The women of his household came, and when **Socrates** had given them the commands he wished, he ordered them to depart.* Opposite this, a figure in the habit of a mourner sits upon a shield, like the German soldier on the coins of Domitian, by which is clearly indicated the [death] of Socrates...

# THE SOCRATES OF JEAN CHIFFLET

<note>original: "IOANNIS CHIFLETII SOCRATES"</note>

The virtue of the Athenians collapsed along with the fall of Socrates. Comedy is also present, leaning upon a playful instrument, drawing a veil over her head as if repenting for what she had said and done against him. A fourth figure moves his hand to his eyes and groans, not so much as if a just man were being punished, but as if Justice herself were being executed. This persona seems to suit no one better than the officer of the Eleven <note>The "Eleven" were the magistrates in Athens in charge of prisons and executions</note>, who, having brought the hemlock <note>term: hemlock (cicuta) — a highly poisonous plant used in ancient Greece for state executions</note>, addresses Socrates for the last time in the writings of Plato: *Now you know the message I bring to you. Farewell, and strive to bear what is necessary as easily as your strength allows.* And as soon as these words were spoken, Plato adds, he turned away WEEPING. A column in the center exalts the loftiness of Socratic glory, according to the ancient custom practiced by the Athenians themselves, by which not only statues but also columns were erected to those who had served the Republic well. Or, certainly, it indicates the constancy and fortitude of the Philosopher even as he was about to die in prison, of which Seneca—everywhere the greatest admirer of Socrates—says:

<margin>Epistle 24.</margin>
> HE DISPUTED IN THE PRISON ITSELF, and refused to leave (even when there were those who would permit his escape); he remained so that he might take away from men the fear of two of the most serious things: DEATH and PRISON.

<margin>The same author, in the book On Consolation to Helvia.</margin>
> With that same countenance with which he had once single-handedly brought the thirty tyrants into order, he entered the PRISON, intending to strip the place itself of its ignominy. For it could not seem to be a PRISON in which SOCRATES was present.

He was the first to discourse on the conduct of life, and the first of the Philosophers to die by condemnation. Renowned for his wisdom, he was always seen with the same expression, neither more cheerful nor more disturbed. No one ever saw him doing, or heard him teaching, anything impious or unjust, as Xenophon is the witness:

<margin>Pliny, Book 7, Chapter 19. Book 1 of the Memoirs of Socrates. Juvenal, Satire 13.</margin>
> *Who, amid cruel chains, would not wish to give a portion*
> *of the received hemlock to his accuser.*

<summary>This page describes the symbolic figures surrounding Socrates' death, including a repentant personification of Comedy and a weeping executioner, while citing Seneca and Juvenal to praise his stoic fortitude.</summary>
<keywords>Socrates, Plato, Seneca, death of Socrates, hemlock, Athenian justice, Stoicism, prison, virtue</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the biographical sketch of Socrates by Jean Chiflet, following the account of his trial and execution.</meta>

# THE SOCRATES OF JEAN CHIFLET

The insults and accusations of the Athenians did not weaken that upright and steadfast man, nor did they even shake him. Indeed, the citizens soon felt such regret for their most wicked parricide <note>The author uses "parricide" metaphorically to describe the killing of a "father" of the city or a venerable figure.</note> that immediately after his death they closed the Wrestling Schools and Gymnasiums. <margin>Laërtius in his Life of Socrates.</margin> They condemned some of his accusers to exile and sentenced **Melitus** to death; furthermore, they honored **Socrates** with a bronze statue crafted by Lysippus <note>A famous Greek sculptor and personal artist to Alexander the Great.</note>, which they placed in the Pompeion, a most celebrated location in the city. Finally, when **Anytus** traveled abroad, the people of Heraclea banished him by edict on that very same day. Lastly, Plutarch, in his book *On Envy and Hatred*, describes the repentance of the Athenians with even greater emphasis: *Those by whose slanders* **Socrates** *was oppressed,* he says, *were so hated and shunned by the citizens as utterly evil men, that no one would light a fire for them, nor answer their questions, nor bathe with them in the public baths. Instead, they would order the servants to pour out the water where those men had washed, as if it were polluted, until at last, unable to bear such hatred, they hanged themselves.* And such is the never-dying glory of **Socrates**; and such was the end of **Anytus**, **Melitus**, **Lycon**, and the adversaries who spoke against him.

->THE END.<-

<image-desc>A woodcut printer's ornament or tailpiece depicting a winged cherub or putto head in the center, flanked by cornucopias overflowing with fruits and floral elements.</image-desc>

<summary>This concluding page details the immediate aftermath of Socrates' execution, noting the Athenians' deep remorse, the punishment of his accusers, and the lasting honors bestowed upon the philosopher. It cites classical sources like Diogenes Laërtius and Plutarch to illustrate the social ostracization and eventual suicides of those who slandered him.</summary>

<keywords>Socrates, Athens, Melitus, Anytus, Lycon, Lysippus, Plutarch, Diogenes Laërtius, repentance, justice, Greek philosophy, Pompeion</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the official ecclesiastical approval for the publication, following the standard legal requirements for books in the 17th-century Spanish Netherlands.</meta>

# APPROVAL OF THE CENSOR.

I have seen the *wisest of philosophers* <note>Referring to Socrates</note> *represented many times upon engraved gems, and I have read the same explained with subtle clarity in this short work, the title of which is: The Socrates of Jean Chiflet, Canon of Tournai, or, A Judgment on Gems Carved with His Image. I have judged this work most worthy of the press, serving as a supplement to the "Abraxas Gems"* <note>A reference to Chiflet's previous work on Gnostic amulets and magical stones</note> *explained with equal success by the same author. To which I attest at Brussels on the Ides of March* <note>March 15th</note>*, 1661.*

->Antonius Sanderus, Priest,<-
->Licentiate of Sacred Theology, etc., Censor of Books.<-

<summary>This page contains the official formal approval (imprimatur) by the book censor Antonius Sanderus, authorizing the publication of Jean Chiflet's study on Socratic gemstones as a sequel to his work on Abraxas gems.</summary>
<keywords>Censorship, Jean Chiflet, Socrates, Engraved Gems, Antonius Sanderus, Brussels, 17th Century, Book History</keywords>
